We measure speculative decoding under production serving conditions (SGLang, CUDA graphs enabled) on H100 and H200 GPUs, at batch sizes up to 64 across 5 corpora. On dense models, verification, not drafting, dominates the energy cost. The saving shrinks as the batch grows, because batching has already shared the weight read that speculation would divide among the accepted tokens. At large batch sizes, block drafters push verification past the balance point between arithmetic and memory traffic into the compute-bound regime, where the saving becomes a loss, and a drafter with low acceptance raises energy per token. Meeting a tighter latency target with speculation then costs additional energy. Larger batches lower energy per token without speculation. Speculation adds to that saving only when acceptance is high, and on the model we tested it leaves little headroom for latency-preserving DVFS.


## arXiv 2610.40280v1 - Latent Prediction Yields Cross-Subject Alignable Representations in Sensory-Evoked fMRI

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, q-bio.NC
- published: 2026-10-29T15:33:41Z
- authors: Serwan Jassim, Victoria Bosch, Daniel Anthes, Elia Bruni, Tim Kietzmann
- query-set: cs.AI

Decoding sensory-evoked functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) data is a central analytical tool in cognitive neuroscience. Here we ask whether self-supervised learning (SSL), which has been scarcely explored in this domain, can yield representations that support strong downstream performance. To do so, we adapt a joint embedding predictive architecture (JEPA) to train participant-specific encoders on brain data alone. The encoders never see the stimulus images or any representation derived from them. Applied to the Natural Scenes Dataset (NSD), we evaluate our learned brain embeddings on the downstream tasks of brain captioning and image reconstruction, as well as cross-participant generalization. Our self-supervised approach achieves competitive performance with parameter-rich supervised pipelines that are trained end-to-end with access to image and language data. We further find that encoders trained independently for different participants can be aligned fully unsupervised, using neither images nor stimuli shared between them. After alignment, a neural decoding head trained on one participant can be applied to another without fine-tuning and retains most of its within-subject performance on image reconstruction and brain captioning. By removing the need for images and shared stimuli, our approach is a step towards pooling fMRI data across participants and, eventually, across datasets.


## arXiv 2610.40278v1 - Neighborhood Elites: Quality-Diversity Search for Uncertainty Quantification in Question Answering

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-10-29T15:32:57Z
- authors: Mesut Ceylan, Arda Baris Basaran, Elgar Fleisch, Filipe Barata
- query-set: cs.AI

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly capable, yet they produce wrong answers indistinguishable from correct ones. In question answering (QA), such an error propagates unnoticed into downstream tasks. Perturbation-based uncertainty quantification (UQ) addresses this by probing a model over a set of input rewrites and reading uncertainty from how much its predictions vary. Yet this set is usually built generically, by free paraphrasing, token-level perturbation, or adversarial search. Such rewrites leave two things uncontrolled that matter in QA: what kind of change is made to the context, and how explicitly the context supports the answer. When neither is controlled, the composition of the set is determined by the generator rather than by design, and the estimate inherits whatever that generator happens to produce. Quality-diversity (QD) search is built to cover a predefined exploration space, so we recast input perturbation as a QD problem and propose \emph{neighborhood elites (NE)}, a neighborhood constructor that spreads answer-preserving rewrites across two axes, the kind of edit and the explicitness of support. We compare NE against three other neighborhood constructors, holding the editor, QA model, uncertainty measure and search objective fixed, and against three scores read from the original context. Across five extractive-QA datasets and five instruction-tuned LLMs, NE improves failure detection against every other constructor in at least 24 of the 25 dataset-model pairs, reaching 0.726 AUROC and 0.211 AUPRC lift over the base rate. Additionally, NE detects failure better than semantic entropy computed over decoder samples, by 0.074 AUROC and 0.062 AUPRC lift, and this advantage widens as the neighborhood grows.


## arXiv 2610.40276v1 - TaskFrame: Connecting Task-Level Planning with Frame-Level Control for Game Agents

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T15:31:37Z
- authors: Guangzhi Wang, Junfu Pu, Yiran Wang, Xingyilang Yin, Honghao Chen
- query-set: cs.AI

Building general game agents requires both high-level task reasoning and low-latency action control in rapidly changing visual environments. These capabilities favor models with substantially different computational profiles, making them difficult to realize within a single model. In this work, we present TaskFrame, a hierarchical planner-controller framework that connects task-level planning with frame-level keyboard-and-mouse control. A vision-language task planner decomposes a high-level goal into an ordered sequence of atomic instructions and is optimized through supervised learning and dependency-aware GRPO. The hidden states of special goal tokens that terminate each atomic instruction provide continuous goal embeddings that are cached and reused across control steps. A causal Transformer controller combines the embedding of the active goal with recent observations to predict discrete keyboard and mouse-button actions and continuous camera motion at every game step. We train the system in three stages: planner training, controller pretraining, and joint alignment. Experiments in Minecraft show that TaskFrame is competitive with general-purpose vision-language models on instruction decomposition, and that in closed-loop evaluation the agent achieves a higher combat success rate than the evaluated baselines.


## arXiv 2610.40257v1 - Learned Terminal Costs for Short-Horizon Model Predictive Control of Autonomous Trucks in Interactive Highway Traffic

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.RO, eess.SY
- published: 2026-10-29T15:23:51Z
- authors: Hooman Hamze, Benjamin Houshmand, Deepthi Pathare, Erik Börve, Morteza Haghir Chehreghani
- query-set: cs.AI

Autonomous highway driving for heavy-duty trucks is a challenging control problem. The greater mass, dimensions and intricate dynamics increase the requirements for cooperation from surrounding traffic in critical maneuvers, requiring a careful balance between safety constraints and efficient long-horizon decision making. Model Predictive Control (MPC) provides rigorous constraint satisfaction but relies on terminal cost which can limit its effectiveness at short, computationally affordable horizons. Reinforcement Learning (RL) captures long-term driving behavior but offers no constraint guarantees and tends toward overly conservative policies when trained under sparse collision penalties. In this paper, we propose a hybrid MPC and RL framework for highway driving in autonomous trucks, where a finite-horizon MPC, solved via dynamic programming, uses a pre-trained RL model for terminal cost estimation. We evaluate the framework in a simulation environment with reactive surrounding traffic and compare it against pure MPC with zero terminal cost, rollout with terminal cost estimation using a base policy, and pure RL. Results show that the hybrid approach achieves zero collisions with the lowest cost of any configuration.


## arXiv 2610.40250v1 - Distributional Reward Calibration and Budget-Neutral Credit Assignment for Multimodal RLVR

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T15:20:12Z
- authors: Lang Chen, Zongxin Xu, Borui Wang, Anran Liu, Shitong Chen
- query-set: cs.AI

We revisit advantage estimation in Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) and observe that any token-level advantage admits a two-stage factorization: a \emph{sample-level baselining} step that converts scalar verifier rewards into a comparable sequence-level signal, and a \emph{token-level credit assignment} step that distributes that signal over the trajectory. Existing estimators typically address only one of these stages: affine baselines preserve the shape of each task's reward law, so heavy tails and binary cliffs propagate into the gradient, while token-level credit rules tie the size of each token's update to raw confidence scales and need not conserve the total credit issued by the verifier. We propose \textbf{TACE} (Two-stage Advantage and Credit Estimation), a two-stage estimator that addresses both stages. At stage~(S1), we replace linear standardization with a one-dimensional optimal-transport map to a standard normal, which gives a common population reward law across tasks and caps finite-sample outliers deterministically. At stage~(S2), we rank-calibrate a stop-gradient evidence contrast within each trajectory and renormalize the clipped token weights to have mean one, so credit is redistributed across tokens while its total mass and sign are conserved. We prove population-level inter-task moment equity, a deterministic tail bound, and mass-conservation, boundedness, and sign-anchoring properties that affine baselines and unnormalized token weights do not satisfy. On a multimodal reasoning suite, TACE obtains the strongest results on a majority of the benchmarks but does not dominate every setting.


## arXiv 2610.40249v1 - Reaction precedents extend retrosynthetic prediction beyond training coverage

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T15:19:54Z
- authors: Jiaxing Sun, Xuli Shen, Jiang Wu, Ruiyi Ding, Ruikang Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI

Reaction precedents guide retrosynthesis, but how far they can compensate for gaps in task-specific training coverage remains unclear. Using 2.71 million reactions, we restricted training and retrieval coverage by transformation or time, then expanded only the retrieval corpus with model parameters fixed. For transformations excluded from training, mean Top-1 accuracy increased from 13.0% to 58.0% on a historical test set and from 4.9% to 23.1% on a temporally held-out test set. For temporal coverage gaps, retrieval expansion enabled a model trained on records before 2017 to match or exceed a non-retrieval model trained on records before 2025 on both test sets. Without task-specific fine-tuning, precedents improved all 14 representative general-purpose models on both test sets and USPTO-50K; the strongest with precedents surpassed all eight representative specialized models. These findings show that inference-time reaction precedents can extend retrosynthetic prediction beyond task-specific training coverage without parameter updates.


## arXiv 2610.40246v1 - EnvOS: Auditing Computer-Use Agents When the World Moves On : Phenomenon-Based Task Construction, Certification, and Evidence-Based Evaluation

- categories: cs.AI, cs.HC
- published: 2026-10-29T15:17:29Z
- authors: Ankush Kadu, Athira Gopal, Niharika Singh, Arpit Jain, Aswanth Krishnan
- query-set: cs.AI

Computer-use agents built on large language models (LLMs) work in software that can change while they act: an appointment moves, a policy is revised, a record is updated. Testing agents under such changes is harder than it looks. When an agent fails, the cause may be the agent, but it may also be a change that did not fire as intended, an update the agent never received, or a grader that scored the episode wrongly. We introduce EnvOS, a framework for building and certifying environments that change mid-task. Before any agent runs, EnvOS checks that each change fires correctly, that the task remains solvable, and that the grader separates correct from incorrect behavior; after each run, separate code re-checks the verdict from archived evidence. We apply it to stale information, where a value the agent has already seen changes before the agent acts on it. In clinic booking, Claude Opus 5 succeeds on 0/16 tasks when nothing extra is shown, 2/16 when reminded to re-check the source, and 15/16 when the updated value is shown; the gain over the reminder holds after correcting for all tests (p = 0.00293). In insurance-claim approval, Opus 5 re-checks on its own and succeeds on 10/10, whereas Claude Sonnet 5 rises from 1/10 to 9/10 when the current policy is shown again, a gain significant in its pre-registered test but not after correcting for all tests. Whether an agent caught a change thus varied with the model and the interface. We also stress-test the grading itself: on corrupted episodes designed blind from the rules, the EnvOS pipeline accepted none of 26 as clean, and on an externally generated set it caught 25 of 39, against 4 for simple log checks. Because every EnvOS environment is generated from a seed and graded by an audited automatic verifier, the same environments are natural candidates for training agents with reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards.


## arXiv 2610.40227v1 - EpiAgent: Epistemic Self-Play for LLM Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-29T15:05:08Z
- authors: Xuanfa Jin, Xinyu Cui, Zhijian Ma, Xue Yan, Jian Zhao
- query-set: cs.AI

Epistemic game theory explains an agent's strategic behavior via the beliefs it holds about others and how these beliefs are updated as the interactions unfold. Modern LLM-based agents, however, often lack an explicit mechanism for learning and updating such beliefs to guide decisions under incomplete information. To bridge this gap, we propose EpiAgent, a framework that equips LLM-based agents with an explicit, learnable epistemic layer, which maintains beliefs over others' private information and combines these beliefs with the agent's available information into an epistemic state for action selection. Concretely, the agent's policy is decomposed into two coupled stages: Epistemic Update, which updates these beliefs from interaction histories, and Action Planning, which selects actions conditioned on the updated epistemic state. By formulating their dependency as a bi-level optimization problem, we introduce Epistemic Self-Play, an algorithm that alternately optimizes the two stages through self-play, utilizing an intrinsic prediction reward to evaluate how well inferred beliefs predict subsequent observations. Empirical results across both mixed and competitive scenarios show that EpiAgent consistently outperforms prompting-based and RL-based baselines. Further analyses reveal the benefits of dedicated epistemic training, improvements in belief inference accuracy, and the contribution of learned beliefs to action planning. These results demonstrate the value of an explicit, learnable epistemic layer for effective multi-agent interactions.


## arXiv 2610.40192v1 - Bridging Semantics and Structure: A Theoretically Grounded Framework for Link Prediction on TAGs

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T14:47:45Z
- authors: Chen Shao, Ruirui Wang, Tobias Kafer, Danai Koutra
- query-set: cs.AI

Link prediction on text-attributed graphs (TAGs) is a fundamental problem with applications in knowledge discovery, recommendation and beyond. Recent progress has highlighted the promise of combining Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) with Language Models (LMs) in order to leverage the complementary structural and semantic signals. However, due to the distinct inductive biases of GNNs and LMs, the design of an optimal fusion strategy remains an open research question. In this work, we present Bridge-LP, an intuitive, simple, efficient and theoretically grounded framework that unifies graph and language representations. It is built around three theoretically-justified design components: (D1) jumping connection of textual proximity, which enables robust LM training; (D2) compressed embedding, which encodes neighborhood information long exploited by classical link prediction heuristics, thereby enriching structural signals in a learnable way; and (D3) cache embedding training strategy, which reduces computational complexity. Together, these mechanisms establish an end-to-end cross-modal framework that scales to large graphs while capturing richer graph-text interactions. We conduct an extensive empirical evaluation, comparing against 40 existing models across 9 diverse datasets. Experiments show that Bridge-LP consistently outperforms state-of-the-art approaches, delivering up to a 43.06% improvement in Mean Reciprocal Rank (MRR) and setting a new benchmark for language-graph integration.


## arXiv 2610.40188v1 - Understanding axial attention in TSFMs through single location regression

- categories: stat.ML, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-29T14:46:50Z
- authors: Andrei-Tiberiu Pantea, Aymeric Dieuleveut
- query-set: cs.AI

Recent advances in Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) increasingly rely on axial attention mechanisms, but despite their empirical success, the theoretical properties of axial attention remain largely unexplored. To bridge this gap, we extend the Single Location Regression framework introduced in Marion et al. [2025] to 2D structured data, in order to study sparse information settings where a target depends on a small number of tokens. We formulate an analytically tractable axial attention-type predictor and rigorously analyze its statistical properties and training dynamics. We prove that under a specific asymptotic regime and with knowledge of the underlying oracle parameters, our predictor achieves Bayes optimality, whereas a class of semi-linear simplifications strictly fails to do so, underlining the role of the inner non-linearity. We prove that for a specific temperature scaling, Projected Gradient Descent converges globally to the optimal oracle parameters. Extensive numerical experiments validate our theoretical findings and highlight the critical role of inverse temperature scheduling in empirical convergence.


## arXiv 2610.40186v1 - Locating and Closing Information Gaps in Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T14:45:43Z
- authors: Yaxin Gong, Chongming Gao, Gangyi Zhang, Chenxiao Fan, Leyang Shen
- query-set: cs.AI

Multi-step Web tasks often require several LLM agents to gather and combine evidence. The communication graph of a fixed workflow restricts which agents can read each other's outputs: evidence retrieved by one agent may never reach the agent that needs it, leaving an information gap and an incomplete or incorrect answer. Adding a connection can deliver the missing evidence, but can also lead the receiver to adopt an incorrect upstream conclusion that displaces a correct answer; the final output alone does not separate the two. We propose Selective Probing and Link Insertion for Closing information gaps in multi-agent Execution (SPLICE), which locates information gaps per query and decides what to adopt after adding an edge. After the original workflow runs, SPLICE uses the query and node outputs to locate a possible gap, adds one directed edge from a source to a receiver to deliver the relevant evidence, and re-executes the receiver and affected downstream nodes, producing a candidate answer. The adoption decision reads both the final answers of the two runs and the process changes the edit caused inside the workflow: how far the receiver moved, where its new content came from, and whether the change reached the final answer. A selector trained on these process features chooses between adopting the candidate answer and retaining the original. On five benchmarks, SPLICE raises the task score on Web tool use by 8.0% and the mean score on four reasoning and planning benchmarks by 5.7% relative to the static skeleton, at 1.25 to 1.44$\times$ the skeleton's cost, and transfers zero-shot to four unseen backbones.


## arXiv 2610.40166v1 - EventSeeColor: Learning to Reconstruct Color from Monochrome Events

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T14:38:48Z
- authors: Chuanzhi Xu, Langyi Chen, Haoxian Zhou, Zelin Zhang, Haodong Chen
- query-set: cs.AI

Event cameras capture visual brightness changes under challenging lighting conditions. However, traditional event-based image reconstruction targets grayscale intensity and produces images without scene color. Color distinguishes surfaces with similar intensity. Reconstructing color from events can reveal appearance differences that reconstructed grayscale images cannot express. Although monochrome event cameras do not directly measure color, their event-triggering patterns depend on surface reflectance, illumination, and the sensor's spectral response. These patterns may contain cues that support color reconstruction. In this paper, we present EventSeeColor, which uses event-reconstructed grayscale and aligned events to jointly guide a diffusion colorizer. Grayscale-derived semantic context supports color inference, while region-balanced chroma supervision and local RGB refinement improve color distribution, brightness, and texture. In the combined evaluation on two real event datasets, EventSeeColor achieves the best structural similarity, learned perceptual scores, and chromatic-region color difference among the external methods.


## arXiv 2610.40161v1 - A Negotiation-Aware Hybrid LLM Framework for Intent Interpretation and Service Discovery

- categories: cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-10-29T14:37:12Z
- authors: Wided Meflah, Hayet Brabra, Walid Gaaloul
- query-set: cs.AI

Increasingly, users express their needs as natural-language intents rather than structured requests. These intents may be incomplete, high-level, or internally conflicting. A conflicting intent either violates fundamental properties of the cloud paradigm or states mutually inconsistent requirements. Classical composition approaches are designed for structured, low-level, and complete requests. They cannot interpret such intents, discover suitable services from provider catalogs, or negotiate conflicting requirements with users. To address this gap, we propose a negotiation-aware framework for intent interpretation and service discovery. The framework first refines the natural-language intent and dynamically detects conflicts. It then structures the intent into a provider-independent TOSCA template. For each specification, it retrieves candidate service offers through deterministic matching grounded in cloud provider offerings. Some conflicts arise during interpretation. Other requirements prove unsatisfiable during discovery. Both are resolved through iterative natural-language negotiation with the user. We evaluate the framework on 70 diverse intents against single-shot and enhanced-prompting baselines. It improves recall and F1 for both conflict detection and service discovery. It also eliminates hallucinated offers and resolves conflicts at a cost proportional to their number.


## arXiv 2610.40160v1 - FARE: Factor-Aware Retrieval with Error-Guided Fusion for Retail Forecasting

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T14:37:00Z
- authors: Chenghao Gu, Zhuokang Shen, Shenghao Wu, Tingxuan Zhu, Weiyi Luo
- query-set: cs.AI

Retail sales forecasting underpins inventory planning, yet promotions and calendar events reshape short-term demand. Although each factor usually has historical occurrences, the complete factor combination of a target week often has few precedents. The challenge is therefore to retrieve informative partial matches and weight them by their reliability, which varies across series. We propose FARE(Factor-Aware Retrieval with Error-Guided Fusion), which ranks historical weeks separately for each known target-week factor and merges the selected weeks into a factor reference under a fixed budget. Guided by shrunk errors from completed development weeks and the current disagreement among candidates, a first gate mixes this factor reference with a shape reference from similar sales histories, and a second gate controls how strongly the mixture corrects a backbone forecast. Both gates remain fixed during testing. On Favorita, M5, Rossmann, and Rohlik, FARE reduces the weighted absolute percentage error (WAPE) of five backbones by 11.46%, 4.06%, 14.63%, and 10.16% on average. With Chronos-2 frozen, FARE also outperforms conventional and existing enhancement methods, including supervised residual correction and retrieval augmentation. Code is available at https://github.com/HousonGu/FARE .


## arXiv 2610.40151v1 - The generative paradox: the Anti-Prompt paradigm for human capability

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T14:35:05Z
- authors: Margarita Trifonov Vadim E. Levit
- query-set: cs.AI

Generative AI in education is usually judged by the output of human-AI teams. That focus overlooks a second outcome: what people can still understand, challenge, repair, and adapt when assistance changes or disappears. We call this tension the generative paradox and distinguish four manifestations: performance, assistance, ownership, and capability stock. We propose developmental function allocation, which complements operational function allocation and meaningful human control by treating retained human capability as a design objective alongside current joint performance. The Anti-Prompt paradigm reverses the default interaction order: when immediate generation would preempt a consequential human act, AI should first create the conditions for human construction. We distinguish the validity of a product, responsibility for its construction, and evidence of capability after support changes. Representative Anti-Prompts, a canonical interaction sequence, and stress tests spanning mathematical reasoning, experimental science, software diagnosis, and lesson design make these distinctions concrete. We conclude with falsifiable predictions about retained capability and a system-level hypothesis about the reproduction and distribution of expertise.


## arXiv 2610.40133v1 - DynaRev: Benchmarking Web Agents under Dynamic Goal Revision

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T14:25:54Z
- authors: Shiqi Zhang, Guochen Zhou, Zesheng Wei, Hai Zhao, Zhisong Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI

Web agents are typically evaluated with goals fully specified before execution and fixed thereafter, whereas real users often revise their requirements during interaction. To study such interactions, we introduce DynaRev, a benchmark of 181 tasks to evaluate web agents under dynamic goal revision. DynaRev uses semantic milestones to deliver revisions at task-defined stages, covers additive revisions, unconditional overrides, and runtime-conditioned overrides, and evaluates correctness, responsiveness, and efficiency through trajectory-grounded evaluation. Five-model evaluations show that agents often satisfy only part of the task requirements, with performance varying across task sources, revision types, and models. Further analyses reveal varied milestone timing, mixed effects of dynamic delivery, and contrasting revision-handling outcomes, with gains from instruction rewriting while preserving browser state and losses from browser resets. DynaRev provides a testbed for studying how web agents adapt to evolving user goals. The code and data are available at https://github.com/zsq259/DynaRev.


## arXiv 2610.40130v1 - Escaping Path Mirages in Offline Goal-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T14:21:50Z
- authors: Junhyeon Bae, Jaebak Hwang, Gwanwoo Choi, Minung Kim, Seungyul Han
- query-set: cs.AI

Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning remains challenging in stochastic long-horizon settings, where compounding value estimation errors hinder reliable goal reaching. While prior methods have sought to address this challenge through various approaches, a key challenge remains path mirage, where agents overcommit to spuriously successful trajectories in offline datasets induced by stochasticity, often leading to failure on difficult tasks. To address this issue, we propose Branch-aware Graph Planning (BGP), which captures stochastic branching structures by identifying high-variability points as graph nodes and designing goal-conditioned edges with segmented learning to avoid unreliable branches and reduce unnecessary stochasticity along paths. As a result, BGP enables reliable goal reaching even in highly stochastic long-horizon environments, and experiments on diverse OGBench tasks show that it substantially outperforms prior state-of-the-art offline goal-conditioned RL methods. Our implementation is available at https://umlic.github.io/papers/BGP/.


## arXiv 2610.40096v1 - Reward-Adaptive Co-Evolution of Policy and Rubric

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T14:00:07Z
- authors: Yizhu Chen, Jianguang Jiang, Jiameng Huang, Chuyu Fang, Bojun Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI

Rubric-based rewards provide fine-grained, interpretable supervision for open-ended reinforcement learning, where holistic scalar rewards struggle to capture multidimensional response quality. Static rubrics can lose discriminative power as the policy distribution evolves, while existing policy--rubric co-evolution methods rely on globally prescribed update schedules that ignore heterogeneous reward informativeness. We introduce RACE, a Reward-Adaptive Co-Evolution framework. At the group level, per-rollout-group training-signal routing classifies groups as informative, diversity-collapsed, or rubric-failed, updating the policy only with informative groups while caching rubric failures. At the phase level, event-triggered phase switching enters rubric optimization upon persistent reward saturation and returns when rubric-reward improvement plateaus. Rubric phases train the generator on cached pairs using a frozen judge and a scale-normalized pairwise-margin reward. Across both levels, online threshold adaptation adjusts routing and switching thresholds through proportional control. Under a unified evaluation protocol, RACE achieves the highest average performance and outperforms the strongest fixed-alternation baseline on all eight tasks, while improving rubric discrimination.


## arXiv 2610.40091v1 - Upcycling Language Models for Multiple Specialized Domains

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T13:58:38Z
- authors: Mingzi Cao, Xi Wang, Nikolaos Aletras
- query-set: cs.AI

Effective multi-domain adaptation of large language models requires prioritizing relevant domain knowledge while minimizing interference from irrelevant domains. Existing approaches commonly use Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) to train a separate adapter for each domain and combine these adapters within one model. However, they either weight adapters equally regardless of the input domain or dynamically weight adapters while still activating all of them including irrelevant ones. Additionally, while efficient, their low-rank structure limits performance. In contrast, upcycling is a model expansion method that adds experts and a router to a dense Transformer layer offering larger capacity than LoRA approaches. In this paper, we propose Domain-Specialized Upcycling (DS-Upcycling) for multi-domain adaptation. We introduce a new domain-aware supervision that allows the router to effectively learn to distinguish domains by selecting the appropriate experts. During fine-tuning on each new domain, we update only the corresponding expert and router while freezing all other parameters. Experiments show that our method achieves the highest average performance across the adapted domains compared to baselines while retaining general performance. With just 256 examples per domain for router supervision, DS-Upcycling achieves 90-98% routing accuracy after adaptation compared with 43-70% for routers trained without our approach.


## arXiv 2610.40075v1 - Learning Safe Flow Matching Robot Policies from Binary Feasibility Oracles

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T13:53:04Z
- authors: Jianming Ma, Rongjun Jin, Yizhi Chen, Yue Gao
- query-set: cs.AI

Robot foundation models increasingly rely on flow-based action generation for general-purpose manipulation, yet the generated actions are not guaranteed to satisfy physical constraints such as joint limits and collision avoidance. This problem is especially challenging when the feasible set cannot be described analytically and is accessible only through black-box feasibility oracles. We study this practical oracle-only setting and propose an oracle-guided differentiable safeguard for safe flow-matching robot policies. The safeguard uses binary feasibility queries to correct unsafe updates in the forward pass, while extracting local boundary information to provide constraint-aware learning signals in the backward pass. Combined with discrete-time flow matching, the same safeguard is used during training and inference, enabling forward--backward aligned policy learning without explicit or differentiable constraint models. Experiments on obstacle-aware robotic manipulation demonstrate 100% constraint satisfaction while preserving task performance.


## arXiv 2610.40071v1 - Agentic process evidence

- categories: cs.AI, cs.SE, eess.SY
- published: 2026-10-29T13:50:54Z
- authors: Yuval Kainan, Carmit Hershman
- query-set: cs.AI

The way software gets built is changing dramatically. AI agents now create pull requests, select dependencies, refactor codebases, interpret test failures, and execute infrastructure changes across the entire Software Development Life Cycle (SDLC). AI agents are autonomous team members, but unlike human engineers, they are ephemeral - their reasoning, decisions, and tool calls vanish the moment the session ends. Modern Security incidents involving intent mismatch or injection are becoming increasingly common - forcing us to address AI Agents as an insider threat. No widely adopted standard yet enables agentic provenance and governance and its obvious that you can't hold an ephemeral process accountable. The git log was designed for human commits. It shows what changed. It does not show what an AI agent did, which model it used, which tools it called, or whether anyone was accountable for the outcome. Every governance framework, every audit trail, every compliance control in the SDLC was built around one assumption: a human made the decision. That assumption no longer holds. This shift introduces 3 main procedural problems (not addressing scale) - Supply-chain vector - The agentic coder is software, so it is a supply-chain security risk. Policy guardrails - How to ensure the agentic coder is compliant with the policies of the organization. Task-alignment - How to align the agentic coder on its tasks and goals. We introduce Agentic Process Evidence (APE), a signed, tamper-evident, machine-readable attestation of agentic processes, linking the sessions to their outcome (process-aware logging). APE enables Policy-as-Code control over the agentic processes, allowing for human in the loop only when it's needed. We support the in-toto style attestation framework, and the protocol is aligned with the relevant regulations


## arXiv 2610.40061v1 - Localizing Concepts in Visual Autoregressive Models

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T13:49:01Z
- authors: Nanxiang Jiang, Yang Liu, Yuanhao Wang, Min Xu
- query-set: cs.AI

Understanding the internal routing of visual concepts is crucial for the safe and controllable adaptation of generative models. While concept localization has been widely studied in diffusion models, the emerging paradigm of next-scale visual autoregressive models remains largely unexplored. In this paper, we introduce LoCo, the first model-agnostic framework to localize where and when specific concepts emerge within autoregressive models. Driven by the native coarse-to-fine nature of next-scale generation, our method precisely maps conceptual knowledge across three distinct dimensions: Layer, Scale, and Position. To systematically localize and evaluate concept routing without context bias, we propose LoCoBench, a comprehensive dataset spanning 10 diverse categories. Extensive probing on image and video autoregressive models like Infinity, HunyuanImage-3.0, and InfinityStar shows that the localized positions are both interpretable and causally related to concept emergence. Building on these insights, we apply our localization method to three key applications: concept erasure, model personalization, and adversarial concept injection. Experiments demonstrate that our targeted intervention achieves state-of-the-art performance, substantially reducing computational overhead while preserving benign utility. Overall, our findings offer insights into how conceptual knowledge is routed during autoregressive generation, introducing a practical pathway for more interpretable, efficient, and secure adaptation.


## arXiv 2610.40055v1 - One Vector or Many? Clustered Optimal-Transport Steering under Quantization

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-10-29T13:46:39Z
- authors: Illia Makarov
- query-set: cs.AI

Activation steering changes what a language model generates by editing its hidden states at inference time. Its most common form adds one vector, the difference of class means (DiM), to every hidden state. CHaRS (concept heterogeneity-aware representation steering) uses optimal transport instead: an entropic plan pairs the clusters of the two classes, and each hidden state moves along a mixture of the cluster displacements, weighted toward its nearest clusters. We study this family of clustered optimal-transport maps for toxicity mitigation in Gemma-2-2B and Granite-3.1-2B, and ask what the plan adds to DiM and whether that survives INT8 and NF4 quantization. We prove an identity: whatever the plan, the displacements averaged with the cluster sizes as weights equal DiM, so a clustered map can differ from DiM only for tokens whose routing weights depart from the cluster sizes. In the CHaRS protocol they stay close, and CHaRS is statistically equivalent to Mean-AcT, which adds DiM at every layer, in all eight pre-specified equivalence tests. A variant fitted on individual tokens, which routes each token mainly to its nearest clusters, leaves 37-39% less toxicity than DiM on Gemma and 51-61% less on Granite at steering strength 3 (toxic-bert), where DiM already removes 76-85% of it. Two other toxicity classifiers confirm the gain on Granite, but on Gemma only one does. At equal strength the variant has somewhat higher perplexity, but at matched toxicity it is slightly more fluent than DiM. Yet a nearly constant map from the same clusters also leaves about 30% less toxicity, so the role of treating tokens differently remains open. Under INT8 and NF4 the variant still leaves less toxicity than DiM, and transferring it from full precision to the NF4 model instead of refitting it adds no significant toxicity after correction for multiple tests.


## arXiv 2610.40051v1 - Rethinking Pre-pretraining

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T13:45:58Z
- authors: Alexander Stern, Tim Rocktäschel, Acyr Locatelli, Laura Ruis
- query-set: cs.AI

We report that allocating compute to synthetic pre-pretraining (PPT) never outperforms pure natural-language training in our compute-matched experiments. This holds across four PPT curricula (k-Dyck, neural cellular automata (NCA), declarative reference corpora, and code) and three training regimes: data-constrained, data-unconstrained, and a high token-to-parameter-ratio regime (881M parameters trained on 50B tokens). When natural language is scarce, repeated epochs over a 100M-token corpus beat every synthetic curriculum on natural-language validation loss, with no consistent downstream accuracy advantage from PPT. We find that PPT gains reported in prior work are sensitive to the weight initialization scheme. In an NCA reproduction, we recover a 5.4% perplexity improvement from PPT under the original initialization scheme. An alternative scheme improves both methods, but the no-PPT baseline more, closing the gap between them. However, when PPT receives additional compute rather than a share of the natural-language budget, gains persist at small natural-language budgets even with improved initialization, but disappear by 1B tokens. Finally, models forget what they learn during PPT in subsequent natural-language training; interleaving the same data throughout training prevents forgetting, but does not significantly improve natural-language performance. Our results suggest limited benefits of synthetic pre-pretraining as a distinct training stage and motivate rethinking how such data are used in language model training.


## arXiv 2610.40046v1 - EPC-RawDC: Equivariant Physics Constrained 3D Diffusion with Raw Data Consistency for CBCT to CT Image Synthesis

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, physics.med-ph
- published: 2026-10-29T13:42:56Z
- authors: Alzahra Altalib, Chunhui Li, Alessandro Perelli
- query-set: cs.AI

Cone beam computed tomography (CBCT) is widely used for image guided and adaptive radiotherapy, but scatter, noise, reconstruction artifacts, and inaccurate Hounsfield units limit its quantitative use. Recent diffusion approaches for CBCT to CT synthesis remain largely image driven, with limited use of the underlying acquisition physics. We propose EPC-RawDC, a conditional 3D latent diffusion framework that combines volumetric generative modeling with two complementary constraints derived from the same scanner specific CBCT forward model. First, geometry equivariant projection constraint exploits the rotational equivariance of the CBCT forward operator by supervising the synthesized CT through correspondingly transformed reference projections. Second, raw data consistency uses noisy projection measurements of the individual acquisition as scan specific supervision during training. The framework was evaluated on two independent clinical cohorts with distinct scanner geometries and projection sources. For JUST, the physics constraints used measured clinical raw CBCT projections acquired with an Elekta system, whereas for SynthRAD2025 the projection data were simulated from the reference CT using a Varian geometry with Poisson photon statistics. EPC-RawDC achieved an SSIM of 0.97 in both cohorts and improved PSNR over DDPM by 2.66 dB on SynthRAD2025 and 2.10 dB on JUST, while reducing mean MAE by 20.3% and 19.0%, respectively. On SynthRAD2025, projection consistency and equivariant errors were reduced by 55.0% and 41.4% relative to DDPM. These results demonstrate that combining a volumetric diffusion prior with scanner geometry and scan specific projection measurements improves quantitative CBCT to CT synthesis across both simulated and measured projection domains. Code available after publication at https://github.com/ALZAHRAALTALIB/EPC-RawDC.


## arXiv 2610.40042v1 - UPPAAi: Towards LLM-Assisted Modeling, Verification, Analysis and Synthesis

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI, cs.LO
- published: 2026-10-29T13:40:33Z
- authors: Peter Dolog, Kim G. Larsen, Marius Mikučionis, Frederik F. K. Rasmussen
- query-set: cs.AI

For more than three decades, UPPAAL has supported the modelling and analysis of real-time and cyber-physical systems. Yet building a suitable model, formalising the intended properties, choosing analysis techniques and interpreting counterexamples remain iterative tasks that demand substantial expertise. Large language models (LLMs) can offer plausible explanations and even solutions, but they are not necessarily correct. We present UPPAAI, an extension of UPPAAL in which users construct, explain, debug and specify models in dialogue with an LLM. The guiding principle is that the user requests, the LLM proposes, explains and orchestrates, and UPPAAL verifies. UPPAAI exposes the UPPAAL editor, queries and verifier through a Tools API. We report on seven case studies, from explaining to a newcomer to learning a controller, covering timed automata, stochastic hybrid automata and strategy learning. LLMs proved to be extremely helpful in not just generating solutions but also pro-actively suggesting relevant UPPAAL features it was not asked about.


## arXiv 2610.40036v1 - Hybrid AI Patterns: Thirteen Ways to Couple Language Models with Rules, and How to Choose Among Them

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T13:37:28Z
- authors: Siegfried Handschuh, Götz-Henrik Wiegand, Lorena Raichle
- query-set: cs.AI

Language models handle text well, and rule engines give conclusions with proofs. Systems that combine the two are usually described by example, and the same coupling turns up under different names in agent frameworks, guardrail toolkits and neuro-symbolic research. This document defines thirteen patterns for coupling a language model with an executable rule language, in the catalogue form introduced for object-oriented design: intent, motivation, applicability, participants, collaborations, consequences, implementation, known uses and related patterns. Four patterns concern what crosses the boundary between model and rules, five who controls whom, three how the model takes part in the reasoning, and one how knowledge is kept over time. Five invariants hold for every combination, and five failure patterns name the couplings that break them. We relate the catalogue to published pattern catalogues and taxonomies of hybrid and neuro-symbolic systems and show where they overlap with it. A decision guide leads from a problem to a pattern. It rests on our measurements, including the cases in which a model alone did as well as the hybrid or better, and it reports what each design cost.


## arXiv 2610.40030v1 - Cheap Retrieval and Observation Credit Assignment from One Quantity: The Value of Information

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-29T13:36:06Z
- authors: Daniel Strauss, Nikolaos Sotirakis, Zhenshan Bing, Alois Knoll
- query-set: cs.AI

Memory in reinforcement learning raises two problems. Attention finds the relevant item by scoring the whole store, which is costly for robots under latency and energy limits. When a retrieved memory pays off, credit for acquiring it must cross the whole sequence. We argue that both are governed by one quantity, the value of information, and propose to estimate it from archived rollouts with a learned world model. We obtain that, when read at the observation, it yields a provable lower bound on the optimal value of the acquiring action, which floors its TD target and attributes this floor to the observation that earned it. Read at the decision, it defines the optimal retrieval set. A pilot retrieval system, to be trained and improved with this signal, solves a memory task with reduced context.


## arXiv 2610.40023v1 - HEMO: Harness Self-Evolution for Multi-Objective Combinatorial Optimization

- categories: cs.NE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T13:32:00Z
- authors: Jianghan Zhu, Cong Zhang, Ni Zhang, Yifan Yang, Yew Soon Ong
- query-set: cs.AI

Automatic operator design (AOD) has increasingly adopted large language models (LLMs) to generate problem-specific search and variation operators for multi-objective combinatorial optimization (MOCO). Yet most methods evolve the operators while leaving the process that discovers them fixed. This limits adaptation as new Pareto trade-offs and operator interactions change where and how the search should proceed. We introduce HEMO, which makes the AOD harness itself a self-evolving optimization target. HEMO maintains a persistent harness state and updates one module at a time, revising the logic that determines which experience to retrieve, which operators to improve, which parents to select, and how to formulate the orchestration of search steps. Downstream solution quality and runtime guide these updates, while a discounted UCB scheduler directs adaptation toward promising modules as their utility changes. A persistent memory filesystem preserves candidate outcomes and diagnostic feedback across harness states, enabling successive states to build on accumulated optimization experience. Experiments across eight MOCO tasks and two structurally distinct evolutionary backbones demonstrate strong performance against conventional evolutionary, neural, and LLM-based AOD baselines, together with effective transfer to related tasks. HEMO extends automatic operator design beyond evolving individual operators to improving the search process that discovers them.


## arXiv 2610.40006v1 - Human--AI Interactions as First-Class Data: A Formal Interaction Model and Runtime for Explainable AI Collaboration

- categories: cs.AI, cs.HC
- published: 2026-10-29T13:23:54Z
- authors: Michela Taufer, Kin Ng, Ian Lumsden, Aashish Pandey, Rajasi Gore
- query-set: cs.AI

AI assistants are increasingly embedded in computational notebooks, scientific workflows, software engineering tools, and learning systems, yet human-AI interactions are often treated as transient prompt-response exchanges rather than durable data products, hindering reproducibility, explainability, replay, behavioral analysis, and adaptive AI support. We argue that these interactions form a new data class requiring formal representation and introduce the Human-AI Interaction Description Language (HAIDL), a formal interaction model with a Backus-Naur Form-inspired concrete syntax. HAIDL encodes each interaction as a structured object capturing computational context, user request, prompt payload, AI response, feedback, problem context, provenance, and model metadata. We integrate this vision into HAIDL-X, a middleware for JupyterLab that instantiates HAIDL by capturing user-bot state, building contextualized prompts, interfacing with AI providers, collecting feedback, and storing interactions in a repository. We add a learning layer over HAIDL that maps interaction objects and notebook execution traces into reproducible measures of user behavior, including engagement, performance, help-seeking, error-oriented use, and learning personas. HAIDL-X provides a foundation for treating human-AI collaboration as structured, queryable, replayable, analyzable data.


## arXiv 2610.40005v1 - Efficient Learning of Mechanism Designs and Compositionality

- published: 2026-10-29T13:23:44Z
- authors: João Pedro Gandarela, Thiago Rios, Stefan Menzel, André Freitas
- query-set: cs.AI

Learning mechanism behaviour requires a representation of how connected parts jointly constrain motion. We study whether exposing reusable constraint subassemblies improves prediction from limited data. Assur-KB represents planar mechanisms through irreducible modules, boundary interfaces, and solve dependencies while retaining source geometry. Our theoretical results characterize transfer through module interfaces and establish physical limits arising from missing interface information and accumulated sensitivity. To test the representation claim directly, the experimental design compares matched mechanism-curve pairs across training budgets within the same multilayer-perceptron family. This controlled comparison establishes the utility of the complete representation in the evaluated learning setting. Assur-derived features provide a geometric-mean $1.8\times$ advantage in sample efficiency over flattened adjacency and position inputs across budgets of 50-10,000 mechanisms. Separate experiments establish invariance under tested joint relabelings, higher valid-generation rates under dyadic composition, and a coarser structural index over 10.02 million mechanisms. In summary, the theory and experiments identify how physical constraint structure supports limited-data prediction and which interface conditions govern compositional reuse.


## arXiv 2610.39992v1 - Accumulating Pruning Constraints: New Exact Polynomial-Space Algorithms for Max 2-SAT

- categories: cs.DS, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T13:12:14Z
- authors: Konstantin Kutzkov
- query-set: cs.AI

We present deterministic polynomial-space algorithms that solve Max 2-SAT on $n$ variables exactly in time $\Ostar(2^{(1-γ)n})$, for a fixed $γ>0$, on several classes of instances whose clause density may grow with $n$. Our main tool extends the classic DPLL branching algorithm. When the search gives a variable its less promising value, comparing the two values of the variable yields a pruning constraint that is satisfied by some optimal assignment. Accumulating these constraints along each branch prunes all but an exponentially small fraction of the assignments. The classes are formulas with many biased variables, semirandom 2-CNF formulas, and Max-Cut on regular expander graphs and on graphs generated by a stochastic block model. In a semirandom formula, the pairs of variables that may share a clause are arbitrary, and the clauses on these pairs are random. For semirandom formulas and block-model graphs, the algorithms are still exact on every input, and the time bound holds with high probability over the input. To our knowledge, these are the first polynomial-space algorithms with such a running time for nontrivial classes of dense Max 2-SAT instances. Beyond these classes, the pruning constraints can be added to any branching algorithm for Max 2-SAT, and we expect them to be of practical relevance for general formulas, in particular for sparse ones, where each constraint involves only a few variables.


## arXiv 2610.39955v1 - Mind The Gap (MTG-AD): Dataset and Fine-grained Evaluation for End-to-End Inline Audio Descriptions

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.HC
- published: 2026-10-29T12:51:38Z
- authors: Subhashini Venugopalan, Jenny Tan, Taylor Roper, Jimmy Tobin, Anton Kast
- query-set: cs.AI

Audio descriptions (AD) are essential for making visual media accessible to blind and low-vision (BLV) users. While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) offer a scalable solution for generating audio descriptions on-demand, their performance on ``inline'' audio descriptions -- which must fit within existing silences in a video -- remains under-explored, particularly for diverse user-generated content (UGC). We present a comprehensive investigation into MLLM-generated inline audio descriptions. First, we introduce MTG-AD, a professionally annotated dataset of 36 hours of videos, each averaging $\sim$5 mins. and 8k+ descriptions in total, co-created by paired blind and sighted AD professionals to provide a high-quality benchmark for this task. Second, we propose MTG-AD-Eval, an evaluation framework based on audio description expert guidelines that provides fine-grained, reference-free actionable metrics for model improvement. Crucially, our framework evaluates the end-to-end task: it renders the generated scripts into audio tracks to assess their fit within the original video's natural silences, minimizing disruptive overlaps. Our analysis across frontier MLLMs reveals that while models accurately describe visual events and substantially reduce speech overlaps, gaps persist in narrative pacing and flow. Finally, we empirically validate that our automated and reference-free metrics correlate significantly with human quality ratings from sighted raters and 7 BLV participants, finding that while users narrowly prefer human-authored audio descriptions, they find MLLM-generated audio descriptions highly beneficial for comprehension. We provide actionable system design guidance and release MTG-AD at https://hf.co/datasets/google/mind-the-gap-inline-ad and MTG-AD-Eval at https://github.com/google/mtg-ad.


## arXiv 2610.39954v1 - When to spend privacy: optimal allocation in private learning

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, math.OC
- published: 2026-10-29T12:51:07Z
- authors: Umberto Biccari, Yongcun Song
- query-set: cs.AI

Privacy accounting bounds the cumulative privacy cost of Differentially Private Stochastic Gradient Descent but does not determine how to allocate a fixed budget across iterations. We formulate the complete Gaussian noise-variance schedule as an open-loop control under a composed zero-Concentrated Differential Privacy constraint, explicitly accounting for schedule selection. For expected quadratic terminal tracking, we prove that every local minimizer exhausts the certified budget, yielding an exact-budget simplex formulation. We introduce Privacy-Budget Adjoint Mirror Descent (PB-AMD), combining pathwise adjoint gradients with entropic updates that preserve positivity, charge floors, and the prescribed budget. Under regularity and coercivity assumptions, we establish existence and budget stability of optimal schedules, design-to-training utility stability, and deterministic, stochastic, and sample-average stationarity guarantees. A linear-quadratic reduction yields an explicit square-root allocation law. Experiments demonstrate tracking improvements over several allocation rules and predictive improvements over uniform allocation in a learning-rate restart setting, where learning-rate allocation performs similarly. Comparisons at a common stationarity tolerance show lower schedule-optimization times than projected gradient descent on the tested problems. The framework connects certified privacy accounting with optimization through nonlinear training dynamics.


## arXiv 2610.39927v1 - Learning Calibrated Confidence for Multi-Agent Collaboration

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-29T12:30:00Z
- authors: Chenxiao Fan, Chongming Gao, Gangyi Zhang, Yaxin Gong, Leyang Shen
- query-set: cs.AI

Complex information seeking on the Web often requires collecting evidence from multiple documents and reasoning across them, as in multi-hop question answering. Multi-agent systems address such tasks by dividing work among specialized agents, where upstream agents produce intermediate answers and supporting evidence that downstream agents build upon. However, upstream answers vary in correctness, and most systems provide no quantitative signal about it, leaving the downstream agent to judge whether to trust or verify what it receives based solely on the textual evidence. In this paper, we propose MASCAL. Each agent learns to produce a correctness probability for its answer by jointly optimizing task performance and confidence calibration in reinforcement learning; the upstream agent passes this probability at handoff, giving the downstream agent a quantitative basis for its decisions, and the final answer also carries a calibrated probability, quantifying the reliability of the system output. To guide the downstream agent in using this signal during training, we introduce confidence-aware weighting, which increases the learning weight at positions responsive to the upstream probability, so that the agent learns more from decisions where it actively uses the signal. Across three collaboration architectures spanning Web retrieval and reasoning tasks, MASCAL improves task accuracy and calibration quality for both agents, and generalizes to deeper collaboration chains and different model scales. Intervention analysis confirms that upstream confidence affects downstream decision behavior, and the learned final confidence supports selective answering and targeted rechecking.


## arXiv 2610.39926v1 - Spectral Invariant Fusion and Refinement for Domain Generalized Semantic Segmentation

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T12:29:41Z
- authors: Yansong Bai, Yunzhi Zhuge, Xinzhuo Yu, Lu Zhang, Jin Zhan
- query-set: cs.AI

Domain generalized semantic segmentation is challenging because it requires accurate dense prediction on unseen domains without any access to target-domain data during training. Existing methods improve transferability through data diversification, invariant learning, or direct use of foundation models, but they often struggle to simultaneously suppress domain-sensitive interference and preserve fine-grained spatial cues. In this work, we propose SIFR, a frequency-aware dual-stream framework that tackles domain shift at both the fusion and decoding stages. SIFR uses spectral modeling as a unified principle to regulate both heterogeneous prior interaction and dense decoder refinement. Instead of directly fusing frozen CLIP and DINOv2 features, SIFR performs spectral-guided invariant fusion to filter out domain-sensitive responses while preserving complementary semantic and structural information. In addition, SIFR incorporates a spectral decoder refinement module that stabilizes multi-scale decoder features through low-frequency statistics-based normalization and adaptive frequency selection, thereby reducing the propagation of residual domain bias in dense prediction. This design enables SIFR to jointly improve domain robustness and segmentation precision under severe distribution shifts. Extensive experiments on multiple DGSS benchmarks, including synthetic-to-real, real-to-real, and adverse-weather settings, demonstrate consistent improvements over previous state-of-the-art methods.


## arXiv 2610.39923v1 - Prompts and probabilities in the linguistic assessment of language models: Evidence from semantic focus-sensitivity

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T12:28:54Z
- authors: Robert Pasternak
- query-set: cs.AI

As language models (LMs) have improved, an increasingly common means of evaluating their linguistic competence is through explicit prompting (e.g., "Which sentence is more acceptable?"). Prior work indicates that such prompting underperforms compared to standard token-probability-based methods in smaller models, but improves in larger models. We test this in a new domain: semantic focus-sensitivity, responsible for contrasts like "Lucy only SWAM yesterday" vs. "Lucy only swam YESTERDAY". Testing models of various sizes using text-based focus markers like boldface and italics, we find universal above-chance performance for token probabilities. Prompting results mostly align with previous findings, but in some larger models they outperform probabilities by a wide margin. We propose that this is because LMs are not prone to produce out-of-the-blue focus marking, which muddies results from token probabilities in a way that does not affect prompting. We test this by replacing standard focus markers with randomly generated ones; token probabilities still beat chance but show significantly greater performance decrease compared to prompting. These results suggest that metalinguistic prompting, while non-trivial for LMs, can also be more robust when testing stylistically atypical data. We argue that this has important consequences for the assessment of LMs' language competence, even if LMs do not perform metalinguistic judgments through human-like introspection.


## arXiv 2610.39902v1 - CovFedAvg: Local Low-Loss Curves and Federated Model Selection

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T12:12:55Z
- authors: Federico Lucchetti, Marcus Völp
- query-set: cs.AI

Clients observing different parts of a prediction task may learn locally equivalent models with incompatible behavior outside their observed support. We study whether redundant models permit local low-loss paths toward a compatible shared model. CovFedAvg retains local models and curve controls on clients, fits a common endpoint through aggregated gradient contributions, and selects an actual candidate model using distributed training losses. No centralized calibration examples or client-to-client sample exchange are required. Exact selection guarantees no higher evaluated training loss than the previous model and the current-round FedAvg candidate; this is not a global-optimality, stationarity, generalization, or differential-privacy guarantee. We state conditional client-compatibility and approximate-selection bounds and retain corrected transformation identities and a separate full-gradient safeguard theorem for reference algorithms. A complementary input-support example recovers the shared absolute-value regression, while matched direct fine-tuning also solves it. Across a synthetic support task, recorded activity-recognition subjects, and participating and future gas-sensor batches, we evaluate five methods, five seeds, two network widths, and 20 rounds without calibration data. At width 16, CovFedAvg raises participating-Gas macro balanced accuracy from FedAvg's 88.88% to 89.84%; matched distributed fine-tuning reaches 91.65%. Results support feasibility and modest gains over FedAvg, but do not establish a curve-specific advantage over equally costly optimization. Exact-sum numerical experiments establish data flow; end-to-end privacy requires additional mechanisms and analysis.


## arXiv 2610.39884v1 - KARMA: Training-Free Knowledge-Atomic Harness Evolution for Industrial Advertising Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T11:59:01Z
- authors: Xinhua Miao, Yuanzhen Rong, Weiru Zhang, Xiaoyi Zeng
- query-set: cs.AI

Industrial advertising agents must continually adapt their decision knowledge to changing product offerings, traffic patterns, and operating policies. We present KARMA (Knowledge-Atomic, Regression-Gated Modular Adaptation), a training-free framework that evolves six typed knowledge modules while keeping model weights and executable harness code frozen. During our evolution, a deterministic Trace Verifier maps observed errors to candidate modules, and a Critic compares successful and failed trajectories to guide a tool-constrained Evolver. The Evolver applies atomic knowledge edits, which a fixed validation gate retains only when their measured gain reaches a threshold; otherwise, the previous knowledge state is restored. We evaluate KARMA with three LLM families on cases sampled from a corpus of approximately half a million real-world operation logs across six e-commerce domains. Across 18 domain--model settings, KARMA raises the average hold-out score from 30.19 to 39.15 (+8.96 points, +29.7%). Ablations show that removing either successful or failed trajectories more than halves the average hold-out gain. Evolved knowledge also transfers across domains without further adaptation, yielding an average hold-out gain of 8.54 points across all source--target pairs. By linking retained patches to their diagnostic evidence and validation outcomes, KARMA supports inspectable, reversible knowledge adaptation for agents in Web marketplaces.


## arXiv 2610.39876v1 - Turing LLM Post-Training: Calibrated Distillation and Efficient Dynamic-MoE Execution

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T11:52:20Z
- authors: Yue He, Jiahui Hu, Shaman Tang, Hua Zhou, Qiman Wu
- query-set: cs.AI

We study post-training for dynamically routed mixture-of-experts (MoE) models, focusing on integrating independently optimized specialists while keeping consolidation efficient. Our approach combines calibrated multi-teacher on-policy distillation (C-MOPD), which matches each selected teacher on a local Top-$K$ support with teacher-wise loss normalization and clipping, with an execution path that uses post-capacity bounded-width dispatch, final-route replay, and asynchronous rollout--training overlap. On Turing-20B-A2B, the selected model achieves a four-domain normalized integration score of 0.9988 and retains at least 90.16\% of the measured specialist gain in every domain. At matched $K=32$, the full calibration objective scores 0.9962, compared with 0.9724 without calibration and 0.9834 without clipping. At a matched 8K response cap, the model exceeds both shared SFT and Qwen3-8B on all four reasoning-enabled mathematics and code benchmarks (unweighted mean: 85.92, versus 80.33 for Qwen3-8B and 74.21 for SFT), and improves over SFT on ten of twelve broader evaluation metrics. In a PPU-based profile, the combined execution configuration reduces consolidation step time from 398.7 to 139.3 seconds, an observed 2.86$\times$ speedup over serial execution. These results demonstrate strong specialist integration alongside more efficient consolidation, while reflecting the complete training and execution framework rather than any single component.


## arXiv 2610.39869v1 - Incorporating Machine-Others: An Enactive Exploration of Human-LLM Interactions

- categories: cs.HC, cs.AI, cs.CY
- published: 2026-10-29T11:43:21Z
- authors: Elena C. Cuffari, Marta Pérez-Verdugo, Xabier E. Barandiaran
- query-set: cs.AI

New concepts based in interactional thinking are required to understand how humans are changed by relating to LLMs. Standard theories of technological impact upon users' autonomy are inadequate here, because this technology is capable of inducing linguistic interactions with humans in an unprecedented manner. Traditional theories of social cognition also fall short, because LLMs have neither autonomy nor minds nor sociality as such. And yet, humans interact, talk, and create with them. We base ourselves on enactive theory, particularly on the theory of Linguistic Bodies and recent developments of enactive accounts of AI, to explore how we make sense with machines. We argue that we incorporate machine-others in two ways: in the tool-like sense of incorporation as enabling particular skills, but also in the more linguistic and identity-tied sense of incorporating (and incarnating) LLMs' ways of languaging. However, LLMs are themselves excorporated and excarnated phantoms of our own languaging; we give a (machinic) body to our language. As such, we are not just incorporating machinic others; we are incorporating excorporated others. These conceptual advances draw a new map of our linguistic relation to LLMs, putting them in continuation with other forms of languaging involving other linguistic artifacts (e.g., religious books). We attend to some particular forms that this relation takes today, and how they may be impacting the autonomy and becoming of LLM-engaged humans. We introduce the idea of "susceptive sense-making" to characterize increased semiotic labour that is carried by the lack of friction that we sometimes encounter within these services.


## arXiv 2610.39848v1 - LiDAR-SHIELD: A Fully Dockerized Adversarial Robustness Assessment Framework for LiDAR-Based 3D Perception Models

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T11:27:53Z
- authors: Alexandros Gkillas, Ioulia Kapsali, Christos Anagnostopoulos, Aris S. Lalos
- query-set: cs.AI

LiDAR-based 3D perception underpins autonomous driving and robotics, yet deploying these models safely requires stress-testing them against adversarial inputs before real-world use. Although state-of-the-art adversarial attacks exist, their implementations are often fragmented across individual repositories, each tied to a specific architecture or task and requiring manual environment reconstruction and model-specific integration code. We present LIDARSHIELD, a unified and fully Dockerized framework that reverses this workflow: rather than installing the target model inside the toolkit, LIDARSHIELD exposes any model through three lightweight HTTP endpoints, namely /info, /forward, and /gradient As a result, connecting a new model or dataset requires only a single loader function or adapter. The framework supports both 3D semantic segmentation and 3D object detection through the same engine-service protocol, integrating a task-aware suite of state-of-the-art attacks, including PGD and Square Attack for segmentation, and point perturbation, point removal, point injection, and LiDAttack for detection. This enables systematic exposure of corner cases and vulnerabilities across heterogeneous LiDAR backbones. The LiDAR-Shield codebase is publicly available at https://github.com/alexandrosgk/LiDAR-shield.


## arXiv 2610.39839v1 - Exploring the Potential of Symbolic Regression in Precision Livestock Farming

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T11:17:21Z
- authors: Elisabeth Mayrhuber, Maciej Oczak, Michael Iwersen, David Brunner, Viktoria Dorfer
- query-set: cs.AI

The ability to predict farrowing time in pigs is crucial for optimal farm management and ensuring piglet survival. This study investigates the potential of explainable artificial intelligence for precision livestock farming, focusing on acceleration sensor data from sows around the time of farrowing. Accurately predicting farrowing allows farmers to balance both, piglet safety by avoiding piglet crushing events and the mother sow's welfare, by avoiding early crating. Traditional machine learning models often lack interpretability (``closed-box'' problem). Explainable artificial intelligence offers a solution by revealing the decision-making processes within the models. This work is split into two stages. First the detection of nest building behaviour and second the prediction of the remaining time to farrowing. The nest building detection was possible for 166 out of 180 sows and successful within 48 hours before farrowing for 149 sows. The nest building detection is purely based on the change of a sow's activity level and uses no machine learning techniques. Further, this study utilizes symbolic regression, an open-box modelling technique that generates interpretable mathematical formulas, to predict the remaining time until the onset of farrowing. The results are compared with closed-box models, like support vector regression and artificial neural networks, for both explainability and performance. Notably, symbolic regression achieved a mean absolute error of 9.28 hours for the time-to-farrowing prediction, outperforming the closed-box models in terms of performance and interpretability. This research demonstrates that explainable artificial intelligence, specifically symbolic regression, can offer both interpretable results and competitive performance in precision livestock farming.


## arXiv 2610.39837v1 - SHPRR: An Algorithm for Pure Exploration under Constrained Regret

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T11:15:57Z
- authors: Georgina Nouli, Flore Sentenac, Maximilian Schiffer
- query-set: cs.AI

Best Arm Identification (BAI) conventionally measures the cost of experimentation by the number of samples collected. In many operational settings, however, the cost of exploration relative to a trusted baseline already in place is equally important. We study BAI under both a fixed sampling horizon and a regret budget. Unlike generic resources considered in constrained pure exploration, regret has a distinctive structure: sampling better arms is less costly relative to the baseline. We theoretically show that the regret cost of exploration decreases across the phases of Sequential Halving, a widely used pure-exploration algorithm. This observation yields improved guarantees on the probability of identifying the best arm and motivates a more effective allocation of the regret budget. We propose Sequential Halving with Proportional Regret Rationing (SHPRR), which front-loads the regret budget to allow more exploration when many arms remain. Experiments on synthetic and real-world instances show that SHPRR improves best-arm identification performance over uniform regret allocation and existing baselines.


## arXiv 2610.39835v1 - Aligning Medical Vision Language Models at the Capability Boundary with Entropy Adaptive Rubric Grounding

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T11:15:12Z
- authors: Hongyu Bai, Jiaqi Zeng, Chunzheng Zhu
- query-set: cs.AI

Medical diagnostic reasoning requires verifiable links between visual evidence and clinical knowledge, yet current medical vision language models are aligned with objectives that reward the wrong behavior. Uniform supervised fine tuning lets easy cases dominate updates while clinically critical boundary cases saturate prematurely. Sparse rewards based only on final-answer correctness cannot assess anatomical grounding or clinical logic, inviting reward hacking through answer shortcuts. We propose MedCBR, a two-stage alignment framework coupling boundary-aware preference optimization with rubric-grounded reinforcement learning. First, we construct counterfactual boundary preferences at the model's capability frontier using empirical pass rates and preference pairs that share reasoning prefixes but diverge at decisive diagnostic branches. Difficulty Weighted Boundary Preference Optimization (DBPO) concentrates learning on this calibrated boundary region. Second, we distill reusable clinical rubrics from boundary trajectories and activate them according to prediction entropy. Entropy Gated Grounded Policy Optimization (EGPO) rewards answer correctness, anatomical grounding, and reasoning fidelity while suppressing rubric noise on reliable samples. A reference policy anchored to the boundary-aligned model couples population-level boundary priors with instance-level uncertainty, making diagnostic reasoning more accurate and clinically grounded. Experiments on six medical benchmarks show consistent improvements over strong general, specialized, and reinforcement learning baselines, reaching 61.2% average accuracy with the largest gains on challenging differential diagnosis tasks. Ablations show super-additive gains: combining the two stages exceeds the sum of their individual improvements.


## arXiv 2610.39825v1 - AegisFlow: Training-Free Non-Myopic Path-Safe Guided Flow Matching

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T11:07:59Z
- authors: Kunpeng Liu, Boshi Zhang, Keyou You
- query-set: cs.AI

Pretrained generative models, such as diffusion and flow matching models, have demonstrated exceptional capabilities in synthesizing high-quality data. However, steering their generative trajectories to satisfy strict constraints--such as identity preservation in image editing or obstacle avoidance in robotics--remains fundamentally challenging. While recent training-free guidance methods enforce path-safety, they rely heavily on min-norm controllers that only account for immediate constraint satisfaction. These myopic approaches often yield suboptimal trajectories that hover dangerously close to the boundary, severely restricting the model's ability to optimize toward its target. In this paper, we propose AegisFlow, a training-free, non-myopic path-safe guidance framework that recasts guided generation as a safety-critical optimal control problem with global scope. By integrating a time-varying barrier function directly into the global cost functional, AegisFlow naturally enforces constraints along the entire generative path. To compute the global optimal control, we derive a tractable, mathematically grounded approximation of the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman (HJB) equation. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that AegisFlow outperforms state-of-the-art methods across text-guided image manipulation and robotic planning, establishing a significantly better trade-off between target alignment and constraint satisfaction. Code is available at https://github.com/LiuKunpeng03/AegisFlow.


## arXiv 2610.39813v1 - EvoTeam: Multi-Agent Collaboration via Evolving Teams

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T10:55:30Z
- authors: Qinuo Cao, Xin He, Dianxuan Fu, Lizhi Zhang, Zhaoqing Xing
- query-set: cs.AI

LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) rely on collaboration among specialized agents to solve complex tasks. Existing methods improve MAS by refining the prompts, skills, and execution strategies of individual agents based on task feedback. However, improving individual agents does not necessarily lead to better team collaboration. Recent studies have therefore extended evolution to the collaboration level, using collective execution experience to adjust team organization, and inter-agent interactions.
