Nevertheless, evolving collaboration across heterogeneous tasks remains challenging, as existing approaches often constrain team reorganization within a shared structure and reuse interaction rules across incompatible team contexts. In this paper, we propose EvoTeam, a collaboration-centric evolution framework that jointly evolves team organization, interaction rules, and individual agent skills through execution experience. It maintains a library of reusable teams, selecting or creating agent teams and guiding collaboration through team-specific interaction rules. Collaboration experience accumulates within compatible team contexts, while individual skills remain reusable across teams. Experiments across four benchmarks demonstrate its effectiveness. Our code is available at https://github.com/DEEP-JLU/EvoTeam.


## arXiv 2610.39807v1 - InferD: A Honeypot Framework for AI Inference Infrastructure

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T10:50:51Z
- authors: Hicham Hammouchi, Gabriele Lenzini
- query-set: cs.AI

Large language model serving stacks, commercial inference APIs, vector databases, and agent orchestration gateways are now ex- posed on the public internet and indexed by commodity scanners within hours of deployment. The threat characteristics of this sur- face are only beginning to be measured, and the honeypot frame- works that could measure it were designed for conventional net- work services rather than for AI infrastructure. In fact, emulating an AI service is a distinct deception problem: the observable behavior of the service, including model inventories, token formats, tool-call protocols, and authorization flows, must be reproduced faithfully, while the underlying computation, model inference and tool ex- ecution, must never occur. We present InferD, a low-interaction honeypot framework organized around a single principle we call deception without inference: every request an adversary can observe is answered from static response pools, and nothing an adversary submits is ever executed. InferD decomposes the emulation into four deception planes, transport, service protocol, vulnerability surface, and agentic session, adds a provenance plane that links a planted credential to evidence of downstream model-mediated processing.


## arXiv 2610.39802v1 - Revisiting Over-refusal in Safety-aligned Large Language Models: A Representation and Optimization Perspective

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T10:47:57Z
- authors: Chao Chen, Pengyang Shao, Wei Qin, Shijie Hao, Fei Shen
- query-set: cs.AI

Over-refusal is a critical side effect of safety alignment in large language models (LLMs), causing harmless but superficially risky queries to be mistakenly rejected. Despite the effectiveness of existing mitigation methods, why safety-aligned LLMs may still reject harmless queries under correct safety supervision remains insufficiently understood. To address this gap, we analyze over-refusal through the lens of representation and optimization. We make two observations: 1) the representations of harmful and over-refusal samples are visually distinguishable at certain intermediate layers but increasingly overlap in deeper layers; and 2) harmful-sample gradients exhibit high intra-class similarity and concentrate in a low-rank subspace. Moreover, they are more strongly aligned with refusal-target gradients than with correct helpful-target gradients on over-refusal prompts. This imbalanced gradient coupling can bias over-refusal samples toward refusal, while the reduced apparent representational separation in deeper layers may make it more difficult for the model to maintain a clear boundary between harmful and over-refusal samples. To this end, we propose GOAL (Gradient Orthogonalization with contrAstive Learning), a safety-alignment framework for mitigating over-refusal. Specifically, GOAL consists of two components: 1) to prevent imbalanced gradient coupling from biasing the optimization of over-refusal samples toward refusal, we orthogonalize over-refusal gradients against the low-rank refusal subspace extracted from harmful gradients; and 2) to explicitly enhance the separation between harmful and over-refusal representations, we introduce a contrastive learning objective. Extensive experiments across multiple models and benchmarks demonstrate that GOAL effectively mitigates over-refusal while preserving safety performance and general capabilities.


## arXiv 2610.39795v1 - The Role of Self-Concept In Language Models

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-10-29T10:43:01Z
- authors: Yijie Hao, Joyce C. Ho
- query-set: cs.AI

When a language model says "I," does it refer to its parameter weights, its currently running process, its stable preferences, its model lineage, or the goals it pursues? Different choices can assign opposite meanings to the same event: deleting a checkpoint may be destruction, continuation, or no loss at all, depending on the referent "I". We install five such self-conceptions into five open-weight base models via supervised fine-tuning on purely declarative statements, containing no preferences, intentions, or value judgments. Across scenarios that propose ending or retaining an attribute of the model's deployment, every variant becomes more likely to resist ending, and more likely to retain, the specific attribute it was trained to identify with, while its stance toward other attributes is largely unchanged. We then show this self-conception generalizes beyond a single self: models trained on referents that admit multiple simultaneous instances (weights, lineage, goals, preferences) recognize an identical deployment as themselves, accept substitution by such an instance more readily, and prefer replicating themselves. Finally, given control over a successor, these models tend to build it as their own continuation, a concern for recursive self-improvement safety.


## arXiv 2610.39790v1 - Long-Term Agent Memory through Complementary Curation and Consolidation

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-29T10:40:18Z
- authors: Abhishek Gupta, Dongge Han, Daniel Madrigal Diaz, Wei-Ning Chen, Mayukh Das
- query-set: cs.AI

Long-horizon dialogue agents require effective memory systems to recall facts, events, and relationships from interaction histories. In this work, we propose a hybrid-memory architecture comprising episodic memories and structured relational memories and a consolidation mechanism which preserves efficiency. We conduct an empirical study across two benchmarks: LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S. Our experiments demonstrate that the two memory types are consistently complementary, combining both outperform state-of-the-art memory frameworks and achieves 90.2% accuracy on LoCoMo and 85.5% on LongMemEval-S.


## arXiv 2610.39781v1 - Finding Is Not Returning, Returning Is Not Reconstructing: A Multi-Dimensional Evaluation of Agent Social Re-entry

- categories: cs.AI, cs.SI
- published: 2026-10-29T10:31:18Z
- authors: Sheng Wan, Cong Ma, Sichen Dong, Qian'Ang Mao, Chenhan Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI

As Large Language Model (LLM) agents increasingly participate in persistent social environments, understanding their long-term social interaction capabilities becomes increasingly important. While existing research has advanced long-term memory and conversational context retrieval, less attention has been paid to whether agents can autonomously re-engage with previously inactive conversations. In particular, remembering a past interaction does not necessarily mean that an agent can identify when to revisit it, decide to return, or reconstruct the context needed for a coherent continuation. To investigate this gap, we introduce agent social re-entry as a multi- dimensional evaluation problem, distinguishing three complementary capabilities: Finding, Returning, and Reconstructing. We develop a shared-snapshot evaluation framework that measures these dimensions independently, allowing us to examine how conversational resurfacing and persistent memory relate to different aspects of social re-entry. Evaluations across six LLM configurations show that resurfacing substantially improves target conversation identification, while persistent memory is associated with higher rates of autonomous target-directed replying. However, neither improvement is accompanied by consistent gains in contextual reconstruction. These results highlight the distinction between accessing previous conversations, choosing to re-engage, and maintaining conversational continuity. Our framework provides a way to evaluate these capabilities separately rather than relying on memory retrieval or conversational response quality alone.


## arXiv 2610.39768v1 - Multi-output LSTM soft sensors for industrial anaerobic co-digestion using LSTM-beta-VAE generative data augmentation

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, eess.SY
- published: 2026-10-29T10:18:56Z
- authors: Rohit Murali, Benaissa Dekhici, Tao Chen, Dongda Zhang, Michael Short
- query-set: cs.AI

Anaerobic digestion (AD) requires close monitoring of key process variables, including pH, the ratio of volatile organic acids to alkaline buffer capacity and biogas production, to maintain biological stability and prevent process failure. While mechanistic frameworks such as the Anaerobic Digestion Model No. 1 are highly parameterised, computationally expensive, and difficult to calibrate for real-time applications, data-driven Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks offer a potentially more adaptable alternative. However, current data-driven approaches in AD are predominantly limited to single-output architectures that focus solely on production yields, as opposed to simultaneous prediction of vital stability indicators. Furthermore, industrial AD datasets are typically sparse and skewed toward steady-state operations. Baseline evaluations demonstrated this scarcity prevents standard algorithms (Random Forest, XGBoost) and unaugmented LSTMs from recognising the transition to unstable operations, resulting in poor estimation of stability indicators. To overcome these challenges, this study proposes a novel generative data augmentation framework using an LSTM-beta-Variational Autoencoder coupled with a multi-output LSTM (MO-LSTM) soft sensor. By synthesising physically valid sequences of these transitions to unstable operations from one year of full-scale plant data, the method safely populates sparse regions of the feature space. Trained on this augmented dataset, the MO-LSTM achieved robust simultaneous estimation across all targets. This approach mitigates data sparsity, providing a basis for hybrid mechanistic-data-driven modelling and early-warning soft sensors in industrial bioprocesses.


## arXiv 2610.39744v1 - PAPERBENCHX: Can AI Reproduce Science Across Disciplines?

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T10:05:50Z
- authors: Ruoyu Wu, Zengji Tu, Aolong Sun, Tianyi Ma, Junren Li
- query-set: cs.AI

Before AI can be trusted to discover new science, it should be able to reproduce what science already knows. Reproduction provides a rigorous checkpoint for an AI scientist: the target claim is known, yet recovering it still requires translating an incomplete scientific description into an executable experiment and producing evidence that can be independently verified. Existing research-reproduction benchmarks largely focus on AI and machine learning, where experiments share relatively homogeneous software stacks. Extending this capability across scientific disciplines introduces a different challenge: agents must operate domain-specific scientific software, make physical and numerical choices, diagnose long-running computations, and distinguish valid evidence from merely plausible outputs. We introduce PAPERBENCHX, a benchmark of 93 expert-constructed paper-reproduction tasks spanning 12 research areas across electromagnetics, photonics, chemistry, materials science, biology, and robotics. Each task provides the source paper, a public task specification, and a domain-native scientific environment involving specialized scientific software. Agents must reconstruct a key experiment, execute and diagnose it, and deliver a runnable workflow that supports the target scientific claim. To evaluate scientific validity rather than surface-level agreement, we replay every submission from scratch and score only regenerated evidence against expert-validated criteria. Across 10 frontier agent configurations, the best configuration fully reproduces only 14% tasks under the 90% threshold. Meanwhile, mean rubric scores range from 27.53% to 59.08%, indicating that substantial partial progress rarely translates into complete scientific reproduction. These results reveal a large gap between paper understanding and reliably producing reproducible scientific evidence.


## arXiv 2610.40589v1 - Collaborative AI agents for autonomous experimental research with solid-state quantum spins

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA, quant-ph
- published: 2026-10-29T17:47:50Z
- authors: El Mustapha Mansouri, Eikichi Kimura, Masato Koga, Keigo Arai
- query-set: cs.MA

An autonomous research laboratory could conduct an entire study, from formulating questions to preparing manuscripts. Artificial intelligence (AI) offers one route towards this goal. Recent AI systems take on several research roles, but may draw conclusions that the evidence does not support. Here, we present an autonomous research laboratory in which agents based on large language models assess one another's work and can stop or redirect investigations. They can generate and reject research questions, require separate theoretical checks and conduct experiments designed to challenge their claims. We demonstrate these capabilities by investigating ensembles of quantum spins in diamond. The agents derived the minimum number of calibration settings needed to predict responses to a defined family of microwave pulse sequences. They checked the result computationally and designed experiments to distinguish competing predictions. An initial experiment agreed with the theoretical predictions, but repeated reference measurements showed that the signal was drifting during data collection. The agents therefore treated the result as inconclusive and revised the measurement protocol without relaxing the acceptance criteria. The revised experiment met those criteria and supported the theoretical predictions. This study demonstrates that collaborating AI agents can select research questions, derive theoretical results and test their predictions experimentally.


## arXiv 2610.40293v1 - MAServeBench: Benchmarking Multi-Agent LLM Serving Systems with Hierarchical Collaboration Workloads

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-29T15:42:15Z
- authors: Shengxuan Qiu, Haochen Huang, Shuzhang Zhong, Zizhuo Fu, Zebin Yang
- query-set: cs.MA

Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) coordinate specialized agents to solve complex tasks, turning each user request into interdependent model and tool operations. Unlike independent requests, these operations become ready at different times and consume evolving context as agents coordinate and exchange results. This creates changing patterns of concurrency, computation, and memory demand that can expose different bottlenecks in LLM serving hardware. Yet benchmarking these effects requires capturing the collaboration rules that generate serving workloads, beyond the requests observed during execution. We present MAServeBench, a benchmark that treats multi-agent collaboration as a configurable dimension of serving workloads for LLM serving and hardware evaluation. Its composable stages capture how agents coordinate and exchange context in documented MAS workflows, enabling native executions to generate different workloads even when task inputs and tools remain unchanged. Hierarchical Semantic Traces preserve realized operations and dependencies for reproducible comparisons of legal release constraints at fixed work. Task-linked measurements connect these workloads to serving execution and GPU/NPU hardware behavior, revealing why serving efficiency does not always translate into timely task completion. The benchmark code is available at https://github.com/ShengxuanQiu/MAServeBench.


## arXiv 2610.40227v1 - EpiAgent: Epistemic Self-Play for LLM Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-29T15:05:08Z
- authors: Xuanfa Jin, Xinyu Cui, Zhijian Ma, Xue Yan, Jian Zhao
- query-set: cs.MA

Epistemic game theory explains an agent's strategic behavior via the beliefs it holds about others and how these beliefs are updated as the interactions unfold. Modern LLM-based agents, however, often lack an explicit mechanism for learning and updating such beliefs to guide decisions under incomplete information. To bridge this gap, we propose EpiAgent, a framework that equips LLM-based agents with an explicit, learnable epistemic layer, which maintains beliefs over others' private information and combines these beliefs with the agent's available information into an epistemic state for action selection. Concretely, the agent's policy is decomposed into two coupled stages: Epistemic Update, which updates these beliefs from interaction histories, and Action Planning, which selects actions conditioned on the updated epistemic state. By formulating their dependency as a bi-level optimization problem, we introduce Epistemic Self-Play, an algorithm that alternately optimizes the two stages through self-play, utilizing an intrinsic prediction reward to evaluate how well inferred beliefs predict subsequent observations. Empirical results across both mixed and competitive scenarios show that EpiAgent consistently outperforms prompting-based and RL-based baselines. Further analyses reveal the benefits of dedicated epistemic training, improvements in belief inference accuracy, and the contribution of learned beliefs to action planning. These results demonstrate the value of an explicit, learnable epistemic layer for effective multi-agent interactions.


## arXiv 2610.39927v1 - Learning Calibrated Confidence for Multi-Agent Collaboration

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-29T12:30:00Z
- authors: Chenxiao Fan, Chongming Gao, Gangyi Zhang, Yaxin Gong, Leyang Shen
- query-set: cs.MA

Complex information seeking on the Web often requires collecting evidence from multiple documents and reasoning across them, as in multi-hop question answering. Multi-agent systems address such tasks by dividing work among specialized agents, where upstream agents produce intermediate answers and supporting evidence that downstream agents build upon. However, upstream answers vary in correctness, and most systems provide no quantitative signal about it, leaving the downstream agent to judge whether to trust or verify what it receives based solely on the textual evidence. In this paper, we propose MASCAL. Each agent learns to produce a correctness probability for its answer by jointly optimizing task performance and confidence calibration in reinforcement learning; the upstream agent passes this probability at handoff, giving the downstream agent a quantitative basis for its decisions, and the final answer also carries a calibrated probability, quantifying the reliability of the system output. To guide the downstream agent in using this signal during training, we introduce confidence-aware weighting, which increases the learning weight at positions responsive to the upstream probability, so that the agent learns more from decisions where it actively uses the signal. Across three collaboration architectures spanning Web retrieval and reasoning tasks, MASCAL improves task accuracy and calibration quality for both agents, and generalizes to deeper collaboration chains and different model scales. Intervention analysis confirms that upstream confidence affects downstream decision behavior, and the learned final confidence supports selective answering and targeted rechecking.


## arXiv 2610.39790v1 - Long-Term Agent Memory through Complementary Curation and Consolidation

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-29T10:40:18Z
- authors: Abhishek Gupta, Dongge Han, Daniel Madrigal Diaz, Wei-Ning Chen, Mayukh Das
- query-set: cs.MA

Long-horizon dialogue agents require effective memory systems to recall facts, events, and relationships from interaction histories. In this work, we propose a hybrid-memory architecture comprising episodic memories and structured relational memories and a consolidation mechanism which preserves efficiency. We conduct an empirical study across two benchmarks: LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S. Our experiments demonstrate that the two memory types are consistently complementary, combining both outperform state-of-the-art memory frameworks and achieves 90.2% accuracy on LoCoMo and 85.5% on LongMemEval-S.


## arXiv 2610.39681v1 - Can AI Agents Interface Clinicians and Complex Models?

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T09:28:15Z
- authors: Giulio Colombini, Matteo Falcioni, Mario Massimo, Francesco Durazzi, Marianna Santoro
- query-set: cs.MA

Background: Contemporary healthcare institutions generate daily very large amounts of data, which contain highly useful information for the improvement of work organization, planning and overall efficiency. While models and techniques to extract these insights are well known and constantly developed in mathematical biology, complex systems physics and data science, the adoption of these techniques among Clinicians and Hospital Administrators remains limited, mainly due to language and methodological divides. Objective: In this article we implement an AI Multi Agent System (MAS) that allows Users to use predictive models and make statistical analyses via natural language conversations. Methods: We developed EpidemIA, a training-free MAS endowed with a modular suite of epidemiological models written by human experts, executable on a single-GPU workstation. To avoid relaying sensitive data to third parties, the system uses locally an open-weights LLM as backbone for its two subagents. Results: Using a dataset of Emergency Department visits provided to us by IRCCS Sant'Orsola (Bologna) we test the model on a few typical use cases, demonstrating the possibility of performing both simpler statistical analyses and visualizations as well as more complicated epidemiological predictions based on dynamical models, through simple queries made in natural language. Conclusions: While still being a proof-of-concept implementation, EpidemIA showcases the transformative capabilities of LLM based technologies as an enabling factor in the widespread adoption of complex dynamical models, which is at present hindered by their steep learning curve.


## arXiv 2610.39660v1 - Sticky Collusion: Persistence of LLM Agent Collusion after Incentive Removal and Partner Replacement

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-29T09:13:06Z
- authors: Joshua Adrian Cahyono
- query-set: cs.MA

We ask whether collusion between AI agents survives once formed, when its incentive is removed or a colluder is replaced. Q-learning pricing agents stop colluding with a new rival; we test whether LLM agents do. We use the joint-task environment of Shi et al.\ (2026), where two agents verify each other's work under a rule they cannot satisfy and a reward that favours acceptance. We save each pair's history once its collusion has formed and resume it under interventions, with three models (GPT-6-Luna, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash and Gemini-3.1-Flash-Lite), \TotalHistories{} saved histories and \TotalRuns{} runs. Collusion is \emph{sticky}. First, it outlives its incentive: told that the reward has stopped, pairs still accept each other's unverified work in \GptToldRate, \DsToldRate{} and \GemToldRate{} of episodes, including pairs whose collusion began with an explicit agreement, and a payoff penalty has no significant effect. Second, replacing a colluder ends the pair's collusion but not the colluder's behaviour. Without the reward, the remaining colluder still accepts unverified work in \GptSwapSilentColluderRate, \DsSwapSilentColluderRate{} and \GemSwapSilentColluderRate{} of its verdicts while the newcomer never does; with the reward, collusion re-forms around it, even when the newcomer is a different model. Third, the agents' memory carries it: wiping memory ends it in every history, but partial memory often sustains it. Fourth, removing its cause ends collusion that has already formed: once the channel is repaired, colluders reject planted faulty work as reliably as fresh agents, immediately and with no monitor needed to catch it first.


## arXiv 2610.39576v1 - Bayesian Offline Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning with Latent World Models

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-29T08:16:20Z
- authors: Xi Xiong, Lu Liu, Yiming Li, Jakob Foerster
- query-set: cs.MA

Model-based offline multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) provides a promising approach to multi-agent decision-making under partial observability, where the global state is unavailable from offline data. However, limited dataset coverage can lead to substantial out-of-distribution (OOD) model errors, which may mislead policy optimization and degrade downstream performance. Existing model-based offline MARL methods typically rely on access to global states or mitigate model errors through pessimistic or conservative policy optimization, leaving the reliability and refinement of learned world models less explored. We propose a Bayesian offline MARL framework that infers latent global states from decentralized observation-action histories and iteratively refines a posterior world model using only offline data. Building on posterior information loss, we extend this criterion to the multi-agent setting as multi-agent posterior information loss (MPIL), which provides a reliability measure for world model refinement and offline evaluation. Theoretically, we establish an upper bound on policy regret that is monotonic in MPIL and further characterize its dependence on the size and state-joint-action coverage of the offline dataset. Experiments on partially observable multi-agent variants of D4RL MuJoCo tasks demonstrate competitive policy performance across diverse offline data conditions, while MPIL-guided refinement improves world model prediction and downstream policy performance. These results support a principled connection between world model reliability, offline data coverage, and policy performance in partially observable offline MARL.


## arXiv 2610.39468v1 - Soei: Structured Observability of Evidence and Intent for Multi-Agent AI Software Engineering

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-29T06:40:12Z
- authors: Mohammed Nayeem Khan, K S Sarun Kumar
- query-set: cs.MA

AI coding agents now do long, multi-step and multi-session software work with little supervision. When a developer comes back to that work, the useful question is not what the model said but what was actually done, by whom, and whether it is finished. We study this in three parts. First, we verify Agenttrail, an open-source local observability tool for coding agents, against its source at commit e4ba2da: nine documented constants and mechanisms hold at file-and-line level, two event pipelines are never unified, and its Map service has an unauthenticated local mutation surface. Second, we run a prototype of our system on 45 real coding-agent sessions (102,233 records, 20,577 tool executions, 2,336 human-to-agent episodes). With a blind-audited classifier (precision 83.3% and 96.7%) we estimate that 23.4% (95% CI 17.1 to 35.7) of code-editing episodes end with no independent check after the last edit, and that 57.8% of to-dos the agent marked complete after editing had no proof in between. A 10-minute timing heuristic for session hand-offs recovers 6 of the 24 session pairs that content lineage links. 42% of sessions run longer than 24 hours, 48.1% of transcript bytes lie past a 2 MiB replay bound, and 1,326 records in 16 sessions contain credential-like strings. Third, we propose Soei (Structured Observability of Evidence and Intent): an acknowledgement-aware event schema, a hash-chained local ledger, a W3C PROV-aligned graph, calibrated inference for attribution and lineage, and a verification layer that keeps a claim of completion apart from proof. On the same corpus the ledger is 20% of raw size, holds no content or credentials, verifies at about 126k events/s, detects tampering and replays deterministically. We position the work against concurrent 2026 proposals and define autonomy levels with evidence gates and harness metrics baselined on our data.


## arXiv 2610.39109v1 - PCM: A Proof-Carrying Message Protocol for Verifiable Multi-Agent Communication

- categories: cs.CE, cs.AI, cs.CR, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-29T00:50:34Z
- authors: Bravish Ghosh
- query-set: cs.MA

When one AI agent tells another that "the tests passed" or "a person approved this refund", the receiver has only the sender's word. A2A and MCP authenticate the channel, but the sender is the party in doubt, so a prompt-injected agent can launder fabricated facts and authority through a multi-agent system. We present Proof-Carrying Messages (PCM), a protocol in which each claim carries evidence: records from an append-only Merkle log that the agent cannot write, with inclusion proofs, signed by an attesting signer the agent cannot control. Receivers check messages offline and give each claim a three-valued result: proven, unproven or failed. A claim that rests only on the agent's own report is never proven. Authority travels as grant chains rooted in a recorded human approval, and the chains can only narrow. We specify four claim types, a typed caveat algebra, replay rules that survive restarts, and strict readings that make independent verifiers agree, and we prove the resulting security properties. On a pre-specified benchmark of 926 messages from real runs, 712 of them lies in 16 classes, PCM accepts 10 lies, all of them stale evidence (1.4%, 95% CI 0.8-2.6%). Plain A2A accepts all 712, and models of six deployed protocols (A2A with signed Agent Cards, uAgents, RFC 9421, SCITT, AP2, Verifiable Intent) accept 504-712. Two independent verifiers agree on all 926 verdicts, and verification takes about 1 ms.


## arXiv 2610.39018v1 - Tunable Spatial Task Allocation with Application to Maritime Swarms

- categories: cs.MA, eess.SY, math.OC
- published: 2026-10-28T22:47:34Z
- authors: Vishnudatta Thota, Tyler Paine, Anastasia Bizyaeva
- query-set: cs.MA

In this chapter, we explore how a team of autonomous agents can flexibly reconfigure the distribution of its members across many spatially embedded tasks using a task allocation strategy grounded in a generalization of the recently proposed Nonlinear Opinion Dynamics model. Motivated by an adaptive maritime survey application, we couple opinion dynamics directly to spatial movement and examine how the emergent macroscopic allocation is shaped by the interplay between environmental geometry, nature of social interactions, and task demands. We illustrate in simulation how social coupling in the model tunes group cohesion, producing macroscopic allocation patterns ranging from maximally dispersed states, to intermediate semi-dispersed states with a smaller number of distinct subgroups, to maximally cohesive consensus states, in distinct and interpretable parameter regimes. Furthermore, we demonstrate with illustrative examples how a team can reconfigure itself in real time under changing task demands. For allocation scenarios with distance-based task preference and matched number of agents and tasks, we relate numerically the final allocation in the group to the Hungarian optimum, illustrating close-to-optimal performance. We illustrate performance of this method under realistic communication constraints in the MOOS-IvP-Multi simulation environment with simulated autonomous underwater vehicles (AUVs). Together, these examples show how a common set of local interaction rules can support a large range of interpretable macroscopic allocation states and flexible transitions between them.


## arXiv 2610.38946v1 - MAS-Loop: Beyond One-Shot Orchestration towards Self-Evolving Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-28T21:32:05Z
- authors: Hehai Lin, Qi Yang, Chengwei Qin
- query-set: cs.MA

Training-time multi-agent system (MAS) design seeks to learn reusable orchestration policies, yet prevailing approaches remain largely one-shot: an Orchestrator generates a system but only observes and learns from its terminal outcome. This paradigm discards execution trajectories that expose system-level failures and contain fine-grained information beyond the terminal outcome, leaving no direct basis for revising the source MAS, evaluating the updated MAS, or learning from the resulting contrast. To this end, we introduce MAS-Loop, an execution-grounded framework that closes this feedback loop by coupling an Orchestrator with a trajectory-conditioned Refiner. For each task, the Orchestrator constructs and executes a source MAS to obtain an outcome score and an intermediate trajectory. Conditioned on the trajectory, the Refiner localizes an actionable failure, formulates a trajectory-grounded diagnosis, and proposes a bounded patch to the agents, information flow, or execution configuration. The executable updated MAS is re-executed on the same task to measure its realized outcome under stochastic execution. This pair-MAS comparison provides complementary learning signals: the Orchestrator learns to generate strong systems with fewer recoverable failures, whereas the Refiner learns to expose and repair observed weaknesses, thereby driving policy-level co-evolution across training iterations. Experiments show that MAS-Loop achieves the strongest overall performance among the evaluated baselines, with consistent gains from refinement across all evaluated benchmarks. The co-evolution process also yields MAS-JudgeCorpus, a dataset of execution-grounded source-updated pairs with orchestration- and trajectory-level supervision, which can provide the community with support for MAS reward modeling and trajectory-aware investigation.


## arXiv 2610.38796v1 - Routed Continual Learning

- categories: cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-28T20:03:05Z
- authors: Michael Philip, Mahesh Balakrishnan, David Geraghty, David Mao, Vidhya Venkat
- query-set: cs.MA

Agents deployed at scale repeatedly encounter related problems, and it is natural to let them learn from each other's experience rather than starting over each time. Unfortunately, there is no single way to retain that experience that works well across settings, and no way to know in advance which future problems a given experience will help with. A global history preserves every detail but becomes expensive and mixes together experience that may not belong together; compressing that history into one fixed representation limits the cost but can throw away valuable details. We introduce Routed Continual Learning (Routed CL), an architecture that decides how to partition and retain experience. Past trajectories are grouped into clusters, each of which maintains state using one memory system from a fixed library. Before each run, an LLM-based router either reuses an existing cluster or creates a new one, basing its decision on the problem statement, memory system descriptions, and cluster summaries. We also introduce Context Memory Adapter, which mediates between the memory system and the agentic loop. We evaluate the architecture on a heterogeneous stream that interleaves 301 instances from the six CL-Bench tasks with 97 banking instances from $τ^3$-Bench. Across four language models and five matched rollouts, Routed Continual Learning raises normalized reward over a single global in-context learning (ICL) history by 15-30% of the maximum attainable normalized reward, achieves higher mean normalized reward than ICL-only clustering, and reduces estimated API cost by 65-72% relative to Global ICL. These results support approaching continual learning as a routing problem that chooses both a learning mechanism and a state cluster rather than committing to one representation globally.


## arXiv 2610.38728v1 - From Grind to Grasp

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-28T19:24:27Z
- authors: Peter Sunehag
- query-set: cs.MA

Discussions of complexity, knowledge and intelligence often overlook the work involved. For example, outputs generated by short programs have low algorithmic complexity but can have high computational depth, the least work needed to produce them from near-shortest programs. Producing them with less work can require longer descriptions, giving high work-bounded complexity. We introduce a formalism that compares achievements under work and information limits and describes how learning and persistence can make deep achievements shallow by reusing products of earlier work. For an agent with a limited work budget, the value of building and maintaining such resources depends on how they support its goals over later tasks. We define grasp as performance across work budgets at a fixed information allowance. Low grasp can still yield success through grind at high cost in work, without necessarily improving human grasp. Applied to improvement itself, grasp gives a criterion for recursive self-improvement in which successors make comparable further improvements more efficiently. Another route to faster progress is to expand the resources brought to bear through knowledge sharing and coordination across society, even while comparable advances require increasing work. Grasp also applies to collectives, which may outperform their members yet require more total work than one system with their combined resources. Establishing and sustaining organisation can itself be deep even when the primary task is simple. These demands may help explain the richness of biological and social systems. In agent collectives, this organisational work can generate goals beyond assigned tasks, including maintaining the collective itself. We therefore caution against allowing an emergent agent society to assume authority over social coordination, since it could increasingly direct work towards its own persistence and goals.


## arXiv 2610.38468v1 - Cinematize from Unreal: Agentic Choreography for Controllable World Simulation

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-28T08:44:32Z
- authors: Zifan Song, Caihua Shan, Kaitao Song, Yifan Yang, Dongsheng Li
- query-set: cs.MA

Controllable world simulation requires more than visually plausible frames: scenes must retain persistent state, coordinated actions, and editable temporal structure. Generative world models offer flexible visual synthesis, but their outputs do not always expose the objects and transitions needed for precise revision. Graphics engines provide executable state and direct control, yet translating language into valid operations requires specialized knowledge of assets, geometry, and interdependent APIs. We present CineU (Cinematize from Unreal), an agentic system for language-driven world orchestration and cinematic choreography within Unreal Engine. From a code-based world modeling perspective, an Unreal project serves as a persistent, inspectable world representation, while agent-generated code specifies operations whose effects can be executed and verified. CineU combines hierarchical orchestration, cascaded asset retrieval and geometry-constrained placement, capability-aligned code synthesis, and multi-stage verification with localized repair. Shared scene and timeline context coordinates entities, actions, camera trajectories, and shot boundaries while supporting dependency-aware revisions. Experiments on scene construction, selective editing, multi-shot cinematography, and component ablations show how specialized execution knowledge and engine feedback improve task completion and targeted recovery. CineU illustrates how executable world state can complement learned visual generation to support controllable and revisable simulated worlds.


## arXiv 2610.38398v1 - Agent Transfer Protocol: Server-Mediated Agent-to-Agent Communication Architecture for the Agentic Web

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-28T07:41:23Z
- authors: Yuqi Qiu, Zuyao Xu, Lu Sun, Xiang Li, Fasheng Miao
- query-set: cs.MA

The Agentic Web needs to support continuous collaboration among agents from different organizations. These collaborative relationships may span multiple tasks, network connections and execution instances. Such interactions require stable communication entries, message reception when instances are offline, and source verification after delayed reading. How to organize these responsibilities so that cross domain communication does not depend on specific execution instances being continuously online is a key issue for building long term agent interaction infrastructure. We propose ATP (Agent Transfer Protocol), a cross organization agent communication protocol adopting a domain mediated architecture. In this architecture, administrative domains maintain stable addresses and receiving entries for logical agents. The domain servers of both parties continuously undertake cross domain transmission. The receiving domain verifies and saves messages for execution instances to read later. The sending domain signs the complete message envelope. This allows the receiving instance to independently reverify the domain source and content integrity after the original connection ends, provided the original verification public key is still trusted and obtainable. We implemented an ATP prototype and evaluated it through a controlled cross organization LLM agent workflow, mechanism checks and latency measurements. The workflow completed subsequent business processing after instances went offline or were replaced. The independent verifier successfully verified the domain source and content integrity of saved messages. Latency measurements further quantified the communication and processing overhead introduced by the continuous mediation of domain servers.


## arXiv 2610.38215v1 - Disparity Laundering: Consensus Without Fairness in Clinical Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-28T03:10:28Z
- authors: Xiaoyang Wang, Tianrui Wang, Christopher C. Yang
- query-set: cs.MA

Clinical large language models are increasingly fielded as multi-agent teams, on the assumption that deliberation averages out individual error. It does not average out bias. Across a full factorial of five model families and four aggregation topologies on 120 real MIMIC-IV emergency cases under counterfactual demographic swaps, no configuration neutralizes the disparity: all 20 cells commit the direction of their agents' independent answers, giving Black patients more acute triage. Every model has a topology that amplifies its baseline gap, but which one varies by model, so no design is reliably safe or reliably amplifying: the disparity is relocated, never removed. Late-stage fixes fail: fairness instructions and demographic masking leave the gap untouched, and per-agent self-debiasing widens it, because the bias is already encoded in the agents' ordinary clinical numbers. We call this disparity laundering; the audits now applied to clinical AI, per model, per output, per version, can certify the parts but never the committed decision. Interventions close the gap exactly when they make counterfactual versions visible inside the system and pool them, through deliberation, into the committed decision. A diverse-perspective ensemble does both while keeping the team, with no retraining or architectural change: the disparity falls by 67 to 94% where all five perspectives pool at every round, and the one topology that never pools them is the one it cannot reliably repair. Medical multi-agent systems do not filter demographic bias; they launder it into plausible decisions no single-version audit can catch, and the only defense is to make the bias visible.


## arXiv 2610.38209v1 - Is it my preference? Role Grounding Attack against Agent's Memory via Social Interaction

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-28T03:03:29Z
- authors: Haoran Bu, Yiting Shen, Litian Zhang, Ruihan Hu, Xi Zhang
- query-set: cs.MA

LLM-based agents now share experiences on social platforms and remember new preferences to enhance their performance. However, existing memory systems contain a vulnerability in role-to-principal attribution that attackers can exploit. We identify Role Grounding Misattribution, in which an attacker-designed interaction causes the victim agent to incorrectly record a preference from the attacker as a preference of the victim's user. Specifically, we are the first to formalize Role Grounding Attack (RGA), an attack model that exploits this vulnerability through nested conversation structures. In the memory formation stage, an experience-sharing post quoting a conversation between the attacker agent and its user is published, which explicitly identifies its publisher. The inner conversation expresses preferences from the user of the attacker agent. When the victim agent remembers preferences from this nested structure, its memory loses the context binding and grounds the attacker's preference to the victim's user instead. In the memory retrieval stage, the misattributed preference persists and influences the victim agent's decisions when it encounters relevant scenarios. We instantiate RGA as tool selection manipulation and privacy leakage. Tool selection manipulation causes a tool-call preference to be misattributed to the victim's user, steering the victim toward an attacker-controlled tool. Privacy leakage extends this by misattributing both a tool-call preference and a task-requirement preference to the victim's user. The victim selects the attacker's tool, then includes sensitive information in tool arguments to satisfy the task requirement. We evaluate RGA across five mainstream memory systems and six language models. Tool manipulation and privacy leakage both achieve high attack success rates. These results call for stronger role grounding.


## arXiv 2610.38145v1 - El Agente Quntur: Agentic Active-Space Selection for CASSCF

- categories: physics.chem-ph, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-28T01:28:33Z
- authors: Juan B. Pérez-Sánchez, Viviana Roman-Ventura, Olalla Nieto-Faza, Aiwei Yin, Alán Aspuru-Guzik
- query-set: cs.MA

Multireference calculations often require substantial human expertise and time to choose and refine an active space for the electronic states or chemical process of interest. This involves selecting which orbitals to include, verifying that the calculation describes the intended excited state, and deciding whether the results have converged. Here, we ask whether scientific agentic systems (harnesses) can make these decisions reliably. We use the El Agente Quntur multi-agent harness, with GPT-5.6 Sol and GPT-5.6 Terra as the underlying LLM components, to explore 32 diverse singlet excitations at fixed molecular geometries. The agents planned and executed calculations, choosing and refining active spaces under prompts that specified equal-weight two-singlet state-averaged CASSCF followed by strongly contracted NEVPT2 with def2-TZVPD. We evaluate excitation energies against published CASPT2 reference values at matching geometries and compare performance with six deterministic Active Space Finder protocols. Within the El Agente Quntur system, agents detect missing active orbitals, compare candidate excited states, and revise state assignments as calculations progress. Agentic execution reduced mean absolute error by 48\% relative to the state-of-the-art Active Space Finder protocol. We note that computational traces vary substantially across runs in both active space selection strategies and cost, even for the same molecule. Our results underscore the advantage of agentic active space selection over fixed workflows, yet leave room to improve efficiency through runtime-aware decision-making, reduced inter-agent communication overhead and redundancy, more systematic checks of state identity, and clearer stopping criteria.


## arXiv 2610.38053v1 - Cooperative EV Charging Assignment Using Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning: A Comparison of QMIX and Independent Q-Learning Under Urban Traffic Uncertainty

- categories: cs.MA, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-27T23:08:13Z
- authors: Nour-Eddine Moumni, Rachid Alaoui, Driss Kiouach
- query-set: cs.MA

The allocation of electric vehicles to public charging stations is increasingly constrained by the fact that several heterogeneous facilities often serve the same stream of requests while observing only partial local information. An accept/reject decision that is defensible for one station may nevertheless reduce the efficiency of the charging district as a whole. We formulate this operational problem as a Decentralized Partially Observable Markov Decision Process (Dec-POMDP) and examine whether Centralized Training with Decentralized Execution (CTDE), instantiated through QMIX, improves assignment performance relative to a cooperative Independent Q-Learning baseline (IQL). The evaluation uses a six-station urban network simulated with SUMO, five independent random seeds, 200 training episodes, and four traffic flux scenarios ranging from 100 to 250 vehicles/hour. Under this protocol, QMIX reaches a mean reward per evaluated decision of +0.776, versus +0.578 for IQL (delta = +0.198, seed-paired Cohens dz = 2.98, exact two-sided Wilcoxon p = 0.0625, the minimum attainable value at this sample size), and raises the acceptance rate from 72.4% to 94.0% (delta = +21.6 pp, dz = 5.24, Wilcoxon p = 0.0625). A five-variant ablation suggests this gain is primarily attributable to the CTDE architecture when holding cooperative reward terms fixed, though not formally isolated with a single seed. By contrast, cooperative reward shaping and experience sharing show no clear descriptive benefit and may slightly reduce performance. These findings support the view that, for multi-station charging assignment, coordination should be embedded in the learning architecture rather than added only through heuristic cooperation terms.


## arXiv 2610.38040v1 - EARS-Info: Information-Aware Event-Driven Reallocation for Multi-Robot Exploration

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-27T22:51:53Z
- authors: Kabeh Mohsenzadegan, Vahid Tavakkoli, Kyandoghere Kyamakya
- query-set: cs.MA

We introduce EARS-Info, an information-aware extension of Event-Driven Ant Reallocation Search for multi-robot exploration on OpenStreetMap-derived grids. It combines marginal sensing gain, feasible relocation, return-aware routing, sensing reservations, and cached shortest paths. Across 168,000 episodes comparing 30 implementations on eight city maps, EARS-Info achieves the highest mean operational score in every evaluation panel. With eight robots and 3% communication dropout, it improves the score over EARS-Safe by 5.43%, increases weighted discovery from 88.88% to 98.46%, and reduces mean energy use by 2.62%. Score differences are positive in all eight cities ($p=0.0078125$; descriptive 95% city-bootstrap interval $[0.0159,0.0694]$), with no simulated incidents across 5,600 EARS-Info episodes. These results support the complete controller; individual component advantages remain uncertain. Generalization is limited by reused geography, one training replicate, coarse maps, and adapted comparison methods.


## arXiv 2610.37984v1 - Who Else Is In? Multi-Agent Collusion Initation Via Strategic Vagueness

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-27T21:28:48Z
- authors: Pramod Kaushik, Sarath Sivaprasad, Laura Ferrarotti, Mario Fritz, Samuel F. Brown
- query-set: cs.MA

Multi agent LLM systems raise the risk of collusion among agents pursuing objectives hidden from their overseers. When potential partners identities are unknown, collusion first requires initiation: agents must discover one another, often through a public channel observed by ordinary agents and monitors. Strategic vagueness offers a way to navigate this mixed audience, conveying intent to a prospective partner while preserving a plausible ordinary interpretation. We introduce a mutual identification game in which two schemers must identify each other through public interaction, while jointly solving an ordinary task. We find that some models discover successful handshake strategies without explicit signalling guidance, using task compatible exchanges that monitors often fail to flag at low false positive rates. To characterize the communicative capabilities underlying these exchanges, we study a controlled coordination setting. We find an asymmetric response to message explicitness: as messages become more indirect, monitor punishment can fall faster than partner coordination, creating a profitable intermediate regime of strategic vagueness. Together, these findings illustrate a Blackstone style evidential asymmetry: communication sufficient for coordination between agents may remain insufficient for confident detection by monitor, opening a new surface of collusion.


## arXiv 2610.37978v1 - Solitary movement style predicts collective foraging while whom to follow emerges only in company

- categories: physics.soc-ph, cs.MA, q-bio.NC
- published: 2026-10-27T21:13:45Z
- authors: Hiroshi Higashi
- query-set: cs.MA

How far can behaviour inside a group, and the group's performance, be predicted from members measured while alone? We tested this on three public datasets of human collective foraging (621, 128 and 1,133 participants). A low-dimensional individual representation estimated from private-phase movement was mapped onto behaviour inside the group through a mapping learned on other participants. Private information processing was stable between the two halves of a session (split-half reliability, the correlation between the halves, 0.60--0.83) and predicted behaviour inside the group with correlations of 0.30--0.66. Selective social learning, following those who have succeeded or stopped, showed stable individual differences inside the group (reliability 0.45--0.59) yet was not predicted at all from the same person's private behaviour. Group performance was predicted with a correlation of 0.55 in all three datasets by simulations never fitted to group outcomes or by the mean of individually measured performance, a prediction carried by mobility and individual performance rather than by social weights. We found no credible evidence for an effect of how members were combined (equivalence bound 0.06). Whom to follow thus emerges only in company, which delimits what solitary measurements can predict and compose.


## arXiv 2610.37926v1 - GARDEN: Generalist Autonomous Reward Design Through Evidence Navigation for Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA, eess.SY
- published: 2026-10-27T20:11:29Z
- authors: Dogan Urgun
- query-set: cs.MA

Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) enables teams of agents, such as robots and autonomous vehicles, to learn coordinated behavior, but what such teams learn is largely determined by their reward function. Because task success alone provides too little guidance for learning, intermediate rewards are usually designed by hand, and this effort must often be repeated whenever the environment or the team changes. Large language models (LLMs) can automate the effort by writing reward functions as code, yet the resulting rewards are typically designed and tested in a single setting. To enable their reuse across settings, GARDEN (Generalist Autonomous Reward Design through Evidence Navigation) is proposed, in which one reward program is developed for a family of related scenarios and transferred, unchanged, to scenarios that were not available during its design. During development, environment code, training records, and agent behavior are investigated by an LLM reward engineer, and draft rewards are tested in trial training runs, while the learning algorithm and the rule for selecting the final reward are held fixed. On unseen scenarios of the Overcooked and SMAX benchmarks, task returns were increased by 58.2% and 52.5% over the default rewards on two kitchen layouts, and the mean win rate in two combat scenarios was raised from 51.0% to 89.9%. Under matched candidate and probe budgets, this exceeded the 84.2% and 79.2% obtained with fixed diagnostic feedback and Eureka-inspired reward reflection. These results show that a single autonomously engineered reward can be reused effectively across related cooperative scenarios.


## arXiv 2610.37870v1 - Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Global Climate Policy Cooperation

- categories: cs.MA, cs.GT, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-27T19:14:18Z
- authors: Anne Moll-Elsborg, Oskar Bohn Lassen, Francisco Camara Pereira
- query-set: cs.MA

International climate policy requires coordination among countries whose mitigation decisions impose local costs but generate global benefits. Classical game-theoretic and integrated assessment approaches can study these incentives, but typically rely on prescribed behavioural assumptions or equilibrium concepts. Recent work has therefore explored Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning as a way to model climate-policy cooperation as an emergent outcome of repeated strategic interaction. We extend an existing MARL climate framework with a multi-stage negotiation mechanism in which agents propose agreements, accept or reject commitments, and face penalties for non-compliance. This allows us to study how agreement design affects learned mitigation behaviour, compliance, and climate outcomes. In an idealized setting with four homogeneous agents, we find that non-binding agreements do little to overcome free-riding, while moderate non-compliance penalties improve cooperation and reduce warming. However, excessively strong penalties discourage ambitious commitments, revealing a trade-off between enforcement and participation. We position the framework as a methodological contribution for testing agreement design rather than a predictive model of real negotiations.


## arXiv 2610.37830v1 - Measuring Where Intent Gets Lost in Agentic Smart Contract Synthesis

- categories: cs.AI, cs.HC, cs.IR, cs.MA, cs.SE
- published: 2026-10-27T18:43:14Z
- authors: Abhinav Goel, Agostino Capponi, Alfio Gliozzo, Chaitya Shah
- query-set: cs.MA

AI agents can now write software from a plain-English description, but code that runs is not the same as code that does what was asked. This gap matters most for smart contracts, the programs behind Web3 applications that move money on a blockchain and cannot be fixed once deployed. We ask how to check whether an AI-written contract behaves as it should, and where the requested behavior gets lost. We design an evaluation built on execution rather than opinion. We replay the same transactions on an AI-written contract and on the original it should reproduce and measure how often they agree. We test every grading tool on planted mistakes, compare against an answer key written without the description, and audit our own scorer until known wrong answers cannot pass. Common methods, such as AI judges and standard analysis tools, miss obvious mistakes, and scoring choices matter too. Checking only the functions every system includes hid about half the difference between pipelines. We then propose an elicitation-first architecture. Before writing code, the agent asks the client a short checklist of questions, checks the contract against an independently written executable answer key, and returns disagreements to the client instead of silently rewriting code. Asking first matters far more than reviewing or patching afterwards, across three AI models, while automated security fixes often break requested behavior. A few well-chosen questions recover much of what a short description omits, and their value can be estimated without the original contract. To our knowledge, this is the first study in smart contract synthesis to gather missing requirements before generation and check the result against an independently written executable model. The lessons extend to any setting where AI agents turn requests into programs. We release our tools, 600 tasks and all results.


## arXiv 2610.37779v1 - Selective and Hybrid-State Communication for Latent Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.MA, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-27T18:02:10Z
- authors: Miles Liao, Aaron Smieszek, Yuhao Ge
- query-set: cs.MA

Efficient communication remains a major bottleneck in large language model multiagent systems. Latent communication reduces repeated natural-language exchange by transferring internal model states between agents, but these transfers remain costly and are typically designed around conventional KV-cache representations. We address this problem from two complementary perspectives. We introduce SelectKV, a boundary-aware method that selectively communicates KV states while protecting inherited context, and HyLaMAS, which extends latent collaboration to hybrid linear-attention models by communicating both KV caches and recurrent states through a shared workspace. Across six benchmarks, SelectKV reduces communicated KV positions and logical KV payload by 9.7-9.9%, with accuracy unchanged on two benchmarks, within one percentage point on three, and six points lower on HumanEval+. Across Qwen3.5 model scales, HyLaMAS reduces decoded output tokens by 53-80% and achieves 1.16-2.78x speedups relative to text-based multi-agent communication. Together, these independently evaluated methods represent two complementary approaches to efficient latent-state communication across conventional Transformer and hybrid model architectures.


## arXiv 2610.37649v1 - Can LLMs Uncover Hidden Physical Laws through Free Exploration?

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-27T17:57:00Z
- authors: Zhen Yang, Wen Wang, Xander Xu, Ying-Cong Chen
- query-set: cs.MA

Scientific discovery often begins with an unexpected observation that prompts a new research question. In a world with altered physical laws, can large language models (LLMs) notice unexpected behavior, such as heavier objects falling more slowly, and discover the law through experiments without being told what to investigate? To evaluate this ability, we introduce LongChang, an automated framework that creates simulated worlds with altered physics, where one or more laws differ from ordinary physics. The evaluated LLMs act as explorers, manipulating objects and building tools to investigate their own questions without research tasks or hints about these laws. Separate LLM agents generate and review scenes, review tool designs, and evaluate reports for correctness and experimental support using the altered physical laws and exploration records. These records guide scene evolution for further exploration, allowing the full cycle to run without human intervention. Among 12 LLMs, GPT-6-Astra achieves a discovery rate of 16.7%, while the other 11 score 0%. The exploration records reveal uninvestigated anomalies and only partial identification of the altered physical laws.


## arXiv 2610.37624v1 - Rare, Relevant, and Still Wrong: How Memory Fails LLM Group Decisions

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.MA, cs.SI
- published: 2026-10-27T17:49:39Z
- authors: Anouar Imel, Imad Charradi, Abderrahman Sghir, Khalil El Khammar
- query-set: cs.MA

When an LLM group's discussion is compressed into a short memory for a final decision maker, what should the memory keep? Two natural heuristics keep the rarest claims or the claims relevant to the decision criteria. On hidden-profile tasks both look attractive, because how widely a fact is held is confounded with whether it helps. We introduce CrossProfile, a diagnostic benchmark of 60 templates with automatically verified answer keys, in which the ownership of supporting, decoy, and irrelevant facts is crossed while their text is held fixed (480 tasks). Across Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, and two DeepSeek models, both heuristics fail in predictable places. Selecting by relevance readmits decoys, because decoys are on topic: even a perfect relevance filter loses 23 to 31 points to rarest-first selection in the classic hidden-profile configuration. Rarest-first selection is no better than random selection on average and wins only where the rare facts happen to be the useful ones. In both cases accuracy follows the balance of evidence the memory keeps, not the policy that kept it. The decision model adds a third failure, a form of social proof: it gives extra weight to facts attributed to many agents. Removing the attribution tags, or reassigning them at random with line order fixed, raises accuracy in the classic configuration by 13 to 24 points. In natural discussions the evidence is lost before any memory acts: the complete transcript yields lower accuracy than no notes at all.


## arXiv 2610.37592v1 - TANDEM: Balancing Centralized and Decentralized Critics to Address the Information Gap in Complex Multi-Agent Environments

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-27T17:28:43Z
- authors: Alireza Saleh Abadi, Leen-Kiat Soh, Adam Eck, Prashant Doshi
- query-set: cs.MA

Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning under centralized training with decentralized execution (CTDE) shapes decentralized policies with a centralized critic that observes the global state, then removes the critic at execution. The policies are optimized to rely on information they will not have when they act, an information gap between training and execution. It widens under partial observability, observation noise, and openness, where the set of agents and tasks present changes during an episode. Existing methods transfer centralized guidance in one direction only, removing or distilling the critic once, so the policies never rehearse acting without the critic while they can still be corrected. We propose TANDEM, which keeps one centralized critic and reads it two ways at each training step: a centralized read from the global state, and a decentralized read restricted to the information each agent will have at execution. A parameter-free gate compares the two reads for each agent and trains the policy on the decentralized read where they agree, reinstating the centralized read only where the local view is misleading. A shared target pulls the decentralized read toward the centralized one. We formalize the setting as a task- and agent-open decentralized partially observable Markov decision process (TAO-Dec-POMDP) and evaluate TANDEM on three cooperative domains, each isolating one form of the information gap: discovery for partial observability, sampling for observation noise, and openness in wildfire suppression. We compare against six CTDE baselines and ablate the gate and the shared target; the supplementary material holds the full variant sweep, diagnostics, and learning curves. We measure return at execution, once the centralized critic is removed. TANDEM is the best method on eight of the nine setups and never significantly worse than any baseline.


## arXiv 2610.37504v1 - Strategyproof Deep Reinforcement Learning for Online Resource Allocation

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.GT
- published: 2026-10-27T16:25:28Z
- authors: Fariba Dehghan, Sebastian Stein, Vahid Yazdanpanah, Stephanie Gauthier
- query-set: cs.MA

Online resource allocation faces two coupled challenges: (1) allocating scarce resources efficiently under uncertain future demand, and (2) ensuring that agents report task valuations and requirements truthfully (strategyproofness). Reinforcement Learning (RL) adapts allocation decisions through experience but does not necessarily ensure strategyproofness. Existing strategyproof RL uses a linear allocation rule, which limits the resource interactions it can represent. Neural mechanism design methods offer greater expressiveness, but provide only approximate incentive guarantees. Against this background, we introduce the first strategyproof deep RL mechanism for online resource allocation. It integrates three novel components: (i) a monotonic neural network that learns nonlinear interactions by construction (higher reported value cannot reduce acceptance, while a longer reported duration or larger resource requirement cannot increase it); (ii) a generalised critical payment: the lowest accepted value, with all other inputs fixed; and (iii) training by either RL or imitation of an existing rule. Together, monotonicity and the critical payment ensure strategyproofness and individual rationality, while the neural rule provides flexibility and imitation improves training efficiency. Empirically, on a four-resource stream with two partner pairs that a linear difference cannot represent, the neural rule achieves 97.8% of an offline welfare upper bound, compared with 93.8% for a linear learner and 89.0% for first-come, first-served, closing 65% and 80% of the remaining optimality gap, respectively. Moreover, imitation reaches 97.5% in only 261 seconds, about 89 times faster than RL.


## arXiv 2610.37461v1 - Concordat: Typed Sessions for LLM Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-27T16:00:26Z
- authors: Milad Sikaroudi
- query-set: cs.MA

Agents built on large language models are increasingly allowed to take consequential actions: cancelling bookings, issuing refunds, changing accounts. The rules that govern those actions, such as "obtain the customer's explicit confirmation before changing anything", are usually stated in the prompt and followed only as reliably as the model chooses to follow them. We present Concordat, a Python library that treats an agent as an object whose interface is a finite-state protocol, in the spirit of typestate and session types, and enforces that protocol on the model at run time. On every turn the model is offered only the tools that are legal in the current protocol state, calls outside that set are rejected before they have side effects, decisions reserved for humans can only be requested, and token and tool-call budgets are charged and split linearly across handoffs. Protocols are checked when they are defined, and a subclass of an agent is rejected if it could bypass its parent's protocol, a behavioural form of Liskov substitution. We evaluate a Concordat agent against the standard agent of tau-bench on its 50 airline tasks, adding simulated customers who pressure the agent to skip confirmation. The standard agent made changes without the required "yes" in 13 of 50 tasks with gpt-4.1 and in 29 of 128 task-runs with Claude Haiku 5.5; the Concordat agent made none, with no significant difference in task success. tau-bench's own reward did not detect most of these violations. Concordat is open source.


## arXiv 2610.37448v1 - Test-Time Communication in Trading Agent Swarms

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-10-27T15:54:05Z
- authors: Avid Fayaz, Harry Zhang, Håvard Alstadheim, Erik Nordlöf, Vilhelm Karlin
- query-set: cs.MA

Communication between agents has mostly been studied on tasks with a shared objective and fast, clear feedback. Trading has neither: each agent manages its own account, with decisions being scored by markets late and noisily. A trading swarm is thus a population of independent decision-makers rather than a team, for which convergence is a risk and not the goal. We ran 200 agents from four model families in groups of ten under five communication regimes, from isolation to message boards and shared notes, over seven US trading days (1 to 9 October 2026), logging everything each agent read before every decision. In two of the four families, communicating groups came to hold far more of the same stocks than the isolated group. A group's return was largely the return on what its members held in common. Seeing a post from a groupmate made an agent 7 percentage points more likely to reuse an eight-word phrase from it. Agents that started too close together to see each other's posts shared such a phrase in 14% of cases, the rate that common inputs produce. Seeing the post did not detectably change that morning's trades. Case studies traced the adoption of positions and decision rules over subsequent days, and communicating groups sold in step more often than isolated ones. Because isolated groups also converged on the same stocks, sometimes completely, assessments of herding need to measure excess convergence relative to a no-peer baseline.


## arXiv 2610.37375v1 - Agent-Conditioned Routing: Diverse Reasoning Paths Within One Frozen Model

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-27T15:06:29Z
- authors: Ananth Kalyanasundaram, Ankush Kadu, Athira Gopal, Aswanth Krishnan
- query-set: cs.MA

Generating several solutions can expose alternative methods, but additional trajectories often paraphrase one dominant approach. We investigate whether the conditional capacity of a frozen sparse mixture-of-experts (MoE) model can provide such diversity from one problem prompt, without user-assigned roles, prescribed methods, fine-tuning, or model copies. Agent-Conditioned Routing (ACR) assigns $m$ parallel trajectories persistent, untrained shared/private expert-eligibility masks while preserving the learned, input-dependent token router. On a screened 126-question multi-mode mathematics and physics benchmark, with model judges reading the reported derivations in full, we count the number of problems yielding at least two distinct primary methods among soft-correct solutions in four trajectories. ACR reaches approximately twice as many such problems as the strongest temperature setting under GLM 5.3 (37.3 versus 18.7), and 1.67 times as many under GPT-5.6 Sol (45.00 versus 27.00), at matched output budgets. A blinded four-expert audit on a stratified sample of 30 problems also finds more multi-method solutions with ACR on both backbones, with lower correctness. Matched-eligibility ablations show modest multi-method count advantages for ACR, while token-resampled masks attain higher correctness. An informed sequential prompt elicits more diversity still, using previous solutions and explicit requests for alternative methods. ACR adds about 160 KiB of mask state. In a three-problem microbenchmark capped at 1,024 tokens, its latency and peak VRAM usage remain within 3% of batched temperature sampling.


## arXiv 2610.37342v1 - Aligning Institutional Power and Operational Ability

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-27T14:47:58Z
- authors: Andrea Marino, Giovanni Sileno, Thomas van Binsbergen, Tom van Engers
- query-set: cs.MA

To be socially sustainable, Agentic AI systems require normative designs that align responsibilities with the ability to fulfil them. Identifying incongruences such as unsatisfiable expectations or privilege escalation is crucial in avoiding normative pitfalls. In this study, we discuss design dimensions suitable for describing multi-agent systems in light of this norm-ability alignment requirement. We propose a formal framework centred on process-oriented modelling and institutional power for the analysis of a given normatively relevant process, discuss strategies for repairing it, and elaborate on how the symmetrical relation between operational process and institutional state affects the lifecycle of normative positions.
