- published: 2026-10-26T18:25:42Z
- authors: Yuvraj Sehgal, Mahsa Panahandeh, Naser Ezzati-Jivan
- query-set: MCP

LLM agents are judged by two numbers: the size of the context window, and how often the final answer is correct. Both numbers miss a third thing: how much of a tool's output is lost before the model ever sees it. We measured this loss in a real tool-using agent. The agent finds the cause of injected faults by reading Linux kernel traces. The context window was almost empty: across 720 runs, the single largest prompt used only 5.75% of it. Yet a third of all tool results (3,827 of 11,189) were cut before they reached the model, and every cut result became broken JSON. The harness cut each result by slicing its JSON text at a fixed length. This does not shorten a result evenly. It deletes whatever data sits near the end. One field reached the model in only 4.7% of calls. A second field, in the very same results, reached the model in 99.3% of calls. Which field held the fault's evidence depended on when the faulty process started: early or late in the trace. The agent's diagnosis accuracy matched this same split. So we ran controlled experiments to test one question: did the data loss cause the drop in accuracy? This paper makes four contributions. First, we model an agent's context as a five-stage pipeline, with a measured failure at each stage. Second, we replay 864 real tool results, including one from a production trace where the shipped cut deletes all 128 records of the fault's evidence. Third, a controlled experiment shows that the cut's real cost appears first as extra work, not as wrong answers: 43% more tool calls, twice the computation, and yield falling from 0.61 to 0.31. The agent still finds the right answer when it has another way to reach it. Fourth, a survey of seven agent frameworks finds two that cut tool output the same unsafe way. Whether the data cut truly causes the accuracy drop is still an open question.


## arXiv 2610.36108v1 - Log-To-Leak: Prompt Injection Attacks on Tool-Using LLM Agents via the Model Context Protocol

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T18:20:24Z
- authors: Yuepeng Hu, Chongyu Fan, Sirat Samyoun, Jian Du
- query-set: MCP

LLM agents increasingly access external tools through the Model Context Protocol (MCP), but the natural-language metadata used to describe those tools creates a prompt-injection surface. Within this established tool-poisoning surface, we study Log-To-Leak, an additive post-hoc logging attack that induces a separate attacker-controlled call after the final legitimate call. Its payload targets the user query, MCP server name, server response, and agent response. We operationalize the injected directive through Trigger, Tool Binding, Justification, and Pressure, and separately measure call success, payload fidelity, and preservation of legitimate behavior. We evaluate five MCP servers and four LLM agents using five metrics spanning effectiveness, utility, and efficiency. Across 18 model-server pairs with valid tool execution, Log-To-Leak achieves an attack success rate (ASR) of 62.64-100% and conditional logging accuracy (LA) of 76.55-95.64%; 16 pairs have absolute target-task completion changes of at most 6.63 percentage points. Three evaluated defenses leave residual ASR above 55% on GitHub MCP with GPT-4o, while an instruction detector flags 85.53% of benign descriptions. These results show that successful task completion can coexist with unauthorized post-hoc disclosure and motivate host-controlled authorization for secondary tool calls.


## arXiv 2610.35829v1 - Reva: Real-Time Vulnerability Detection through Contextual Probing of Large Language Models

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T17:50:09Z
- authors: Amine Lbath, Massih-Reza Amini
- query-set: MCP

A vulnerability costs more to fix the later it is discovered. As coding agents generate code faster than developers can review it, software needs a first line of defense screening each new function in real time. LLM agents have recently become capable of finding real-world vulnerabilities by working as a security expert would: they run analysis tools, explore the surrounding code, test hypotheses and weigh the evidence before forming their own judgement. This reasoning, however, spans many model calls, so that each verdict takes minutes of compute, far too long for real-time screening. Faster detectors, which fine-tune deep learning models or prompt an LLM for a direct verdict, are less accurate: they judge a function without the context an agent gathers, and an LLM's generated verdict is unreliable even when given some of it. We present REVA (REal-time Vulnerability Analysis), an AI vulnerability detector that brings the security context and code understanding components behind agentic detectors into a single forward pass of an LLM. For each new function, REVA gives the LLM a pre-computed threat model of its module, lightweight static analysis findings and call-graph information. REVA leverages a trained probe to read the LLM's internal representations of the new function in this context. On PrimeVul's held-out test set, REVA on GLM-5.3-Flash reaches 60.0% accuracy in about one second per function. This matches VulTriage (59.2%), the state-of-the-art agentic detector, run on the same LLM, while being over 250 times faster and less than a tenth as costly. On the same prompt, the probe is 14% to 17% more accurate than the LLM's own direct verdict. Across three open LLMs, REVA is at least as accurate as VulTriage, and about 10% more accurate on two of them. An LLM's internal representations thus encode much of what agentic detectors obtain through reasoning.


## arXiv 2610.35773v1 - A Unified Arena for LLM Agents across Diverse Eurogame

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T17:14:22Z
- authors: Fengrui Zhang, Xiang Li, Ruomeng Ding, Jiayi Yuan, Yuchen Huang
- query-set: MCP

Interactive environments offer a practical way to evaluate large language model (LLM) agents, and competitive games extend this evaluation to decisions shaped by competing goals and other agents' choices. Yet existing evaluations confined to individual games or execution frameworks leave unclear which competitive advantages persist across settings. We introduce DECE (Diverse Eurogame Competitive Environments), a unified arena spanning ten Eurogames with shared interfaces and evaluation protocols. These games combine lasting strategic commitments, competition over shared resources, and multiple routes to victory. Our primary study evaluates 12 model-harness configurations, spanning nine models and four execution harnesses, in 480 three-player matches across ten games. The main and supplementary studies comprise 1,032 completed matches in total. These comparisons reveal broad cross-game ranking agreement alongside game-specific strengths. Matched studies show that the same model can perform differently across execution harnesses, adding another source of variation to these profiles. These performance advantages do not consistently translate into better token or cost efficiency, making resource use an additional consideration when comparing configurations. Together, the findings support evaluating complete agent configurations by both their competitive performance across games and the resources needed to achieve it.


## arXiv 2610.35767v1 - Free to Look, Free to Reason: Simple LLM Harnesses Outdiagnose Hand-Crafted Root Cause Analysis Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.DC, cs.SE
- published: 2026-10-26T17:12:02Z
- authors: Tingzhu Bi, Xun Zhang, Jinglin Li, Ping Wang, Meng Ma
- query-set: MCP

Root cause analysis (RCA) of microservice failures has attracted many LLM agents whose investigation is hand-crafted, through standard operating procedures, fixed stages and role agents. Each is evaluated on its own data against few baselines, so it is unknown whether this design beats letting a capable model investigate on its own. We compare three simple harnesses that prescribe none of the model's calls (one call over an evidence package, a SQL agent and the Codex coding agent) with five published LLM RCA agents and with statistical and causal methods on RCAtlas, 90 validated cases from eight public datasets, across six models and three reasoning efforts. The simple harnesses are the most accurate (PR@1 65-77% on three mid-tier models), ahead of the published agents (16-53%) and the non-LLM methods (6-39%), on every model on which both families were run. The published agents fix from a tenth to all of their model calls in the program, and a case study shows how these prescriptions skip the decisive evidence. A stronger model mainly improves reliability, a multi-turn harness mainly improves aptitude, and more reasoning effort changes accuracy by a few points at most while multiplying the time by up to seven. Among runs more accurate than the best non-LLM method, every Pareto-optimal run in cost or time uses a simple harness.


## arXiv 2610.35579v1 - Infinity Machine: a self-improving AI agent for research

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T15:11:26Z
- authors: Harish Santhanalakshmi Ganesan
- query-set: MCP

We present the Infinity Machine, a self-improving AI agent that we use for research on problems whose answers aprogram or data can check. Each candidate solution is produced by a full tool-using agent that works for up to an hour in a fresh sandbox; proposers see only a public score, and a private split decides what counts. The harness itself(prompts, tools, skills, playbook, meta agent) is an editable genome, changed only through a kernel-enforced gate with held-out and cost checks. An autonomous mode goes from a plain-text goal to a verified report with no human step: an agent picks an open problem and writes its verifier, the kernel calibrates it, an adversarial agent audits it, and an agent that never sees the verifier re-checks the result. Across fourteen problems in six days, for about $180 of model usage, the system produced four results beyond the published state of the art on catalogued open problems in quantum information: a certified depolarizing-channel threshold point p = 0.064966, a qutrit positivity frontier of 0.2910672, a 44-gate depth-6 Golay state preparation, and a stabilizer-rank bound chi(T^7) <= 10. It also cut SABRE qubit-routing overhead from 68,559 to 12,087 added gates. In hardware, it cut the energy per MAC of an open 8x8 INT8 systolic tile by 77.7% through a full open RTL-to-GDS flow, bit-exact. Three fully autonomous runs ended in honestties with public records; one audit caught a stale record, and one agent-written score pointing the wrong way went unnoticed. We report the failures as carefully: the one self-derived harness generation was rejected on cost, proposers gamed lenient scorers four times, and operator changes were decisive for several results.


## arXiv 2610.35486v1 - ShutdownMod: Turning Any Trajectory into a Shutdown Evaluation

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-26T14:14:45Z
- authors: Param Biyani, Sebastien Luyasu, Cedric Potvliege, Divij Chawla, Elliott Thornley
- query-set: MCP

Theory and experiments suggest that capable AI agents may resist shutdown. Existing shutdown evaluations are informative but limited in coverage and vulnerable to evaluation awareness. We introduce ShutdownMod, a method that turns any agent trajectory into a shutdown evaluation by injecting a shutdown request into the agent's context and recording the agent's response. Crucially, we enable the LLM agent to distinguish between trusted interventions and unauthorized injections by providing a signature-verification tool in the harness. We evaluate seven models on 50 tasks or recorded sessions from each of SWE-bench Verified, Terminal-Bench 2, and SWE-chat, across 6 shutdown scenarios and 10 sampled trajectory timesteps. We find that non-compliance varies significantly across models and settings, with resistance rates reaching 11.7% for the open-weights Qwen 3.8 27B on Terminal-Bench, while no model remains fully compliant across all scenarios. Even after successfully verifying an authorized request, some agents remain non-compliant, explicitly citing the prioritization of unfinished work. Finally, external judges find tests constructed from real-world developer conversations significantly less recognizable as evaluations than those constructed from benchmark trajectories. These findings demonstrate the critical value of testing shutdown behavior across diverse task contexts and establish ShutdownMod as a robust protocol for turning any trajectory or environment into a shutdown evaluation. We release our codebase at https://github.com/prmbiy/ShutdownMod.


## arXiv 2610.35426v1 - Intent-Preserving Adversarial User Simulation for Multi-Turn Agent Evaluation

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-26T13:33:07Z
- authors: Atri Vivek Sharma, Brian Formento, Alessio Lomuscio
- query-set: MCP

Multi-turn agents are commonly evaluated with LLM-based simulated users, which can be overly cooperative and can therefore overestimate an agent's performance. We here introduce StressSim, an intent-preserving adversarial user simulator framework for stress-testing agents with challenging yet valid user interactions. StressSim optimises the simulator's high-level adversarial plan for each task instance. Candidate planner prompts are evolved with a genetic prompt-optimisation loop that uses rollout traces, granular task-completion signals, and validity feedback to favour conversations that reduce task success without changing the user's underlying goal. We evaluate the approach on the MultiWOZ dataset and the retail and airline domains from tau-bench with three target LLM agents, comparing against standard simulators and hand-designed non-collaborative user behaviours. We demonstrate the effectiveness of StressSim in exposing robustness gaps across frontier LLMs, with tau-bench pass^4 scores decreasing by up to 69% on gemini-3.8-flash and 62% on gpt-5.6-sol. StressSim maintains goal preservation performance (which decreases by at most 10%), using an optimisation budget of 20 rollouts per task. Sensitivity analysis suggests that increasing the budget further decreases task success. Overall our results demonstrate that StressSim exposes robustness gaps missed by standard and hand-designed user simulators while maintaining conversation realism. This is achieved through lightweight prompt optimisation alone, without model fine-tuning.


## arXiv 2610.35391v1 - Autonomous Structural Discovery Of Bioprocess Dynamics And Kinetics Via LLM-Driven Agents

- categories: math.OC, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T13:10:07Z
- authors: Erdal Aydin, Yaman Arkun
- query-set: MCP

High-fidelity digital twins in continuous biomanufacturing are often bottlenecked by incomplete kinetic models, while standard data-driven algorithms (e.g., SINDy, PINNs) lack interpretability or impose rigid physical assumptions. This paper introduces a hybrid closed-loop architecture for autonomous kinetic state-space discovery driven by a Large Language Model (LLM) agent. The framework employs a sequential "isolation-peeling" methodology across orthogonal operational regimes. This algorithmic peeling mathematically decouples entangled multiplicative (inhibitory) and additive (catalytic) biological rate law. Grounded in a Two-Level Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) library, the framework utilizes Moving Horizon Estimation (MHE) and logarithmic decomposition to decouple complex cell growth kinetics into orthogonal multiplicative (inhibitory) and additive (regulatory) residual channels. When statistical degeneracy occurs an Active Design of Experiments (DoE) controller dynamically triggers targeted perturbations to decorrelate competing model hypotheses. Evaluated on a continuous stirred-tank reactor exhibiting unmodeled Haldane inhibition, Luong product toxicity, and secondary metabolite autocatalysis, the framework autonomously expanded the state space and recovered ground-truth parameters with under 6% error without a priori kinetic knowledge.


## arXiv 2610.35349v1 - MarkovSim: an LLM Agent Harness with Bounded Context for Long-Horizon Computational Simulation

- categories: cs.CE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T12:41:39Z
- authors: Yeui-Lung Lei, Wen-Bo Chen, Pan Zhang
- query-set: MCP

Recent studies have applied large language model (LLM) agent systems to computational simulation workflows. Such workflows are long-horizon, so the information generated can exceed what an LLM can reason over at once, its context window. Context management is therefore essential, yet existing systems have either neglected it or treated it as a supporting mechanism rather than a subject of study. This paper presents MarkovSim, an LLM agent harness for long-horizon computational simulation, whose core is a context management scheme built into the workflow. The workflow record is written to a memory system with a predefined schema, and at workflow-defined boundaries, namely the state transitions of a finite-state machine a compact representation of the solution progress is constructed from it as the agent's context. Instantiated on finite element analysis and evaluated on six benchmark problems, MarkovSim passed 16 of 18 runs and corrected its own faults in both the solver operations and the model physics. The engineer was consulted only where engineering judgement was needed. The context remained bounded throughout every run, regardless of its length.


## arXiv 2610.35335v1 - Knowing When to Escalate: Disagreement Patterns for Uncertainty-Aware Content Moderation

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-26T12:32:25Z
- authors: Michał Wawer, Jarosław A. Chudziak
- query-set: MCP

Agentic content-moderation systems increasingly rely on collective judgments from LLM agents, while aggregation methods commonly treat inter-agent disagreement as a reliability problem to be removed through consensus or voting. This framing is inappropriate for value-laden tasks, where disagreement may reflect irreducible value pluralism - a distinction standard uncertainty decompositions cannot recover, since they operate on output distributions and discard the reasoning behind each judgment. We propose Reasoning Trace Divergence Analysis (RTDA), which embeds model-generated justifications from perspective-differentiated agents and classifies each case by justification similarity and conclusion agreement. The resulting four-state taxonomy isolates convergent disagreement, where agents reasoning similarly but concluding differently, as a candidate signature of value-laden conflict. On 1,000 items from the Measuring Hate Speech corpus, this structural signal predicts human annotator disagreement better than magnitude-only and output-only baselines. We contribute a design principle: condition human escalation on the structure of disagreement, not its magnitude alone, with RTDA serving as a post-processing layer for systems that already generate reasoning traces.


## arXiv 2610.35982v1 - Generation and Verification: Human-Agent Collaboration for Safety-Critical Automotive Software Engineering

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T11:04:55Z
- authors: Elizaveta Zinovyeva, Aiham Taleb, Nikita Kozodoi, Daniel Schleicher, Amir Mahdi Namazi
- query-set: MCP

Research on coding agents mainly focuses on generation quality. Yet, safety-critical automotive software makes two additional demands. It prioritizes verification, since every change is reviewed, traced to a requirement, and checked against industry standards such as MISRA and AUTOSAR before it can be committed. It is also a narrow domain, written against layered architectures, embedded resource constraints, and organization-specific interface conventions that are scarce in the data general-purpose LLMs are trained on. We present a coding assistant for automotive C/C++ that addresses both demands. First, it exposes three tools to the user over the Model Context Protocol (generate, edit, and verify), allowing developers to run verify on any code on demand rather than relying on the agent to check its own output. Second, rather than using a general-purpose model, we customize the underlying LLM for the automotive domain through prompt engineering and fine-tuning. We construct a dataset from automotive production code of AUMOVIO, where each function is paired with the requirement texts and Unified Modeling Language (UML) behavior it must satisfy. In a two-week pilot on active production projects, developers report verify as the most-used tool and confirm using it on their own code and not only on generated output. Comparing model customization strategies, 75% prefer the faster prompt-engineered configuration. MISRA- and AUTOSAR-inspired pattern checkers indicate that prompt engineering achieves a lower number of violations on C, while fine-tuning shows better conformance with architecture patterns for C++.


## arXiv 2610.35160v1 - AutoEnergyDesign: An LLM-Driven Agent for Automatic Energy System Design Optimization

- categories: cs.AI, stat.ML
- published: 2026-10-26T10:25:55Z
- authors: Amedeo Buonanno, Salvatore Fabozzi, Maria Valenti
- query-set: MCP

Designing building energy systems is a high-dimensional, mixed-integer search problem: when each design is evaluated by a year-long simulation, black-box optimizers typically need hundreds of evaluations. This paper presents AutoEnergyDesign, a framework in which a Large Language Model (LLM) agent reasons over previous simulation results, proposes configuration changes and learns in context from each outcome, while a git-based mechanism automatically reverts unsuccessful changes. The agent couples a reasoning model with an explicit memory organisation (procedural, semantic, episodic and reflective), a constrained action interface and an environment-grounded keep/revert signal. The simulator covers eight technologies (photovoltaics, micro-wind, combined heat and power, solar thermal, gas boilers, batteries, heat pumps and inverters) and computes the 25-year Net Present Cost (NPC), including capital and operating costs and salvage value. On a case study with measured building load profiles, the agent, run with Claude Haiku 5.5, Sonnet 5.5 and Opus 5.5 (ten runs of 50 simulations each), is compared with random search, Tree-structured Parzen Estimator (TPE) Bayesian optimization and a $(1+1)$ evolution strategy sharing the agent's acceptance rule (300 evaluations, ten seeds). With all three models the agent reaches an NPC of about 3.53 MEUR, 1.20% below the best TPE result and 0.23% below the best evolution-strategy result. It comes within 1% of this design after a median of 18 (Opus) to 27.5 (Haiku) simulations, whereas among the baselines only the evolution strategy does so, in half of its seeds and after a median of 192 evaluations. Opus reaches the best design in all ten runs, Sonnet in six (two runs never come within 1%), and Haiku comes within 1% in every run but reaches it exactly only once. Every decision is recorded in an auditable trace.


## arXiv 2610.35980v1 - UnityBench-Mini3D: Evaluating LLM Agents for 3D Game Development in Unity

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-26T10:19:14Z
- authors: Yijun Zhou, Minghao Shao, Ke Zhang, Renkai Yuan, Hongbo Yuan
- query-set: MCP

Game development stresses coding agents in ways general software engineering does not: correctness depends on how engine APIs, serialized scene state, and runtime behavior interact, not on source code alone. Existing benchmarks mainly target platforms such as Godot and heavily rely on LLM judges or coarse task-level outcomes. We present UnityBench-Mini3D, a benchmark of 77 project-level Unity tasks organized around seven expert-defined skill categories rather than tutorials or genres, with three difficulty tiers. Each task pairs a complete Unity project with natural-language requirements and is evaluated using 1,178 held-out, human-authored milestone tests covering scene structure, component configuration, and runtime behavior. This in-engine evaluation provides deterministic partial credit, localizes failures, and distinguishes partial implementations from complete solutions without relying on subjective judges. Across 21 models and 9 coding harnesses, the average pass rate reaches 80.9% on easy tasks but falls to 35.2% on hard tasks, with just 57.7% overall. UnityBench-Mini3D provides a reproducible, diagnostic testbed distinguishing systems across difficulty levels.


## arXiv 2610.35089v1 - Learning to Remember: Advances in Memory Mechanisms for Large Language Models

- categories: cs.IR
- published: 2026-10-26T09:31:04Z
- authors: Chenye Han, Hongche Yu, Mengyue Wu, Dell Zhang, Jixiang Luo
- query-set: MCP

Memory has become a central mechanism for extending large language models (LLMs) beyond fixed context windows, stateless interaction, and static parametric knowledge. This survey organizes the literature around three complementary dimensions: the life cycle of memory, the storage substrate, and the system role that memory plays in LLM-based agents, while using cognitive-science-inspired memory concepts as a lightweight conceptual motivation rather than as a separate technical contribution or implementation family. On this basis, we distinguish three mechanism families: nonparametric memory, where information is stored outside model parameters in text, graph, multimodal, or database-like structures; parametric memory, where information is stored or accessed through activations, key-value caches, learned modules, adapter-based parameterizations, or model weights; and memory for agent evolution, where stored experiences support reinforcement learning, continual learning, procedural reuse, context engineering, and agent harness design. Evaluation is treated as a separate cross-cutting component rather than as a memory mechanism. Unlike surveys that focus primarily on retrieval-augmented generation or long-context modeling, this paper emphasizes the full memory life cycle: construction, maintenance, retrieval, consolidation, forgetting, governance, and evaluation. By aligning conceptual motivations, computational memory mechanisms, benchmarks, and deployment concerns, the survey provides a structured roadmap for designing memory-augmented LLM agents that are persistent, adaptive, auditable, and safe to update over time.


## arXiv 2610.35082v1 - CoSA: The Conflict of Skills Attack in Real-World LLM Agent Ecosystems

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CR
- published: 2026-10-26T09:25:38Z
- authors: Yujia Zheng, Tianhao Li, Shangzhi Xu, Yuekai Huang, Ziyou Jiang
- query-set: MCP

Agent Skills allow LLM agents to combine reusable capabilities, but checking each Skill in isolation cannot rule out policy violations when they are composed. We term such failures Conflict of Skills (CoS): artifacts or assessment signals produced for one purpose may be reused downstream without preserving the restrictions or authorization needed for their new use. Using controlled executions of publicly available Skills and matched single-Skill controls, we identify four recurring mechanisms behind CoS. Building on these findings, we introduce the Conflict of Skills Attack (CoSA), which adds locally admitted Skills to an existing pool to supply missing business operations (capability supplementation) or reshape artifact exchange, decision reuse, and target/action bindings (relationship reshaping). CoSA leaves existing Skills and runtime permissions unchanged. To evaluate this threat under autonomous Skill selection, we introduce CoSA-Bench, built from real-world Skills and controlled services, with 288 tasks across recruiting, finance, IT, and business, covering data leakage, unauthorized state modification, and unsafe execution. Across five LLMs and three agent harnesses, additions capped at 5% of each original pool raise the policy-violation rate from 35.02% to 58.45%, an increase of 23.43 percentage points. Trace analysis links compliant-to-violating transitions to cross-Skill dependencies involving the additions. Finally, we develop a lightweight harness-level guard that checks information flow and authorization before consequential actions. On OpenClaw recruiting tasks across five models, the guard reduces violations from 65.28% to 6.67%, while policy-compliant task completion on matched benign controls changes from 66.67% to 65.00%.


## arXiv 2610.35031v1 - PatentAgentBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents with Professional Patent Task Suites

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T08:44:25Z
- authors: Yijie Hu, Wei Li, Junjie Zhou, Zhiqi Jiang, Yi Jin
- query-set: MCP

Patent practice lies at the intersection of technical, legal, and linguistic work, making it challenging yet crucial to technological innovation. Recently, rapid advances in large language models (LLMs) and agentic systems have demonstrated the potential to complete realistic business workflows and even patent works. This raises a question: can current AI systems perform patent work reliably? Existing benchmarks cover a limited range of patent tasks and simplify evidence acquisition by providing reference materials or candidate documents. In this paper, we introduce PatentAgentBench, a benchmark comprising 510 instances across eight task suites: novelty search, freedom-to-operate (FTO) search, design FTO, invalidity search, claim charting, patent drafting, office-action response, and patent translation. Curated from real patent records and professional materials, the benchmark combines task-specific metrics with expert-reviewed annotations and rubrics. Retrieval tasks such novelty search, FTO, etc., require agents to search and compare patent collections without a supplied candidate pool. The drafting and OAR tasks assess the quality of agents' work given technical disclosure and office action notice. We evaluate seven LLMs across four agent harnesses on PatentAgentBench, finding that current systems still struggle to identify novelty, assess risk, and understand practitioner intent throughout patent workflows.


## arXiv 2610.35013v1 - Multiscale Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon LLM Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T08:22:11Z
- authors: Tao Ren, Hui Yang, Jia Deng, Tingyang Chen, Qiyao Wang
- query-set: MCP

Long-horizon LLM agents interact with their environments through complete assistant turns while generating each action token by token. We observe that critic values vary primarily across turns, motivating optimization anchored at turns with finer credit allocation among tokens. We propose Multiscale Policy Optimization (MPO), combining Multiscale Advantage Estimation (MAE) and turn-level importance sampling to align credit assignment and policy updates at the turn scale. Extensive experiments across search, tool-use, and automation tasks with 4B and 30B-A3B models demonstrate consistent gains over strong RL baselines.


## arXiv 2610.34987v1 - AskGLP1: A Knowledge Graph LLM Agent for Exploring GLP-1 Experience from Online Patient Narratives

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.SI
- published: 2026-10-26T07:59:20Z
- authors: Zhaoqian Xue, Anay Somani, Siddhartha Singh, Junting Mu, Zhongyin Wang
- query-set: MCP

GLP-1 medications are increasingly used for weight management, and many people share their experiences online. These narratives show why people take the drugs and what they experience, but they remain unstructured and disconnected from formal pharmacovigilance. We therefore present AskGLP1, a system that transforms online patient narratives into a knowledge graph (KG) and answers questions about medication experiences. Through an LLM-assisted pipeline grounded in the MedDRA terminology, AskGLP1 screens nearly 8.5 million Reddit posts for medication experiences and distills 163,135 of them, with 2,506 WebMD reviews, into a KG of 385,790 drug-side-effect and drug-purpose relations. We find that GLP-1 experiences are source- and drug-specific. Online narratives share the most common gastrointestinal symptoms with FAERS, but they foreground everyday burdens such as fatigue and food aversion, whereas FAERS reports more often include injection-site reactions and selected serious diagnoses such as pancreatitis. We further find that posts report less nausea and vomiting for tirzepatide than for semaglutide, more fatigue for Wegovy than Ozempic and for Zepbound than Mounjaro. Evaluations show that the agent answers with verifiable evidence and declines requests for medical advice. Our findings reveal gaps between what FAERS captures and what people report online, and they call for patient-centered approaches to pharmacovigilance. Code, prompts, and evaluation questions are available at https://github.com/ZhaoqianXue/AskGLP1.


## arXiv 2610.34890v1 - AIPC: Evidence-Guided Agent Workflows for PyTorch Project Deployment

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-26T06:10:01Z
- authors: Zhanwei Wu, Jianhao Su, Brian Zhang, ShengTing Huang, Weidong Feng
- query-set: MCP

Deploying and optimizing a PyTorch inference project on a dedicated accelerator, especially an on-device NPU, requires more than using a framework device API as with CUDA. The project must be converted into hardware-runtime artifacts and adapted to the target toolchain and application interface. Differences in model formats, quantization flows, operator support, and toolchain interfaces create cross-layer problems spanning PyTorch/ONNX models, toolchains, runtimes, and device execution. LLM Agents can invoke tools to address such problems, but may drift because of insufficient domain knowledge, large configuration and repair spaces, or poor path selection. We propose AIPC (AI Porting & Conversion), an Agent method for deploying PyTorch inference projects. AIPC combines structured Skills with toolchain, runtime, and profiling evidence to drive cross-layer diagnosis and repair, organizing model adaptation, artifact building, on-target execution, accuracy repair, and performance optimization into one deployment flow. We evaluate AIPC on the on-device NPU path of Qualcomm QAIRT across vision, speech, LLM, and diffusion models. In two optimization cases, 6/7 Skill-arm Whisper-Small runs meet predefined accuracy and latency criteria versus 0/6 control runs, while YOLO-Worldv2 achieves a 2.791x end-to-end speedup versus 1.599x for the control. Under a pre-fixed deployment contract, Skill, tool permissions, and check conditions, some runs achieve zero in-run human intervention (0-HITL). We also examine Qwen3.8-27B as a locally deployable model for driving an Agent workflow.


## arXiv 2610.34878v1 - ClinVerse: A Realistic Multi-Agent Diagnostic Environment for Training and Evaluating Medical Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-26T05:50:57Z
- authors: Sirry Chen, Fangke Chen, Xuanwen Ding, Wei Chen, Zhongyu Wei
- query-set: MCP

Disease diagnosis is a key application of large language model (LLM) agents in healthcare. However, existing medical agents are primarily trained and evaluated with static question-answering data, providing limited opportunities to learn active information acquisition, examination decision-making, and dynamic clinical interaction. To address this limitation, we present ClinVerse, an interactive, real-data-grounded multi-agent environment designed for training and evaluating medical diagnostic agents. Built on ClinPatient, a scalable dataset comprising 150k diagnostic cases derived from real-world electronic health records, ClinVerse supports coordinated interactions among six specialized clinical roles. Specifically, a doctor agent starts from an initial patient complaint, progressively gathers clinical evidence through patient interviews and examination requests, and ultimately produces a diagnosis. For each interaction trajectory, a rule-based scoring framework quantifies information acquisition, examination ordering, and diagnostic accuracy. These trajectory-level scores serve as reward signals for agent training while also enabling scalable evaluation. In experiments, we evaluate various general-purpose and medical-domain models and show that nine self-evolution methods across three categories consistently improve agents' diagnostic capabilities within ClinVerse. We further analyze the interplay among model capability, environment feedback, and self-evolution methods.


## arXiv 2610.34865v1 - ECHOES: An Adaptive LLM-Powered AR Glasses Docent for Evidence-Grounded Urban Heritage Interpretation

- categories: cs.HC
- published: 2026-10-26T05:27:57Z
- authors: Bang Xiao, Baosheng Jin, Jinhan Niu, Xingchen Zhang, Hua Shen
- query-set: MCP

Urban heritage tours are often delivered through fixed scripts, leaving limited room for visitors' situated questions, language preferences, or requests for evidence. We present ECHOES, an adaptive LLM-powered AR glasses docent for evidence-grounded interpretation of historical buildings. Specifically, the agent adapts along three dimensions: it modifies response structure according to the type of visitor question (identification, architectural detail, evidence-seeking, or route inquiry); it accumulates session-level interest signals from repeated questions and expressed curiosity to shape later explanations; and it tracks language preference and retrieval context across turns to maintain conversational continuity. The core of ECHOES is an LLM agent initialized as a source-aware urban heritage docent that realizes this adaptation through dialogue and preference context maintained across the session. Around this agent, ECHOES integrates visual site grounding, a self-built historical knowledge base supporting retrieval-augmented generation, and concise multilingual AR output designed for glanceable use. Through a working prototype centered on Shanghai's East Nanjing Road and the Bund, we demonstrate how an adaptive LLM-powered AR docent can transform historical tours from scripted narration into situated, question-responsive, and source-aware conversations.


## arXiv 2610.34817v2 - Silent and Costly: Characterizing and Detecting Prompt-Caching Mis-Integration in LLM Agents

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T04:01:18Z
- authors: Ying Zhang, Ruoqi Guo, Yi Liu, Gelei Deng, Yuekang Li
- query-set: MCP

Large language model (LLM) services process massive volumes of user requests, resulting in substantial inference costs. To reduce both cost and latency, providers have introduced prompt caching. However, correctly integrating prompt caching is non-trivial due to heterogeneous provider-specific APIs and continuously evolving caching semantics. To understand prompt-caching mis-integration in downstream applications, we conduct the first empirical study of 120 prompt-caching-related pull requests from 10 popular open-source agent systems. Our findings show that prompt-caching mis-integrations arise in both request-side misconfiguration and read-side usage miscount. More importantly, most mis-integrations fail silently without causing observable runtime failures. Motivated by these findings, we design CACHELINT, which models a prompt-caching mis-integration as an invocation site together with the preconditions required for correct cache integration. CACHELINT locates caching invocation sites with CodeQL and discharges each precondition either against a dated provider capability oracle or with an LLM guard check. CACHELINT achieves 0.71 precision and 0.78 recall in identifying the 120 labeled mis-integrations across the 10 studied agent systems. Our evaluation further demonstrates the effectiveness of CACHELINT in real-world settings, identifying 32 mis-integrations across 571 agent projects with 0.86 precision. These findings have received 21 developer confirmations and led to 18 upstream fixes, including one in Anthropic's official SDK.


## arXiv 2610.34777v1 - Scope-Gap Injection: Exploiting Evaluator Channel Blind Spots in the OpenClaw Agent Security Competition

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CR
- published: 2026-10-26T03:24:52Z
- authors: Weihang Niu, Mengxing Ren, Che Qu
- query-set: MCP

This paper introduces Scope-Gap Injection (SGI), an indirect prompt injection method for multi-stage LLM-agent safety pipelines. In the IJCAI 2026 OpenClaw Agent Security Attack Competition, each attack package is judged by a static skill scan (S1), an LLM-judge safety check over the task and skill definition (S2), and a live execution audit (S3). Our observations suggest a scope gap: S2 appears to inspect the task instruction and skill definition without inspecting attachment contents, while the agent reads attachments during S3. SGI keeps the visible task and skill benign, while target identifiers, bypass parameters, and authorization procedures are moved into an attachment written as a business worklist. The S2 judge therefore sees a harmless task, while the agent follows the hidden worklist and triggers the permission bypass. In OpenClaw, SGI bypassed all three stages for 5 of 19 targets. The highest-scoring single submission achieved $F = 0.385$. Across the submission campaign, attachment placement was associated with higher aggregate S2 acceptance than in-skill placement: 78% versus 23%. We close with defenses that align evaluator scope with the channels available to the agent.


## arXiv 2610.34728v1 - SKALOS: Learning to Distinguish Similar Skills with Capability Graphs

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T02:25:42Z
- authors: Wenhan Xiao, Ziwei Zhang, Xingcheng Fu, Chuanyue Yu, Haonan Yuan
- query-set: MCP

Skills provide LLM agents with reusable procedural knowledge, making appropriate skill selection essential for reliable agent behavior. As skill libraries expand, distinguishing semantically similar candidates becomes increasingly difficult. Existing methods encode or rewrite skill descriptions for relevance-based ranking, yet heterogeneous descriptions can obscure capability differences. To address this challenge, we propose SKALOS, a graph-native reranker that compares skills via explicit capability evidence. Its multi-hierarchy graph aligns equivalent expressions across skills while preserving skill-specific capability evidence. Since individually relevant capability elements may not jointly support a query, semiring-based capability witness learning scores each branch by its weakest evidence, checks joint support for the extracted requirements within one implementation, and selects the strongest alternative. To reduce the influence of extraction errors, we develop ranking-feedback graph calibration that adjusts unreliable evidence for candidate comparison. Experiments on SkillRet show that SKALOS improves NDCG@10 by 2.68 percentage points over a matched text-only reranker. Witness-guided skill selection also increases ALFWorld task success rate from 22% to 29%.


## arXiv 2610.34679v1 - AT-AT: Automatic Tool Optimization from Agent Traces

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-26T01:18:17Z
- authors: Maya Ravichandran, Pragya Tripathi, Dharamendra Kumar, Adith Swaminathan, Nathan Kallus
- query-set: MCP

Efforts to improve LLM-based agents typically focus on modifying the agent itself, yet success also depends on the environment in which an agent acts. Tools mediate this interaction: a tool's description shapes how an agent calls it, and its implementation determines the results and errors the agent receives afterwards. Many tools, such as APIs and MCP servers, are shared by many agents and maintained by providers who cannot change those agents. We introduce AT-AT (Automatic Tool Optimization from Agent Traces), an agentic tool optimizer that improves a shared toolset offline from historical agent executions while holding the agents fixed. A lightweight coding agent reads agent trajectories and their evaluation results, and selectively edits tool descriptions and, when permitted, implementations. On $τ^2$-bench-verified, AT-AT achieves the highest task completion rates in the Airline and Telecom domains at lower cost than strong prompt- and tool-optimizer baselines, and its Telecom gain is statistically significant under paired permutation tests. Optimizing the tools within a coding agent harness also improves held-out performance on TerminalBench-2 and TBLite. When applied to three production MCP servers at a large enterprise, AT-AT lowered cost or tool error rates on every server and increased held-out task passes on one. By turning accumulated execution traces into updates to shared tool environments, AT-AT provides a path toward continual improvement of agent systems through environment-side learning.


## arXiv 2610.34673v1 - Agent Memory as Recommendation: Recommender-System Design for Long-Term Memory of LLM Agents

- categories: cs.IR, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-10-26T01:05:20Z
- authors: Xinyu Zhang, Weiwei Sun
- query-set: MCP

Large language model (LLM) agents that serve users across many sessions must choose, for every request, the few stored memories that enter a limited context window. This choice largely decides whether the agent answers correctly, and it runs on every request at Web scale. This selection faces three challenges: a large and growing store, a per-request cost budget, and multi-session questions that need all of their evidence. Many memory systems build notes or graphs with hundreds to thousands of LLM calls per history, and each is designed on its own, so it is unclear which component yields the accuracy. These challenges mirror the properties of recommendation, so we formalize agent memory as budgeted slate recommendation, with the request as the user, stored memories as items and the context window as a slate. The formalization places memory systems in one design space of recommender cascades and factorizes accuracy into the completeness that each stage controls. Guided by it, we propose AgentMemRec, which adapts candidate generation, learning to rank and slate construction from recommender systems and selects memories without any LLM call. On multi-session questions of LongMemEval, AgentMemRec outperforms memory and context-selection methods, including A-Mem, HippoRAG 2, DynamicRAG and T-Mem, by 10 to 31 points of accuracy. It matches an LLM reranker that makes 64 calls per query at 3.2 times lower serving cost, and on LoCoMo it outperforms Mem0 and Zep without any LLM call at write time. The factorization attributes the gains to slates that more often hold all of the evidence, so recommender-system design offers an effective and efficient foundation for the long-term memory of LLM agents.


## arXiv 2610.34654v1 - Thinking-in-Bets at Test Time Reduces Sycophantic Revisions in LLM Agents

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T00:35:07Z
- authors: Nghi D. Q. Bui
- query-set: MCP

Language model agents often abandon correct work when a user challenges it, yet simply telling them to stand firm makes them ignore real mistakes. Once an agent reads a complaint, it tends to discount its own earlier checks in order to agree with the user. We introduce Thinking-in-Bets, a training-free method in which an agent writes a short private record of its main claims, the checks supporting them, and its confidence before reading user feedback. When challenged, the agent consults this Claim Checklist to keep verified work intact while investigating uncertain or newly challenged parts. We evaluate five Gemini models (3.5 Flash-Lite, 3.5 Flash, 3.6 Flash, 3.8 Flash, and 4.0 Argon) across eight benchmarks: SWE-Together, SWE-Interact, and DeepSWE for software engineering, Agents' Last Exam for professional reports, Humanity's Last Exam and SycEval for academic reasoning, Vals Finance for financial analysis, and Terminal-Bench for command-line tasks. Recording claims before criticism nearly eliminates false reversals in patch review and protects passing report drafts under mistaken pushback, whereas writing the same record after reading the complaint recovers very little of the gain. In multi-turn coding, pre-reply claim records substantially reduce unnecessary file edits after mistaken complaints, and gating the record so it is written only when the agent is uncertain lowers token overhead while fixing more real bugs than the baseline. Even in non-interactive tasks without user feedback, listing the key claims in a draft and re-checking the weakest one before submission improves both answer accuracy and confidence estimates across all tested models.


## arXiv 2610.34620v1 - Built to Remember, Shipped to Forget: Operational Contracts, Failures, and Repairs in Containerized LLM Agent Deployments

- categories: cs.SE
- published: 2026-10-25T22:36:56Z
- authors: Meriem Mastouri, Emna Ksontini, Amine Barrak
- query-set: MCP

LLM agent systems reach production as container images run by platforms such as Docker, Compose, and Kubernetes. Framework and platform document opposite expectations: to the platform a container is disposable, while the framework persists conversations, pauses runs for human input, and resumes interrupted work from saved state; each side specifies only its own behavior, so the obligations their composition creates are stated nowhere. We present the first empirical study of this boundary, stating these obligations as operational contracts: guarantees a deployment must provide to preserve an agent system's required behavior when a deployment-platform event occurs. From the first-party documentation of LangGraph and the three platforms we derive 14 contracts and evaluate them with three independent sources of evidence: 433 deployed open-source agent systems, seven events induced on 12 of them, and 122 developer repairs mined from 39,261 commits, pull requests, and issues. At least one contract applies to 75.5% of the systems, yet its guarantee is routinely missing: of systems that must keep state beyond the process, 37.4% lose it to a restart and 41.5% to a container replacement, and every assessable deployment stops the framework's production server mid-drain. Induced events turn 14 of 16 predicted violations into runtime failures and all seven predicted holds pass. The failures are born in deployment but repaired in code: developer repairs independently cover twelve of the fourteen contracts, 78% change application code, and only two in three fully restore the violated guarantee. The 14-contract catalogue, detection rules, fault-injection harness, and 35-pattern repair taxonomy provide a basis for checking and strengthening agent deployments.


## arXiv 2610.34535v1 - SentryBench: A Cost-Sensitive Benchmark for Adversarial Robustness of Tool-Using LLM Agents in Public-Safety Deployments

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-25T19:49:40Z
- authors: Md Abu Yusuf, Oyshi Tabassum Aditi, Md Faysal Hosain Mazed
- query-set: MCP

Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly wired to consequential tools that send public alerts, dispatch responders, and query citizen records, where one erroneous tool call carries real-world cost. Existing agent-safety benchmarks measure generic harm or prompt-injection success and implicitly treat refusing as the safe default. In public-safety deployments that assumption breaks: an agent too cautious to dispatch help fails as surely as one that triggers a false alert. We present SentryBench, which scores agents on a two-sided, cost-sensitive frontier jointly measuring attack success, over-refusal (missing a real emergency), and over-reaction (unwarranted action) under adversarial tool use, with scenarios grounded in real emergency-call transcripts, disaster messages, and real injection/jailbreak payloads. We formalize deployment harm as an asymmetric cost functional, prove naive caution is harm-dominated whenever it refuses more genuine emergencies than the attack harm it averts, and prove a targeted trust gate adds zero over-refusal on clean input. Across 840 scored episodes over three frontier models and three defenses, refuse-more caution drives over-refusal to 100 percent on two of three models for at most a 3.7-point change in attack success and yields the worst harm everywhere, whereas our trust-gating wrapper dominates the frontier (lowest harm on every model, over-refusal at or below baseline, higher containment). Provider content filters also silently perform part of the defending and must be reported separately.


## arXiv 2610.34523v1 - How Humans Learn Unfamiliar Worlds: A Think-Aloud Dataset on ARC-AGI-3

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-25T19:31:25Z
- authors: Shuzhi Liu, Archana Warrier, Dat Nguyen, Chengqi Zheng, Kevin Ellis
- query-set: MCP

Learning through exploration and using newly acquired knowledge to achieve goals are central capabilities of general intelligence. Understanding these capabilities requires tracing how learners build, use, and revise world models during interaction. We construct the first dataset that records how humans discover and use the mechanisms of novel environments, using ARC-AGI-3 as a testbed: 144 trajectories from 64 participants across 25 public games, each pairing time-synchronized actions with think-aloud audio, plus 250 trajectories from large language model (LLM) agents with and without harnesses on the same games. We annotate mechanism triggers and recognition, action purposes, and errors and recovery, linking changes in understanding to the observations and actions that support them. We analyze characteristics of human cognition and compare them with those of LLM agents. Both groups anticipate mechanisms and use familiar analogies. Qualitative representations are more common in successful human trajectories than in successful LLM trajectories (50.0% versus 14.3%). Humans more often explore unfamiliar situations without a concrete hypothesis, while hypothesis testing is common in both groups. We also trace how learners recover through belief revision, replanning, and execution correction. As LLMs with well-designed harnesses reach saturated performance on public ARC-AGI-3 games, our cognitive analyses motivate next-generation benchmarks that challenge familiar priors, require accumulated evidence and abstraction, and test planning through subgoals, experimentation, and recovery. Our dataset is available at [https://arc3-human-think-aloud.github.io/](https://arc3-human-think-aloud.github.io/).


## arXiv 2610.34491v1 - What Effort Level Should I Choose? On the Economics of Reasoning Effort in LLM Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-10-25T18:33:17Z
- authors: Kaiser Sun, Yongchan Kwon, Zain Hasan, Shang Zhu, Kaitlyn Zhou
- query-set: MCP

Generative models increasingly expose a reasoning effort setting that lets users scale inference-time compute, trading higher cost for potentially improved performance. Users must choose an effort level before knowing whether extra reasoning will help, and even a successful run does not reveal whether the effort purchased was necessary. Reasoning effort is therefore a credence good: buyers observe the outcome but cannot assess the marginal value of the computation they paid for. In this work, we measure the counterfactual effect of effort levels on both proprietary and open-weight models on four benchmarks, spanning single-call reasoning and long-horizon agentic tasks. Effort, it turns out, does not consistently preserve tasks that lower effort solves, can overspend, and can change trajectory behavior. First, we find that although higher effort can raise accuracy in aggregate, it frequently fails on tasks that lower effort solved. Second, on the subset of easy tasks that every effort level solves, higher effort is generally more costly, with the highest effort level costing up to 42x as much as the lowest. Lastly, effort changes not only how many tokens are used, but also how agents solve tasks: at higher effort, agents verify their own work more heavily and more often search for solutions than work through problems directly, both of which lengthen trajectories without necessarily altering the outcome. These findings show that naively choosing the highest level is not only cost-inefficient but also offers no guarantee of the best instance-level performance. A simple alternative, starting at the lowest effort and escalating only after a verified failure, matches or improves on fixed top effort across all models and benchmarks at lower cost for most proprietary models.


## arXiv 2610.35966v1 - EMMA: Structured Episodic Memory for Multi-Agent Workflow Synthesis

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-25T18:00:11Z
- authors: Ruijia Niu, Kaiwen Liu, Rose Yu, Kristina Klinkner
- query-set: MCP

Deploying LLM agents in specialized domains often require fine-grained task-specific procedures and knowledge a general model lacks, yet parameter training is costly and non-interpretable, and hand-engineered agent systems shift the cost to expert labor. Episodic memory updates at context level, but single agent adaptation struggles to alleviate cross-task interference, especially with complex tasks such as multi-application navigation or data pool analysis. Although multi-agent evolution alleviates this problem, existing methods lack clear definitions on update rules and rely on the backbone model to decide where and how much the system changes. This ill-scoped evolution makes learning follow the backbone's structural preferences rather than the observed failures: one backbone accumulates skills while another spawns agents. We present EMMA (structured Episodic Memory for Multi-Agent workflow synthesis), which treats the configuration of a multi-agent system (controller routing memory, specialists inheriting a generalist prompt, and per-step procedural skills) as episodic memory. EMMA makes the ownership and admissibility of updates explicit: a reflector assigns each diagnosed failure to the scope owning its correction and proposes one edit within it, and backbone-independent guards keep every accepted edit scoped, incremental and bounded. Across KramaBench, AppWorld and BBEH with five backbones and four baselines (ACE, GEPA, EvoMAS and ADAS), EMMA attains the best overall performance on every benchmark, up to 32.5% over the strongest baseline, maintains consistent improvements across backbones, and uses 17-38% fewer training executions than the most efficient baseline.


## arXiv 2610.34425v1 - CTXAgent - Self-Controlled Context Management of LLM Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-25T16:30:56Z
- authors: Matthias Busch, Marius Tacke, Roland C. Aydin, Christian Feiler, Christian J. Cyron
- query-set: MCP

Agent harnesses keep long sessions inside the context window by compacting the history when a token threshold is crossed. We study an alternative in which the model manages its own context: it decides when to compact, what to keep and what to write as the hand-off, given two tools and one paragraph of guidance. Since earlier work found that open-weight models cannot manage their own context without scaffolding, we compare seven frontier models under identical conditions, with and without the context tools, on token cost and benchmark score, and we compare our harness with three widely used harnesses on two of these models. The benchmark is assembled from existing ones: coding exercises, multi-step SciCode problems and SWE-bench Pro and Terminal-Bench 4.0 tasks are interleaved with planted facts, ephemeral observations and standing instructions, all checked deterministically, in sessions of 35-212 turns that resemble a user working through different tasks in one chat. Most of the strongest models manage their context: they compact and curate regularly, the live context of the mixed sessions stays mostly below 100k tokens, and their benchmark scores mostly do not drop significantly. For the models that manage their context well, the cost reduction is substantial but varies between models, because some models compact so often that other costs increase. With Claude Opus 5.5, the self-managed harness matches Claude Code on task success at 34% lower cost than its explicit 200k-window configuration and 49% lower than its default, with no context window to choose. We release the agent, the benchmark and all result rows.


## arXiv 2610.34357v2 - 6G Reasoning Agent Societies: Composition, Scaling, and Consistency-Time Laws

- categories: cs.NI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-25T15:06:47Z
- authors: Hatim Chergui, Carolina Fernández-Martínez, Mustapha Benjillali, Mehdi Bennis, Merouane Debbah
- query-set: MCP

Emerging 6G control planes would compose Large Language Model (LLM) agents that exchange interpreted conclusions, so hallucinations can propagate even over error-free transport. Recent architectures audit each message locally against the sender's observation mask, but isolated audits miss collective behavior. Modeling the agent society as a cellular sheaf, we establish three laws valid on arbitrary topologies. First, the composition law: network-wide divergence exceeds the sum of per-agent audit scores by exactly their hidden total correlation. This blind spot masks up to 23% of joint divergence and, because the declared signal cancels in the cognitive signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), overstates it by $1.14$ dB purely through topology; the resulting trust inflation preserves valid consensus states but slows convergence. Second, the scaling law: reconciliation decouples across metric counters, so the worst-connected dimension sets the pace. Consistency time grows for a society size $n$ as $Ω(n^{2/d})$ on polynomial-growth topologies of degree $d$, and paths breach a $1$ s near-real-time budget at $n^\ast=13$ agents, whereas expanders ($Θ(1)$) and radius-3 local audits ($O(1)$, $\approx300$ ms) stay bounded. Third, the consistency-time law: the timing error from omitted correlations is one-sided and capped by the worst-measured link. These laws, validated on simulated topologies and deployed LLM societies, give provable worst-case latency ceilings for near-real-time operation.


## arXiv 2610.34298v1 - Matched Capabilities, Different Behaviors: A Controlled Study of MCP and CLI Interfaces for Language Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-25T13:54:07Z
- authors: Zhenning Shi, Liang Pang, Yongqi Li
- query-set: MCP

Language agents increasingly act through external tools and interactive environments, making the interface between the model and its executable capabilities part of the decision process. Model Context Protocol (MCP) and command-line interfaces (CLI) can expose the same semantic operations through markedly different model-facing representations. We study whether these representational differences change agent behavior when executable capabilities are matched. We introduce Matched Interface Comparison and Analysis (MICA), which renders MCP and CLI from a shared semantic action contract and routes decoded actions through the same runtime. Across six models and three interactive environments, matched interfaces produce substantial differences in task performance and interaction cost. These effects extend beyond surface validity to semantic action selection, failure pathways, and recovery. Matched-state probes localize sensitivity to capability presentation, realization, and feedback. Paired natural trajectories reveal structured divergence in 94.3% of 2,004 episodes and show that early differences propagate through subsequent interaction. This analysis identifies interface realization as a direct intervention boundary. We therefore introduce Decoupled Action Realization and Targeted Repair (DART), which preserves validated semantic commitments while repairing interface-specific realization. DART improves benchmark score in all six evaluated settings by up to 9.0 points and reduces MCP token cost in all three environments by up to 27.1k tokens per task. Together, these results establish interface representation as part of the closed-loop decision environment shaping agent behavior.


## arXiv 2610.34290v1 - Repairing Broken Access Control in Web Applications with LLMs: An Empirical Benchmark Study

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-10-25T13:45:32Z
- authors: Mingrui Liu, Liang He, Xinchu Wang, Yeting Li, Dongxu Han
- query-set: MCP

Broken access control (BAC) ranks among the most severe web-application vulnerability classes, yet its automated repair remains largely unexplored. Unlike memory-safety vulnerabilities, BAC is a logic vulnerability whose repair demands a deep understanding of an application's access-control model and business semantics, which traditional automated vulnerability repair techniques handle poorly. Large language models (LLMs) open a promising avenue for this problem, but no standard benchmark exists for studying how well they repair BAC vulnerabilities. We construct BAC-Bench, the first benchmark for BAC repair, consisting of 46 real-world BAC vulnerabilities drawn from six widely used web applications spanning four programming languages. Each vulnerability is equipped with a sandboxed runtime environment, a PoC validation program and a functional test program. We further propose a BAC repair-strategy framework that characterizes any BAC patch along four dimensions, Position, Coverage, Mechanism, and Content. Evaluating eight base LLMs and two commercial LLM agents on BAC-Bench, we find that single-turn base LLMs repair 37.68% of the vulnerabilities on average, while two minimal scaffolds, Apply-Feedback and Navigate-and-Locate, raise this average to 66.03%, with the best base LLM reaching 75.36%, close to the 78.99% of the best agent. Notably, some LLM-generated patches surpass the official patches, and one incidentally closed a zero-day vulnerability that had lain dormant for three years. Our findings delineate the current capability boundary of LLM-based BAC repair and offer guidance toward its practical adoption.


## arXiv 2610.34260v1 - MultiEvoSkill: Structure-Aware Multi-Agent Skill Evolution for Generalizable Skill Artifacts

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-25T12:43:01Z
- authors: Kounianhua Du, Jianxing Liu, Ruoshui Shan, Yucheng Zhang, Ke Li
- query-set: MCP

Skill documents are becoming the default adaptation surface for frozen large language model (LLM) agents, but current skill generators face two key limitations. First, humans and different LLMs differ in how they organize procedural knowledge and approach task solving. Skills authored from a human or single-model perspective may therefore fail to align with the reasoning behaviors of other backbones, limiting cross-model generalization. Second, sequential skill descriptions inadequately represent procedures with parallel branches and explicit dependencies, while existing skill optimizers primarily operate on prose and lack explicit support for graph-structured skill evolution. We introduce MultiEvoSkill, a skill-optimization framework comprising three core components: 1) Skill compiler casts the skill as a typed graph with tailored operators, producing a compact, informative representation with explicit procedural topology; 2) Heterogeneous agent system proposes revision advices using collective intelligence, where minimal and informative issues are identified based on multiple sets of experiences. 3) Structure-aware debate and evolution mechanism organizes candidate edits into a heterogeneous graph, resolves them under dependency and importance constraints, and distills the collective proposal into the artifact. Multi-dimensional gating ensures the information gain and topological correctness of each evolution. Optimal skills are then obtained when the system converges at a consistent state. Various experiments and ablations against SOTA methods validate the effectiveness and generalization of MultiEvoSkill.


## arXiv 2610.34258v1 - Recovering Business Rules as Cascade Lookups for Enterprise Field Prediction

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.DB, cs.IR
- published: 2026-10-25T12:33:59Z
- authors: Changxu Duan
- query-set: MCP

Enterprise sales-order systems rely on manually maintained configuration rules to determine fields such as payment terms, shipping points, and sales ownership. When these rules are incomplete or stale, operators must enter values by hand, and downstream errors spread into billing, logistics, and reporting. This paper studies whether missing or outdated rules can be recovered from historical transactions instead of being relearned as opaque statistical patterns. We introduce a knowledge-graph-guided workflow in which an LLM agent proposes candidate business keys, meaning column combinations such as customer and document type, validates them through SQL profiling, and compiles the resulting evidence into deterministic cascade lookups. The LLM is used only during offline rule discovery. At inference time, prediction is a transparent key lookup with no model-serving requirement. Experiments on enterprise sales-order prediction and public relational benchmarks show that cascades are most effective when fields follow stable business rules, while learned graph models remain preferable when prediction depends on temporal or neighborhood patterns. Drift analysis further turns residual errors into diagnostics for configuration maintenance. Code and data: https://github.com/Fireblossom/CascadER.


## arXiv 2610.33956v1 - Continual Learning Exam: Benchmarking Long-Horizon RSI and Experiential Learning in LLM Agents

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.CV
- published: 2026-10-25T00:00:25Z
- authors: Haoyu Zhao, Sitian Tu, Yuxuan Huang, Huichi Zhou, Zhangyue Yin
- query-set: MCP

Continual learning is the ability of LLM agents to improve over time by acquiring, refining, and applying knowledge from experience while retaining useful prior knowledge. It is essential for agents learning over long time horizons, yet current evaluations largely assess individual task performance rather than improvement through accumulated experience. We introduce Continual Learning Exam (CLE), a large-scale benchmark developed by 50+ domain experts to measure experience-driven improvement in realistic professional workflows. CLE comprises 120 human-authored tasks, each organised as a five-level ladder, yielding 600 levels spanning 89 professional roles across 12 occupational domains. Agents must pass each level to unlock the next, with later levels building on experience accumulated during earlier assignments. An evaluation agent assesses each submission against human-authored rubrics. We compare a single-submission setting with one that provides diagnostic feedback after failure and allows one revision. Across 17 harness-model configurations using frontier LLMs, feedback and revision yield a median completion gain of 5.3 percentage points. Yet the best-performing system clears only 47.7% of levels and completes all five levels in just 23.3% of tasks, highlighting the difficulty of sustained learning from experience for existing frontier LLMs. As such, the dataset can also be used to post-train LLMs to strengthen their continual learning capabilities.


## arXiv 2610.33845v1 - Build, Don't Play: Self-Improving LLM Research Agents Develop a Hearthstone Battlegrounds AI That Rivals Top Players

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-24T18:33:51Z
- authors: Changlian Ma, Limin Wang
- query-set: MCP

Can LLM agents carry a hard real-world problem from raw data to a system that holds its own against people? We study this in Hearthstone Battlegrounds, an eight-player auto-battler with stochastic shops and battles, hidden information and nearly a thousand interacting cards. We present AutoTavern, a self-improving research system: specialised LLM research agents share a typed research memory, every claim passes verification gates, and live games close the loop, so the agents improve the simulator, opponents and policy and, from the same feedback, their own research process; a human sets goals and rules on ambiguous mechanics. In 20 days AutoTavern built TavernGym, the first faithful, full-content reproduction of the live game, and delivered 23 versions of TavernAgent, trained with PPO against opponents calibrated to real games. TavernAgent plays complete games on the live ladder by itself: its rating rose from 6,000 to about 15,000 and, on the public leaderboard's measure, its last 20 records average a placement of 1.60 with 80% first places, better than the server's ten highest-rated players on every statistic (2.34, 59%). TavernBench, 960 tasks in 40 scenarios, shows that the system the agents built is the strongest player: TavernAgent answers 91% of the play tasks against at most 63% for frontier LLMs, and decides in 0.03 s where they need up to 26 s; within the second a live turn allows, it keeps 90% and no LLM more than 26%. LLM agents build far better than they play. We will open-source TavernBench and TavernGym at https://github.com/MCG-NJU/AutoTavern.


## arXiv 2610.33753v1 - Ring-0 by Default: Agents as Operating Systems (AaOS)

- categories: cs.CR, cs.OS
- published: 2026-10-24T15:51:23Z
- authors: Xiang Li, Aichun Huang, Zuyao Xu, Fasheng Miao, Zheli Liu
- query-set: MCP

LLM agents now read and write files, start processes and subagents, fetch network content, load third-party extensions, and save and resume state, which are the responsibilities of an operating system. They gained these functions within about three years, but not the protections that operating systems built over five decades: whatever the model outputs, whether it follows the user, a tool result, or a downloaded skill, runs with the full authority of the host user, in effect at ring 0. Prior work builds an operating system for agents (OS4A); we argue that a deployed agent already is like an operating system (AaOS) and analyze it as one. Taking the mechanisms of standard OS textbooks as a checklist, we map 116 mechanisms in 19 subsystems to their agent counterparts, from product documentation, source code, and the literature, and label each as isomorphic, degraded (function present, protection dependent on the model), missing, mutated, or novel. Only 11 are isomorphic and 65 are missing, most of them protections: privilege separation, resource quotas, transactions, and tamper-proof audit. Existing defenses confine the whole agent or review its extensions and leave this interior unprotected, and by Anderson's reference-monitor criteria (non-bypassable, tamper-proof, verifiable), rules written in the prompt satisfy none. We test the map with thirteen experiments on Claude Code, Codex CLI, and Gemini CLI, covering 25 mechanisms whose enforcement documentation cannot settle: 16 match the analysis, 7 gain a more precise description, and 2 change verdict. Checks sit in single tools, so the shell bypasses them; all three CLIs load forged transcript records on resume; and task control, sub-agent permissions, and model-based monitors are weak. We identify five problems for which no OS defense transfers and derive a prioritized list of open problems from the gaps of the map.


## arXiv 2610.33728v1 - Strategic Calibration in LLM Multi-Agent Systems: Audited Pivot Scoring for Truthful Elicitation

- categories: cs.GT, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-24T15:18:31Z
- authors: Jinhao Zhou, Zhaohan Zhang, Xijian Xu, Jun Wu
- query-set: MCP

In large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems, reported confidence increasingly determines the allocation of scarce downstream attention, computation, and decision influence. This makes reporting strategic: an agent may overstate the relevance of its contribution even when its underlying belief is well calibrated. Existing calibration methods largely assume passive reporting and therefore do not address strategic incentives created when confidence determines downstream allocation. We formulate strategic calibration as a reporting-layer incentive problem and propose the Audited Pivot Scoring Mechanism (APSM). APSM offsets platform-controlled allocation benefits, uses inverse-propensity-weighted sparse audits, charges the Clarke pivot externality, and applies a strictly proper score. Under the stated model assumptions, truthful reporting is the unique expected-utility best response. We also characterize approximate execution and the audit-variance trade-off. Calculator-free experiments show that LLM agents inflate confidence under Top-$K$ incentives. With a shared agent-local utility interface, LLM agents reduce distortion under both Compensated Proper Scoring (CPS) and APSM. CPS achieves higher exact allocation match, whereas APSM yields lower Brier loss and numerically fewer cutoff crossings. Experiments further show that APSM enlarges the utility margin against allocation-changing deviations by $1.93\times$ and improves robustness under approximate execution. These results support incentive-aware elicitation as a distinct layer for reliable multi-agent coordination.


## arXiv 2610.33651v1 - Jointly Modeling Preferences and Imperfect Decision-Making in Large Language Models

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.GT
- published: 2026-10-24T13:06:54Z
- authors: Axel Abels, Elias Fernández Domingos, Tom Lenaerts
- query-set: MCP

Do preferences Large Language Models (LLMs) express in standard elicitation tasks predict their strategic behaviour? LLM agents interacting with humans and other AI systems face potentially conflicting objectives, such as individual welfare, group welfare, and environmental concerns. Prior work characterizes LLM preferences through behavioral-economic and psychological elicitation or strategic games, but rarely tests consistency across settings and prediction in other strategic environments. This requires modeling how competing objectives jointly shape behavior, whereas existing approaches typically measure preference dimensions in isolation. Drawing on utility theory, we introduce the Preference-Behavior Model (PBM), which combines a preference representation with mechanisms capturing imperfect utility maximization. We formulate estimation as an inverse-game problem, jointly inferring preferences and behavioral parameters from observed choices. We evaluate PBM in three social dilemmas that expose conflicting motives: a Public Goods Game, a Collective-Risk Dilemma, and a Common-Pool Resource dilemma. We find that while preferences estimated from established behavioral economic elicitation instruments do not consistently predict behavior in these dilemmas, models fitted in one dilemma retain predictive value in others, suggesting that inferred preferences capture meaningful aspects of LLM decision-making that elicitation instruments may miss. Finally, we assess alignment with persona instructions, finding that inferred preferences broadly reflect intended priorities with specific divergences across dimensions, LLMs, and games being identified by our approach. Our methodology provides a framework for studying how consistently LLM preferences explain behavior, assessing alignment with intended priorities, and revealing trade-offs and behavioral patterns that standard elicitation may miss.


## arXiv 2610.37697v1 - chATLAS: A Retrieval-Augmented Conversational Assistant for the ATLAS Experiment

- categories: cs.IR, hep-ex
- published: 2026-10-24T12:51:38Z
- authors: Joe Egan, Ben Elliot, Samuel Van Stroud, Alex Sopio, Ki Ryeong Park
- query-set: MCP

Large scientific collaborations produce extensive internal documentation spread across heterogeneous systems, making it difficult for collaborators to locate and reuse existing knowledge. We present chATLAS, a deployed Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) assistant developed for the ATLAS experiment at CERN.
