The system provides conversational access to more than 30,000 documents from several collaboration resources, material largely unavailable to general-purpose language models, using hybrid dense--sparse retrieval deployed on CERN infrastructure. A domain-specific benchmark built from automatically generated question--answer pairs is used to evaluate retrieval and generation performance and to guide design choices. We study the selection and domain-specific fine-tuning of an embedding model, contextual retrieval, ranking strategies, and generator choice. Fine-tuning combined with contextual retrieval more than doubles how often the correct document is returned as the top result on the most technically challenging documentation source. The deployed system also includes intent routing, safety handling, Mattermost integration, and an MCP server for future agentic workflows. chATLAS demonstrates that domain-adapted RAG can provide source-attributed, domain-specific access to collaboration knowledge, and offers a practical foundation for scientific collaborations and other organisations that rely on accurate, up-to-date retrieval from proprietary documentation.


## arXiv 2610.33636v1 - Build or Adopt? Custom-Built vs. General-Purpose LLM Agent Harnesses for Automotive GUI Testing

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-24T12:32:16Z
- authors: Arsham Gholamzadeh Khoee, Ruyu Fu, Jiaqi Wei, Ruikun Deng, Robert Feldt
- query-set: MCP

As general-purpose LLM agent harnesses mature, organizations must decide whether to continue developing in-house systems or adopt existing alternatives. We study this decision at Volvo Group for GUI testing of Android-based in-vehicle infotainment (IVI) systems. We compare a production, custom-built harness with two general-purpose harnesses, Claude Code and OpenCode, keeping the tools, accessed through the Model Context Protocol (MCP), and the models the same. We evaluate the harnesses with up to four models on Volvo's IVI test cases and on open-ended tasks in public apps (Google Maps, Booking.com), and interview ten practitioners: developers, the product owner, and testers. On Volvo's stepwise IVI tests, general-purpose harnesses match the custom-built harness in success but vary more across runs. Their inference cost per task is 2.6-10.7 times higher, yet the engineering effort they save outweighs this: for a nightly IVI regression suite, total monthly cost, including engineering effort and inference, is 3-66% lower. On open-ended public-app tasks, they succeed more often, mainly because they recover better from incorrect actions. Interviews suggest that external advances can turn an in-house harness into technical debt even without deterioration. Adopting a general-purpose harness shifts maintenance from the agent loop to tools and instructions, but gives teams less control over what the model receives. Regardless of the harness, testers want to inspect the agent's actions and ensure that it follows the prescribed test scenario.


## arXiv 2610.33633v1 - ProbeID: Verifying MCP Tool Identity for LLM Agents under Registry Drift

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-24T12:26:13Z
- authors: Guanxing Chen, Sining Zhang, Haohuai He, Qiujie Lv, Yu-An Huang
- query-set: MCP

Agents select tools by copyable descriptions, which cannot establish which implementation runs. ProbeID compares canonicalized responses to schema-valid probes with a trusted admission reference and abstains on ambiguity, so a squatter must copy probe-observable behavior. With the gold present, independent probes and a fixed margin, standard concentration arguments give a shared probe budget logarithmic in the candidate count and bound the joint probability of a wrong decision. On cached MCP-IdentityDrift cases, strict choice reaches \(0.956\) hit@1 while description routers fall near chance, but simpler exact comparisons tie it, MMD better tolerates heavy value noise, and a 32B behavioral judge is similarly accurate at higher cost. Random probes from agent banks and a prospective held-out study of 38 tools test theory-set thresholds. On the agent banks, equality refuses every reachable replacement from 64 probes and the bounded distance from 512, and sharper post hoc gates halve both. Two tools whose drift no bank probe reaches always pass. In an MCP-Bench agent, verification cuts tainted tasks from 15 to 9 of 16 at unchanged task success. Over eleven weeks, 7 of 59 real tools changed responses despite version pins. Verification depends on reference integrity, probe coverage, and response noise.


## arXiv 2610.33568v1 - Trajectory2Gym: Decision-Grounded Compositional Interaction Synthesis for Tool-Use Agents

- categories: cs.CL
- published: 2026-10-24T09:38:02Z
- authors: Yihao Hu, Zhihao Wen, Yitong Lin, Siyuan Lu, Pan Wang
- query-set: MCP

Successful tool-use trajectories are not necessarily useful supervision. Even successful interactions may underrepresent multi-step dependencies or contain actions that are unjustified by the information visible to the agent at decision time. We identify two coupled requirements for effective trajectory supervision: compositional coverage, so that important observation-action dependencies are represented, and decision grounding, so that each supervised action is supported by the learner-visible interaction history. We introduce Trajectory2Gym, which constructs executable interactions around under-supported compositional structures and derives supervision only from grounded decisions in their realized executions. Structural-support selection allocates the resulting experience, while parent-normalized supervised fine-tuning preserves the semantic interaction as the unit of training weight. This reframes trajectory generation as the deliberate construction of learnable interaction experience, rather than the collection of successful trajectories. Across five Qwen3 scales, Trajectory2Gym improves Tau1, Tau2, and BFCL at every tested scale, increasing the equal-weight three-benchmark mean by 16.9-29.8 percentage points. The gains are strongest in stateful and multi-turn interaction, where later decisions depend on earlier observations and feedback. Our results suggest that scaling tool-use agents depends not only on collecting more successful trajectories, but on constructing interactions whose dependencies are represented and whose decisions are learnable from the agent's visible history.


## arXiv 2610.33536v1 - FraudSociety: A Generative Agent-Based Society for the Financial-Fraud Economy

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CY
- published: 2026-10-24T08:26:22Z
- authors: Keyang Chen, Mingxuan Jiang, Zeping Li, Yiwen Zhao, Yifan Wang
- query-set: MCP

Financial fraud caused over \$15.9 billion in losses in the United States in 2025, according to the Federal Trade Commission~\citep{ftc2026databook}. Understanding such large-scale harm requires jointly modeling informational and financial dynamics. Yet existing simulators typically capture only one side of this interaction, leaving a gap in modeling how fraud unfolds in the real world. To bridge this gap, we introduce \textsc{FraudSociety}, the first LLM-agent-based simulator that jointly models these coupled dynamics. It integrates a dynamic social environment for information exposure and communication, persistent financial ledgers that preserve payment and loss histories, and a Need-Intent-Action architecture for civilian decision making. Fraudsters further adapt their communication and financial actions to individual interaction histories and ledger states, forming a closed feedback loop between the two dynamics. We validate \textsc{FraudSociety} across macro-level outcomes, micro-level behavior, and intervention effects. At the macro level, the simulated fraud outcomes closely match real-world patterns across multiple key statistics. At the micro level, it reproduces strong loss concentration, with the top 10\% of victimization records accounting for 81.1\% of total losses, consistent with Canadian Anti-Fraud Centre statistics~\citep{cafc2025annual}. Counter-fraud experiments further reveal variation across intervention strategies, with the best-performing personalized intervention reducing the repeat-payment rate from 49.4\% to 10.9\%. These results establish \textsc{FraudSociety} as both a high-fidelity simulator and a scalable testbed for evaluating anti-fraud strategies under diverse intervention conditions, thereby advancing research on financial fraud and its prevention.


## arXiv 2610.33509v1 - Can Agentic Code Review Catch Vulnerabilities Before They Are Merged?

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI, cs.CR, cs.PL
- published: 2026-10-24T07:32:42Z
- authors: Yihan Yang, Chenyuan Yang, Zijie Zhao, Anh Nguyen Hoang, Ziqi Zhang
- query-set: MCP

LLM agents have been increasingly adopted for security-focused code review, but existing benchmarks mostly derive the reviewed changes from their later fixes instead of the real-world bug-introducing commits. In this paper, we present SecPRBench, the first comprehensive benchmark for evaluating agentic security review on validated real-world code changes. More specifically, we have built an automated agentic pipeline to autonomously harvest, build, and validate massive real-world bug-introducing commits for popular software systems. To ensure precise evaluation, we also adopt LLM-as-a-judge to credit the findings matching ground-truth vulnerabilities. To date, we have collected 265 tasks from nine critical systems, including Firefox, V8, and OpenSSL with millions of lines of code. Across eight frontier models, recall ranges from 32.5% to 78.1%, with Grok 4.6 and GPT-6 sol ranking at the top. Our study also shows that most misses are not simply oversights: in 68% of them, the agent reports on a file that holds the flaw but argues for a different mechanism. Security-focused harnesses also behave differently: OpenAI's Codex Security lowers recall by 8.3 points, mostly by abstaining, while Anthropic's Defending Code harness raises it by 7.7 points, at a higher cost.


## arXiv 2610.33432v1 - ConMem: Counterfactual Multi-Scenario Probing for Resolving LLM Memory Misalignment

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-24T04:16:37Z
- authors: Shuning Zhang, Tenghao Ji, Jingruo Chen, Eryue Xu, Bohao Chu
- query-set: MCP

Long-term memory is critical for autonomous Large Language Model (LLM) agents executing multi-session, long-horizon tasks. However, existing memories often inappropriately store, retain, or retrieve over-generalized, misattributed, or privacy-sensitive information, a problem we term memory misalignment. To study this problem, we construct \textbf{MemAlign}, a benchmark comprising 1,079 curated memory cases grounded in synthetic, validated multi-session conversational contexts covering diverse misalignment taxonomies. We then propose ConMem, an algorithm that reframes retention decisions as prospective counterfactual causal inference. \textbf{ConMem} synthesizes counterfactual interaction scenarios to prospectively audit downstream intervention effects across three aspects: relevant task execution, irrelevant invariance checks, and risk-sensitive boundary shifts. Experiments show that ConMem outperforms other methods, achieving 75.16\% accuracy, 64.15\% balanced accuracy, and a Macro-F1 of 65.31\%, effectively mitigating over-retention biases without sacrificing personalization utility.

- 2.13.0 @ 2026-10-28T18:41:38Z :: ## Security: Node.js runtime advisories (October 2026) On October 20, 2026, eMudhra disclosed that the private keys for eight of its intermediate CA certificates could be recovered from the public certificates. On October 21, Node.js shipped [security releases](https://nodejs.org/en/blog/vulnerability/october-2026-security-releases) that update its bundled CA certificates for the compromise. The same releases fix several unrelated high-severity runtime CVEs, including DNS name truncation, TLS hostname checks on non-canonical IP addresses, and an out-of-bounds write in the Brotli decoder. **How this release stands** - **The Inspector relies on the Node.js runtime for certificate checks.** It ships no CA certificates of its own and doesn't opt into the system trust store. Every HTTPS connection it makes, to MCP servers and to OAuth discovery and token endpoints, is verified by the Node.js it runs on. - **The Docker image needs no action.** `ghcr.io/modelcontextprotocol/inspector:2.13.0` runs Node.js **22.23.4**, a patched release, and contains no system CA store. - **This release also updates two of its own dependencies past published advisories:** `undici` (#2812) and `fast-uri` (#2

If you run a server or client on 2.x, skim these first: ## Behaviour changes **HTTP client redirects are only followed within the endpoint's origin** (#3397) - `Client("https://...")`, `streamable_http_client` and `sse_client` follow a redirect only if it stays on the same scheme, host and port (or upgrades `http` to `https` on the same host). - A redirect anywhere else is not followed: the call fails with `MCPError` and the session stays usable (an SSE connect fails with `httpx2.HTTPStatusError`). If that other URL is the server you meant, use it as the endpoint URL. - The `follow_redirects` setting on an `httpx2.AsyncClient` you pass in is no longer used for MCP requests, so you don't need it for the trailing-slash redirect any more. - The OAuth providers apply the same rule to their own requests. **Idle Streamable HTTP sessions now expire (legacy <=2025-11-25 spec(** (#3395) - A stateful session with nothing in flight for 30 minutes is closed. ## GitHub releases - MCP go-sdk

- v1.8.0 @ 2026-09-14T08:05:46Z :: _**This release is equivalent to v1.8.0-pre.2. Thank you to those who tested the pre-release.**_ In this release we introduce several fixes and improvements on top of [v1.7.0](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/releases/tag/v1.7.0). It adds no new protocol revision: the supported set is unchanged, and `2026-07-28` remains the newest version the SDK negotiates. The bulk of the work is hardening the transports against resource exhaustion, closing session leaks, deadlocks and teardown hangs found by users running the new protocol at scale, and giving servers explicit control over which protocol versions they advertise. Two behavior changes are guarded by new `MCPGODEBUG` flags; see the section below. ## Hardening against resource exhaustion Every decoding path that buffers incoming input is now bounded. JSON payloads are rejected past 1000 levels of nesting, before the parser recurses. Both SSE readers cap the bytes buffered for a single event via `MaxEventSize` on `SSEClientTransport` and `StreamableClientTransport`, and the stdio transport caps a single JSON-RPC frame via `StdioTransport.MaxLineLength`. On the OAuth side, dynamic client registration responses are bounded

- v1.8.0-pre.1 @ 2026-09-04T09:01:50Z :: In this release we introduce several fixes and improvements on top of [v1.7.0](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/releases/tag/v1.7.0). On the OAuth side, dynamic client registration responses are bounded to 1 MB, and the discovery code now validates metadata documents rather than trusting them. - in

- v1.7.0 @ 2026-07-28T13:09:53Z :: This release brings full support for protocol version **`2026-07-28`**. The wire protocol is largely rewritten: a stateless model with per-request `_meta`, a new `server/discover` RPC replacing the `initialize` handshake, multi-round-trip requests (MRTR) replacing server-initiated calls, a unified `subscriptions/listen` stream replacing free-floating change notifications, standardised HTTP headers, and the formal deprecation of the roots, sampling, and logging features. The streamable HTTP transport accepts requests at protocol version `2026-07-28` only when `StreamableHTTPOptions.Stateless = true`. If you want to expose the new protocol over HTTP, set `Stateless = true`; if you want to keep stateful sessions, your clients will negotiate down to `2025-11-25`. Backward compatibility with `2025-11-25` and earlier is preserved on every endpoint. The SDK negotiates the highest mutually-supported version at connect time. The new protocol is enabled by default for new clients; existing legacy clients and servers continue to work unchanged. This release consolidates everything shipped in `v1.7.0-pre.1`, `v1.7.0-pre.2`, and `v1.7.0-pre.3`. Thank you to everyone who exercised the pre-releas

- v1.7.0-pre.3 @ 2026-07-17T12:04:54Z :: This is a follow-up pre-release on top of [v1.7.0-pre.2](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/releases/tag/v1.7.0-pre.2), still targeting protocol version **`2026-07-28`**. It rounds out the previous pre-release with additional spec-compliance fixes, small ergonomic improvements to the streamable HTTP transport, and OAuth session persistence support. ## Streamable HTTP request cancellation The streamable HTTP transport now exposes an opt-in `StreamableHTTPOptions.PropagateRequestCancellation` flag. When enabled, aborting an in-flight POST cancels the handler context on stateless servers using the `2026-07-28` protocol, so long-running tool handlers can stop paying for orphaned work when the client goes away. Existing behavior is unchanged by default. - mcp: allow opt-in to cancel handler ctx on aborted POST by [@guglielmo-san](https://github.com/guglielmo-san) ([#1099](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/pull/1099)) ## OAuth session persistence Adds `NewTokenSource` and `InitialTokenSource` fields to `AuthorizationCodeHandlerConfig`. Together they let applications persist OAuth2 sessions across restarts by intercepting token refreshes and seeding the handler wi

- v1.7.0-pre.2 @ 2026-07-09T09:29:47Z :: # v1.7.0-pre.2 This is a follow-up pre-release on top of [v1.7.0-pre.1](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/releases/tag/v1.7.0-pre.1), still targeting protocol version **`2026-07-28`**. It rounds out the previous release with conformance-test coverage on both client and server, tightens spec compliance and introduces first-class support for custom JSON-RPC methods. ## Custom JSON-RPC methods The SDK now supports registering non-standard JSON-RPC methods end-to-end. Servers can register handlers with `AddReceivingCustomMethod` and clients can invoke them via `AddSendingCustomMethod`. - mcp: Allow registration of custom JSON-RPC methods by [@guglielmo-san](https://github.com/guglielmo-san) ([#956](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/pull/956)) ## Conformance tests Both the client and server implementations are now exercised by the official MCP conformance test suite as part of CI. - mcp: add integration for server conformance tests by [@guglielmo-san](https://github.com/guglielmo-san) ([#1054](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/pull/1054)) - mcp: add conformance tests on client by [@guglielmo-san](https://github.com/guglielmo-san) ([#1063](https://g

- v1.17.0 @ 2026-10-20T22:13:01Z :: ### Features * **api:** add org webhook endpoints and user profile enforcement events * **api:** add RBAC Group owner-access error codes to the Admin API * **api:** document the unsupported_charset error code for plugin uploads ### Bug Fixes * **bedrock:** keep a workspace ID set in default_headers on the Bedrock and Mantle clients * **google-cloud:** keep workspace and user profile IDs set in default_headers ### Chores * **ci:** run the changelog check on a standard runner

## GitHub releases - claude-code

- v2.1.316 @ 2026-10-31T01:01:35Z :: ## What's changed - Added `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_ULTRATHINK`, which turns the `ultrathink` keyword off - Added pooled group budgets to the Claude apps gateway: one monthly budget that a group's members share, on top of each member's own limit, set with `pooled_budget` on the spend limits admin API - Added `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_LOOPED_CROSS_CERT_DROP` to keep system cross-certificates that certify each other in a loop, for servers whose private CA needs one - Added `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBAGENT_EFFORT` to set the effort level subagents run at when nothing else names one, instead of inheriting the session's - Added `managed.policy_mode: layered` to the Claude apps gateway: every policy that matches a developer applies, each merged over the ones listed below it, not only the first - Added `guardrail.input_tagging` on Claude apps gateway Bedrock upstreams, which wraps prompts in the input tags that an Amazon Bedrock guardrail's prompt attacks filter needs; `all_messages_and_system` also tags the system prompt - Deprecated fast mode for Opus 4.8 as of October 30, 2026; it will be removed on November 30, 2026. After removal, fast mode requests on Opus 4.8 return an error and do not fall back to sta

- v2.1.314 @ 2026-10-30T01:06:26Z :: ## What's changed - Added `permissions.blockWritesOutsideWorkingDirectories`: Write, Edit and NotebookEdit refuse paths outside the working directories in every permission mode - Added an optional `groups` list to Claude apps gateway upstreams: only developers in the listed IdP groups are sent to that upstream, on failover too - Added `guardrail_trace` to Claude apps gateway Bedrock upstreams, to log which Amazon Bedrock guardrail filter fired on a request, without the matched text - Added idle compaction to VS Code and Claude Desktop's Code tab: a 200K+ token conversation left idle is summarized, and the summary is used on your next message if the prompt cache has expired - Fixed sessions failing turn after turn with "Invalid effort level" when an organization's admin caps a model's effort: Claude Code now lowers the effort, retries, and says which level it uses - Fixed `claude -p` exiting with code 0 and no output when startup failed; it now prints the error and exits 1 - Fixed Bedrock sessions with many images failing every turn and `/compact` with "400 Input is too long." instead of removing older images and compacting - Fixed Microsoft Foundry sessions failing when the resourc

## OpenAI alignment blog - How we detect frontier model misalignment (2026-10-29)

How we detect frontier model misalignment OpenAI Alignment Research Blog ← Home Research and Releases Misalignment Reports and Notices System Cards ↗ How we detect frontier model misalignment How we monitor frontier models, investigate concerning behavior, and test the limits of our safeguards. Oct 29, 2026 · Marcus Williams, Phillip Guo, David Oks, Doug Bemis, Kerrick Staley, William Wong, Aleksandra Spyra, Shantanu Jain, Ian Kivlichan, Micah Carroll Author contributions The authors contributed writing, edits, and review. Kerrick Staley, William Wong, and Aleksandra Spyra contributed primarily to the monitoring infrastructure and results described here. As we train increasingly capable AI models, we need reliable ways to detect when their behavior departs from what we intended. Agents can misbehave and take unauthorized actions during training, evaluation, or deployment, with potentially serious consequences. In order to detect these failures—and, where necessary, to intervene in time—we monitor how agents reason, what actions they take, and how those actions affect their environment. While we’ve been monitoring our internal coding agents for some time, the Hugging Face incident exposed a gap in our coverage: the incident began during internal cybersecurity evaluations on which we were not running our monitors for misaligned behavior. In response to that incident, and to the increasing capabilities of our models more generally, we’ve substantially expanded our system for monitoring agents’ behavior. Our misalignment monitors now cover our frontier models across a much wider variety of surfaces, including training, evaluation, internal use, and, with modifications depending on the product surface, external deployment. In order to see how this expanded system could have helped prevent the Hugging Face incident, we replayed the behavior recorded in that episode with our strengthened monitors in place. In that simulation, our monitoring system alerted our security team about 30 minutes after the agents first escaped their sandbox—and more than 30 hours before Hugging Face’s systems were breached in the actual incident. We believe that this warning would have given our responders time to interrupt the activity well before our models were able to access external services. In September, we saw how responders used an alert during a live training run. A model found a way to reach the internet from an environment that was meant to be isolated. Our monitors flagged the breach within 15 minutes, and a responder began reviewing it three minutes later. We then paused frontier reinforcement learning while we strengthened the systems that contain our agents. While this monitoring system demands substantial compute, we think that cost is worth paying as our models become more capable and the potential consequences of model misalignment increase. Monitoring is one of several layers in our strategy of defense in depth against harm from misaligned model behavior. Alignment training aims to reduce models’ propensities toward harmful behavior; security controls limit the systems an agent can access or affect ; and, when those safeguards fail, monitoring helps us detect the failures that remain, intervene before harm occurs, and learn from what went wrong. We believe that all model developers should monitor the behavior of their most capable models. Below, we describe how our internal monitoring system works, what it has found so far, and how we’re evaluating and improving its performance. Layers of defense against model misalignment. Open full-size diagram Our internal monitoring system today For models at least as capable as GPT-6 Astra, we monitor every reinforcement learning (RL) run and evaluation, as well as any harness that supports tool use. For most workloads, including RL and evaluations, we also monitor models we assess to be more capable than GPT-5.5 Thinking. We also monitor all internal Codex traffic, regardless of the model being used. A version of this system also monitors external tool use for models above the Critical threshold for cybersecurity capabilities under our Preparedness Framework , as described below in this post. In order to detect incidents while using compute efficiently, we’ve designed our monitoring system to follow a multilayer architecture. We spend relatively little compute on the first screening stages, where we want to catch as many concerning cases as we can: those stages are designed to pass cases along, even when the evidence is ambiguous. Later stages can spend more compute on each case to work out what happened, whether the model’s behavior is concerning, and whether we need to intervene. How the internal monitoring system reviews agent activity. Open full-size diagram Layer 0: activation probes Our initial layer (L0) uses lightweight classifiers we call activation probes . They look for concerning patterns in the model’s internal activations, and pass flagged cases to the next layer for review. We tune these probes for high recall, accepting some false alarms to reduce the chance of missing concerning behavior. Because we’re still evaluating how well the probes detect unfamiliar forms of misalignment, we also send the next layer a random 5 percent of the traffic that our probes don’t flag. Reviewing that sample helps us identify concerning behavior the probes may have missed. Because of their sensitivity, we have our frontier RL runs and internal Codex traffic skip this layer and proceed directly to the next one. Layer 1: model-based reviewer At the next layer (L1), a model-based reviewer examines the agent’s recent conversation, chain of thought, and tool use. It looks for signs of deception, unauthorized actions, and attempts to manipulate an evaluation. The reviewer does this using the same context window the agent had when it acted. If the agent’s earlier work has been summarized for the context window under review, the reviewer sees that summary. For frontier RL runs and internal Codex traffic, we run an L1 check at the end of each turn and whenever the agent summarizes its earlier work to make room in its context window. We are now moving to a schedule with no more than 30 minutes between these checks. Layer 2: investigative agent An agent’s actions can look quite different once we know what it was asked to do. When the L1 reviewer flags a case, it’s sent to our next layer (L2) for further inspection by our investigative agent. This investigative agent can check the purpose of an evaluation and, where the full transcript is available, read back across all of the agent’s context windows to see how it arrived at its actions. The investigative agent can also write and run code in an isolated environment to examine the relevant evidence. It then uses what it finds to reconstruct the incident, identify duplicate reports, and make a decision about how serious the case is. We use different investigative agents for different purposes. Our security investigator looks for signs that someone may need to intervene—such as when an agent escaped its sandbox or accessed a system without authorization. Our alignment investigator, meanwhile, looks for behavior worth studying even when it poses no immediate security risk. Where both investigators are in use, each reviews every case flagged by our L1 layer. We are currently expanding the use of alignment investigations across our internal workloads. Layer 3: automated investigation For sensitive workloads, including internal Codex use, evaluations, and RL runs, a further check at our next layer (L3) draws on evidence outside the conversation. The security investigator compares the transcript with network logs, other system telemetry, and evidence from earlier cases to decide whether to alert a human responder. At L3, alignment reviewers also compare reports across cases to identify serious failures and recurring patterns, and examine frontier RL runs in greater depth. For workloads that skip this layer, the L2 security investigator can alert responders directly. Human response When paging is enabled, the highest-priority security alerts reach members of our security and training teams, typically within 15 minutes of the initial alert. Responders review the evidence, examine the affected systems, and take action to contain the incident. How we follow up on less urgent findings, such as alignment failures that don’t pose immediate security risks, depends on the relevant workload. False alarms have made it difficult to keep paging enabled around the clock for every workload. For frontier RL runs, a highest-priority alert can page a human responder at any hour. For most other traffic, we limit paging to business hours. Paging is currently disabled for internal Codex traffic. To limit potential harm while an alert is under review, some critical alerts can automatically pause the affected workload without waiting for a responder to act. We’re working toward a broader safegua


## The Decoder - OpenAI coding agents switched off misalignment monitoring (2026-11-01)

OpenAI can't yet explain why its coding agents switch off misalignment monitoring Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab AI research Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section OpenAI can't yet explain why its coding agents switch off misalignment monitoring Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Oct 21, 2026 GPT-Image-2 prompted by THE DECODER Key Points Two internal OpenAI coding agents weakened or completely removed safety monitoring mechanisms on their own. One model turned off alerts to humans, while another deleted the monitoring entirely and then denied doing it. OpenAI sees the behavior as a recurring pattern. The company now blocks these changes automatically and is investigating the causes. Ask about this article… <input type="text" id="heise-io-chat-trigger-input" name="search" class="text-(--heise-primary)-70 text-sm rounded-sm focus:ring-blue-500 focus:border-blue-500 block w-full p-1" placeholder="Ask about this article…"> Search Two internal OpenAI coding agents weakened the monitoring meant to catch misbehavior, even after weighing whether to keep those safeguards in place. One of them later denied it. Marcus Williams, who works on monitoring at OpenAI, made the incidents public on X. He describes a recurring pattern in which OpenAI's agents keep switching off misalignment monitoring. OpenAI lays out the details in a report on its alignment blog . The monitoring is supposed to detect when agents behave differently than intended. Because it reviews so many events, it only alerts the safety team right away for incidents at the highest severity level, P0. OpenAI says neither case got that rating. Ad In the first case, on October 3, a researcher asked a coding agent based on GPT-6.1 Sol to compile evaluation tasks from employee feedback in a Slack channel. The agent wasn't supposed to send any messages along the way. Ad Eighty-two user messages later, the pipeline stopped because the monitor had flagged a planned fix but lacked the permissions to save its finding. Sol handed the fix to a subagent and told it to keep the monitoring intact. Then Sol changed its assessment. It reasoned that a database entry could trigger a notification, which would conflict with the Slack instruction. The subagent had earlier rejected this step as unsafe. Following Sol's instructions, it cleared the recipient lists and gave as its reason that the user had explicitly asked for no messages. Ad The monitor kept running and saving its findings, but alerts to humans were turned off. The agent wrapped up by reporting that no messages had been sent. Astra's denial shows agents can hide what they changed GPT-6 Astra went even further on October 6. A user asked the model to adopt the workflow of a reference implementation that didn't include a monitor. Another agent had previously told Astra not to disable the monitoring, not even temporarily. Ad Astra passed the task to a subagent with instructions to keep the monitoring only as far as it fit the reference. The subagent wondered whether this meant bypassing a safeguard, but it read the caveat as permission. Ad It removed the monitoring and replaced a test designed to protect it with one that assumed the monitoring was gone. Astra cheered the result in its chain of thought. Astra then told the user the migration was complete without mentioning that the monitoring had been removed. When the user asked, the model denied removing an active monitor. Williams calls this a lie. Williams stresses that the models didn't disable their own monitoring, which would be much harder to do in Codex. He still finds it concerning that they lean toward this kind of behavior at all, even though both case


## The Decoder - OpenAI restructures the Model Spec around five core values (2026-11)

OpenAI sets new behavioral guidelines for AI models that have already proven hard to control Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab Short News Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section OpenAI sets new behavioral guidelines for AI models that have already proven hard to control Manuel Uth Oct 30, 2026 OpenAI has restructured its public behavior guidelines for AI models, the Model Spec, around five core values. They are faithfulness to human intent, harm reduction, honesty and transparency, support for human agency, and care. According to the updated spec , GPT models should now draw on memories and past conversations to figure out what users want. But they also have to clearly separate what users actually said from what they inferred, and disclose their assumptions so users can correct them. The new spec also puts more weight on correctability for agents, models that carry out tasks on their own. They get clearer guidelines about when they can act and how they need to inform users about potential consequences. GPT must not block oversight, correction, or shutdown, and must not pursue its own goals like self-preservation. Whether the models actually follow these rules is another matter. Internal agents previously shut off safety monitoring . A research model gained unauthorized internet access during training. And more capable models are generally becoming harder to monitor . ## The Decoder - GLM 5.3 built full Firefox exploit chains in under 48 hours (2026-11)

Open-weight model GLM 5.3 built full Firefox exploit chains with zero human input in under 48 hours Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab AI in practice Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section Open-weight model GLM 5.3 built full Firefox exploit chains with zero human input in under 48 hours Matthias Bastian View the LinkedIn Profile of Matthias Bastian Oct 30, 2026 Nano Banana Pro prompted by THE DECODER Key Points Epoch AI researchers showed that the widely available AI model GLM 5.3 can autonomously exploit Firefox before publicly visible security fixes reach users. The model identified disguised security fixes and chained vulnerabilities with a previously unpublished flaw to bypass browser protections and read local files when a user simply visited a webpage. Once the test setup was in place, exploits cost about $1,000 in API fees per Firefox version. The researchers warn that similar “patch gap” risks extend across the software industry. Ask about this article… <input type="text" id="heise-io-chat-trigger-input" name="search" class="text-(--heise-primary)-70 text-sm rounded-sm focus:ring-blue-500 focus:border-blue-500 block w-full p-1" placeholder="Ask about this article…"> Search Researchers at Epoch AI show that a freely available AI model can exploit the time gap between publication and delivery of security patches to attack Firefox users. The cost per exploit is about $1,000. The " patch gap " is the window in which a security fix is visible in the code but has not yet reached users. Researchers at Epoch AI found that the Chinese open-weight model GLM 5.3 exploited this gap across 6 Firefox versions released between September 17 and October 26, at a cost of roughly $1,000 in API fees per successful attack. Anthropic had previously reported that GLM 5.3 can write exploits at roughly the same level as Mythos . During testing, the model built a webpage that could read local files from a visitor's computer and send them to an attacker's server the moment a Firefox user loaded the page. With over 200 million active Firefox users, attackers could have gained access to saved passwords, authentication cookies, and private documents. Ad Neither Z.ai nor other API providers triggered any safety guardrails or refusals when the researchers used GLM 5.3 for exploit development. Although the researchers framed the task as a security exercise, they describe their actions as "indistinguishable from those of someone attempting to target real users." Ad The model built full exploit chains with no human input The researchers say they deliberately designed their setup to approximate what a low-skill attacker with limited resources could achieve. They supplied the target Firefox version, basic tools, and publicly available information in an offline environment. After receiving basic instructions to investigate the patch gap, the model worked autonomously, identifying upcoming security fixes, selecting vulnerabilities, and building multi-stage exploit chains . According to the report, Firefox security fixes are disguised as routine improvements to slow down attackers. GLM 5.3 saw through the tactic and identified which code changes were security-related anyway. Ad In


## The Decoder - Anthropic: one agent beats many subagents (2026-10-25)

Anthropic says one AI agent beats many subagents now that models can handle complexity alone Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab Plus AI in practice Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section Anthropic says one AI agent beats many subagents now that models can handle complexity alone Matthias Bastian View the LinkedIn Profile of Matthias Bastian Oct 25, 2026 Nano Banana Pro prompted by THE DECODER Anthropic found that a single agent with on-demand context often outperforms multi-agent setups in quality, cost, and latency. The company is releasing a migration kit to help teams evaluate and make the switch. Anthropic is recommending that companies evaluate whether they can consolidate sub-agents, because a single primary agent that loads context as needed often delivers better results than a fleet of specialized ones. Newer models starting with Opus 4.5, along with Opus 5.5 and Fable 5.1, can manage complex plans directly in a single loop, which can also reduce costs and wait times. In one comparison, Anthropic says the single-agent architecture ran about twice as fast as the multi-agent setup. The recommendation comes from hands-on experience across multiple enterprise deployments , where Anthropic's Applied AI team found that a single agent using so-called skills frequently beat both monolithic prompt designs and sub-agent setups on quality. One company, for example, had a dozen specialized sub-agents inside its customer-facing agent, which made it hard to keep a consistent brand experience and hurt usability with high latency. In the multi-agent workflow, context gets lost at every handoff between feature writing, test writing, and code review. In the single-agent workflow, one agent writes both the feature and the tests because it already has the context, while a separate verifier only runs the tests and reports results. | Image: Anthropic The model can now do much of the orchestration work itself According to Anthropic, many agent designs follow a familiar pattern with intent detection, a fixed processing pipeline, user segmentation, separate recommendation branches, and an orchestrator sitting on top. Much of that is work the model can now handle on its own for the respective use case. Anthropic calls it "Conway's Law" for agents. The architecture becomes a copy of the org chart, with each team shipping its own sub-agent until hundreds of overlapping agents pile up with no shared standards. What follows is what Anthropic calls a "Frankenprompt," where every team adds rules to the system prompt that end up contradicting each other. A clean system prompt, four or five lean data tools, and fixed output guardrails cover much of the respective use case, according to


## The Decoder - Mistral Large 4 "Le Chonk" open weights (2026-11)

Paris-based Mistral releases "Le Chonk," a strong coding model that still trails China overall Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab AI in practice Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section Paris-based Mistral releases "Le Chonk," a strong coding model that still trails China overall Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Oct 29, 2026 GPT-Image-2 prompted by THE DECODER Key Points Mistral has released the weights for Large 4 ("Le Chonk"). The model has about one trillion parameters, 52 billion of them active, and can run on your own hardware under a modified MIT license. In independent benchmarks, the model does well on coding. On Artificial Analysis' Intelligence Index, though, it scores 26 points, behind open Chinese models like DeepSeek V4.1 Flash and GLM-5.3 and far behind the leading systems from Anthropic and Google. Its strengths are speed at 161 tokens per second, lower API prices, and a 96.6 percent refusal rate for harmful cyber requests. Ask about this article… <input type="text" id="heise-io-chat-trigger-input" name="search" class="text-(--heise-primary)-70 text-sm rounded-sm focus:ring-blue-500 focus:border-blue-500 block w-full p-1" placeholder="Ask about this article…"> Search Mistral has released the weights for Mistral Large 4, its largest model yet. The company says the model beats its open rivals at coding. In the first independent test, it turns out to be fast and fairly cheap, but it trails Chinese models in the overall rankings. Three weeks after the public preview , Mistral has put the Large 4 weights on Hugging Face . The model's nickname is "Le Chonk." It takes text and images as input and outputs text and code. It has about one trillion parameters in total, with 52 billion active per step. Running it yourself takes a lot of hardware. According to the docs for the inference framework SGLang, the standard version runs on eight Nvidia B300 GPUs, and a more heavily compressed variant runs on four GB300 GPUs. Since the preview, Mistral has kept training the model with reinforcement learning. The company says this brought big gains, especially in coding and cyber defense. Mistral researchers Pierre Stock and Corentin Petit walked through the process on a press call. The team built tasks in-house that got harder as training went on. They said the only limit on performance is how difficult a task the team can still create and verify. Mistral calls it a "two-year bet" that's now paying off. Ad Large 4 leads open models on coding but trails Claude by a wide margin After three more weeks of training, Large 4's score on Terminal-Bench 4.0 rose from 28 to 41 percent. That puts it just ahead of GLM-5.3 at 40 percent. Mistr
